My ather does not solve the dicult roble o distinguishing legitiate versus illegitiate deerence to exerts, o drawing on knowledge without being subordinate to it. For exa le, it is the echanis so ably described by Khalil Muha ad in his work on the role social science layed in the rst hal o the twentieth century in the subordination o Aerican citizens o Arican descent.5 Muhaad there shows how social scientists convinced o their own objectivity used statistical ethods to give an objective covering to racial bias. In this book, I define political propaganda as *the employment of a political ideal against itself*. Someone who presents subjective values, or self-interested goals, as the embodiment of objective scientific ideals is therefore producing paradigm examples of propaganda. My father's academic work is thus clearly a large influence on my own.

In sociology and democratic theory, the mid-1980s included the great German political theorist Jürgen Habermas's universal pragmatics turn. Habermas sought to describe the ideal speech conditions for democratic deliberation, and turned to analytic philosophy of language for help. Heading to my freshman year at the State University of New York at Binghamton in the fall of 1986, I knew that I was going to study philosophy, and had the vague sense that the democratic project centrally involved the philosophy of language and Kant. Via a circuitous route, I was led to the intensive study of philosophy of language, logic, and linguistics at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, where I earned my PhD in 1995.

During the decade I was immersed in the arcane details of formal semantics and pragmatics, the United States was in the throes of a mad experiment in mass incarceration, falling largely on the heads of the minority who were the descendants of slaves. I.' to refer to any case that involved a breach of the rights of young Black males who belonged to the jobless category of the inner city ghettos.

N. H. I. eans 'no huans involved.' "7 Wynter's article links the ethod o dehuanization o Aerican citizens o Arican descent to the dehuanization o Arenians by Turkish an- nationalists in the First World War eriod, and Jews by Geran nationalists during the Second World War eriod. In these latter cases, the dehuanization was a rearation or ass slaughter.

It would be one thing i only Black hilosohers and intellectuals were calling attention to the crisis o racially driven ass incarceration. In an interview with the artist Tuac Shakur in the early 1990s, he says, "When I sing, 'I' living the thug lie, baby, it's hoeless,' one erson ight hear that and just like the way it sounds, you know what I' sayin'? But I'm doing it or the kid that really lives the thug lie, and eels like it's hopeless. So when I say hopeless, when I say it like that, like, I reach hi. You understand? And even i when I reach hi it akes it glorious to the guy that doesn't live that lie, I can't hel it, it's a act he'll dro the thug lie soon enough. But or the erson I was trying to reach, he'll ick it u and I'll be able to talk to hi again."8 It's hard to think o soeone who is describing a liestyle as hopeless as glorifying it. Yet Tuac and artists like Ice Cube, who were atteting to counicate the griness and inhuanity acing inhabitants o the ghetto by reresenting, in the rst erson, characters exeriencing it, were nevertheless described as "gangsta raers" who were "gloriying" a situation they were in act reresenting as hoeless.

Throughout the 1990s the state and ederal rison syste was assively exanded, an exansion otivated in the olitical shere by racist ear ongering in the guise o objective science. The decade eatured notorious exales o the kind o technicist discourse that resents racist ideology in the guise o objective science. The neuroscientist Carl Hart exlains how scientists colluded in a racially biased drive to exaggerate the risks of certain illegal drugs to justify draconian sentencing policies, including wildly distorted sentencing policies for cheap versions of cocaine used in poor Black communities, versus versions of the same drug used in wealthy white communities.9 Hart also shows how the arguments that motivated differential sentencing for crack cocaine and the purer version favored by wealthy whites were part of a racist scientific narrative about drugs and Black pathology dating back to the early parts of the twentieth century.

During my entire adult life in the United States, scientific "experts," from medical doctors to "expert" police interrogators, have packaged racial bias as objective fact. The examples are too numerous to mention, but they include the fictitious "crack baby" syndrome, the Central Park jogger case, and another example I explore in this book, the "super-predator theory," introduced in 1995 by the then Princeton political science professor John Dilulio in a successful attempt to motivate the adoption of adult prison sentences for young Black offenders. Dilulio predicted a fivefold increase in violent crime in the United States from 1995 to 2000 (violent crime in the United States began dropping in 1991, and continued to drop continuously between 1995 and 2000). By the end of the 1990s, it was apparent that somehow, despite the rhetoric of and indeed sincere belief in a recently achieved democratic equality, there was drastic racial and economic injustice, evident to those suffering it, but somehow invisible to most of the rest of us. Around that time I started reflecting upon how to address systematically the topics of this book, though it was far from clear to me then that I would eventually write a book on the topic.

The lead-up in 2003 to the Iraq War again raised the philosophical mystery of the power of propaganda. It is to state the obvious to say that hilosohy has, throughout its history, deonstrated undeniable sexis and isogyny. Why are we so inclined to conuse, quite sincerely, objective clais o reason with what turns out to be, in retrosect, biased and sel- serving oinion? Here are two ysterious acts about olitical contestation. First, the olitical clais o ebers o disossessed grous are routinely disissed, even by sincerely well- meaning elites. Secondly, disossessed and resource- oor grous are hindered in olitical action. The obstacles to olitical action do not erely consistent in a lack o required resources; it is very oen the case that ebers o disossessed grous see to lack the required knowledge, or the required condence, to act to alleviate their own oression. their existence. But certain grou identities are deocratically robleatic; the Teutonic identity constructed by National Socialis is an obvious exale. My ocus in this book is olitical rhetoric; "roaganda" is y nae or it. Rhetoric is aong the earliest toics o hilosohical reection. I hilosohy has "core" toics, rhetoric is aong the. Both Plato and Aristotle wrote treatises on olitical rhetoric, the subject o this book. It is one o the basic toics o hilosohy, traditionally conceived. Political rhetoric is the subject o Plato's dialogue the Gorgias. Socrates there argues that rhetoric is not a science; it is a "knack" based on "guess work." Socrates is suggesting that there are no general rinciles that one can convey to others which redict what one should do to successully sway others nonrationally. One cannot thereore teach how to aniulate others. The aniulation o others deends uon articular acts about societies that are not art o a science o rhetoric. I do not here rovide a anual o roaganda. Instead, I ex lain what it is, why it atters, and the echanis by which it is eective. I argue that harul roaganda relies uon the existence o awed ideologies resent in a given society. Dierent awed ideologies exist in dierent societies. Proaganda exloits and strengthens the. # PROPAGANDA IN THE HISTORY OF POLITICAL THOUGHT

There is a sile and coelling arguent, known since Plato, which would lead us to exect that even aarently robust liberal deocracies are such in nae only. The arguent is as ollows. A certain or o roaganda, associated with deagogues, oses an existential threat to liberal deocracy. The nature o liberal deocracy revents roagandistic state ents ro being banned, since aong the liberties it erits is the reedo o seech. But since huans have characteristic rational weaknesses and are suscetible to attery and ani ulation, allowing roaganda has a high likelihood o leading to tyranny, and hence to the end o liberal deocracy.

The arguent is central to the lengthy history o olitical hilosohy, ro antiquity to the twentieth century. Jean-Jacques Rousseau is correct when, in The Social Contract, ublished in 1762, he declares the roble to be olitical hiloso hy's central reason or sketicis about deocracy:

The reason our olitical theorists go astray is the ollowing: All the states they see were badly constituted to begin with, and they are struck by the act that no olity o the kind I have described could ossibly be ket going within those states. They tell over to theselves, with vast auseent, all the absurdities that a cray scoundrel or sell- binder could ass o on the eole o Paris or London.1

It is not accidental that the roble roaganda oses or deocracy is so central in the long history o olitical hilosohy. Philosohers have historically taken the stability o a olitical syste to be a way o evaluating it against other olitical systes. It is or this reason that Aristotle chooses de ocracy as the least bad o the various ors o governent in his Politics. But even Aristotle recognized (in Politics, book 5, chater 5) that deocracy's aw, the articular instability it aces, cae ro "deagogues" who alternately "stir u" and "curry avor" with the eole. Aristotle clearly recognized that a chie danger to deocracy was awed ideology and deagogic roaganda.2

The arguent has been at the center o hilosohical discussions about the stability o the syste o any or o de ocracy ro antiquity to the twentieth century, and has been accordingly central to the evaluation o liberal deocracy as a olitical syste. Curiously, however, the roble has co letely droed out o discussion in hilosohy. Why so? Here is a suggestion. On one concetion o norative olitical hilosohy, the goal is to describe the noratively ideal coonents o an ideal liberal deocratic state. But in an ideal liberal deocratic state there is no roaganda. So roaganda as a toic is no longer visible.

In ideal political theory, the roble o how to ove ro an actually awed state guided incoletely by liberal de ocratic ideals to an ideal liberal deocratic state is known as the transition problem. It is usually osed in ters o how to change ro an unjust distribution o goods to a just distribution o goods. The tension between two liberal values, rights to rivate roerty and equality, is at the center. Given the sel concetion o olitical hilosohy as the study o roerties o the ideal liberal deocratic state, the transition roble counts as "alied" olitical hilosohy. To label soething as "alied" in hilosohy is to arginalize its study. Ethics is "ure" hilosohy; alied ethics is "iure" hilosohy, t or those who cannot absorb the disciline in its ure or.

It is ossible to rae the roble o roaganda in ters o the transition roble. The question would be how to transition ro a kind o deliberation that is not deocratic to genuine deocratic deliberation without violating the reedo o seech.3 Ideal olitical theory does thereore allow or a sace to address what one ay arguably regard as the ost central question o deocratic olitical theory. It is a question, yet to be addressed in the disciline, o applied political philosophy. It is to be addressed at soe oint aer the issue o how to ove ro an unjust distribution o goods to a just distribution o goods is solved.

The roble raised or liberal deocracy by roaganda is whether the ost central exression o its value, liberty (realized as the reedo o seech), akes liberal deocracy undaentally unstable. The concetion o norative olitical hilosohy I have sketched allows a sace, though a arginalized one, to address the question. But it hinders insight into the historical centrality o the roble. Rousseau very clearly attributes to all olitical hilosohers o his tie the view that "no olity o the kind I have described [a deocracy] could ossibly be ket going within those states." But the idea that it could ose soe kind o undaental concetual roble to liberal deocracy is now dicult to understand.

Charles Mills has develoed this oint into an objection to the very ethodology o ideal theory:

[I]deal theory either tacitly reresents the actual as a sile deviation ro the ideal, not worth theorizing in its own right, or clais that starting ro the ideal is at least the best way o realizing it. . . . Alost by denition, it ollows ro the ocus o ideal theory that little or nothing will be said on actual historic oppression and its legacy in the present, or current ongoing oppression, though these may be gestured at in a vague or promissory way (as something to be dealt with later).4

In the same paper, Mills argues that ideal theory presupposes an "idealized cognitive sphere," in which "[a] general social transparency will be presumed, with cognitive obstacles minimized as limited to biases of self-interest or the intrinsic difficulties of understanding the world, and little or no attention paid to the distinctive role of hegemonic ideologies and group-specific experience in distorting our perceptions and conceptions of the social order." In other words, topics such as flawed ideology and propaganda clearly fall outside the scope of the ideal theoretic project.

In previous work, Mills calls our attention to the nonideal character of the classic texts in political philosophy:

The classic texts of the central thinkers of the Western political tradition—for example, Plato, Hobbes, Locke, Burke, Marx—typically provide not merely normative judgments but mappings of social ontologies and political epistemologies which explain why the normative judgments of others have gone astray. These theorists recognized that to bring about the ideal polity, one needs to understand how the structure and workings of the actual polity may interfere with our perception of the social truth.5

Political philosophy concerns itself with normative judgments, how things ought to be. One view of normativity holds that it is only reasonable to hold someone to what they ought to do if it is within the bounds of her capacity to do it. If one ought to obey a law, it must be possible to obey that law. Otherwise, that law sets an impossible demand. On this view, the normative judgments of political philosophy are bounded by what is within the reasonable capacity to expect of human societies. On another view of normativity, ideals can guide even if they cannot be completely realized. But even this view is bounded by the possibility of being guided by those ideals; it may be that sufficiently remote ideals cannot function to guide in human societies. The study of what is within the reasonable capacity of human societies is social theory.

The view that political philosophy can be done without social theory presupposes that social theory will not place significant and unexpected restrictions on political possibility. Therefore, political philosophy without social theory involves extreme idealization in the construction of its models. As the philosopher Kwame Anthony Appiah has argued, the idealizations involved in ideal theoretic political philosophy are akin to the ideals involved in the decision theoretic axioms governing rationality. My view of the decision theoretic ideals is that they provide a false and distorting view of the ordinary picture of rationality when they are considered as ideals for agents with bounded rationality like us. I hold a similar view for ideal conceptions of the state, when such ideas are theorized without simultaneous attention to social theory.6

This book is clearly influenced by Mills. However, it is easy to misunderstand Mills in ways in which it may also be easy to misunderstand the project here. There is a commonly held view that politics is simply about power and interests, and the political vocabulary is only ever used strategically. The rhetoric of political and moral ideals is just one more weapon in a game whose object is to seize power and, along with it, the goods of society. One might worry that Mills's objections to ideal theory come from this dark perspective.

In a book published in 1901, Vilfredo Pareto begins the twentieth century with an articulation of the view that politics is simply about power and interests:

Buddhism, which proclaimed the equality of all men, has generated the theocracy of Tibet; and the religion of Christ, which seemed especially made for the poor and humble, has generated the Roman theocracy.... The decline of the old elite and its increasing arrogance at the time of the Reformation can be clearly seen in the emergence of the robber barons: Sickingen and Hutten are two types of such a revolutionary knighthood. As usual, the new elite leaned on the poor and humble; as usual, these believed in the promises made to them; as usual they were deceived, and the yoke weighed even heavier on their shoulders than before. Similarly, the revolution of 1789 produced the Jacobin oligarchy and ended with the imperial despotism. This is what has always happened and there is no reason to believe that the usual course of events should change now.7

The view is also clearly articulated in the works of Carl Schmitt, who writes, "[A]ll political concepts, images, and terms have a polemical meaning. They are focused on a specific conflict and are bound to a concrete situation; the result (which manifests itself in war or revolution) is a friend-enemy grouping, and they turn into empty and ghost-like abstractions when this situation disappears."8

Because of the enduring controversy of Schmitt's National Socialist political affiliations, he is not the official midtwentieth-century spokesperson for the view that politics is only about power and interests. But it is a not uncommon view in political and social science that the complexities of administration make democratic accountability too difficult to be realistically managed by a society substantively guided by democratic norms. On this view, the democratic vocabulary has no application, and should instead be employed by those versed in economics and policy—what we now call "experts"—to mask from the masses an illiberal and undemocratic reality. The wide contemporary acceptance of the antidemocratic position is due to the fact that what could be regarded as an authoritarian conclusion has been presented in the economic language of efficiency.

It is one thing to dismiss descriptions of the regular misuse of democratic political ideals when they are presented alongside National Socialist ideology or the Orwellian ideology o certain branches o conteorary social science. It is uch ore dicult to disiss such descritions when they issue ro the ens o hilosohers suering under the yoke o oression. The orer eschew norative vocabulary in their descrition o what they take to be the inevitable olitical reality. The latter are clearly engaged in a norative roject o critique. There is no ossibility, in reading Delany, W. E. B. Du Bois, C. L. R. Jaes, or Charles Mills, o construing the as endorsing the elites' use o the concets o liberal de ocracy to seize ower. It is clearly a deand or oression to be hilosohically addressed, rather than a hilosohical endorseent o the echanis o oression. It is not inconsistent with ideal theory. It is a deand or the reorulation o the task o norative olitical hilosohy to lace social theory on equal ooting. It is why any olitical hilosohers who are ebers o oressed grous sel- describe as working in "social and olitical hilosohy," whereas ebers o rivileged grous oen sel- describe as working in "olitical hilosohy."

Philosohy, classically understood, has as its task both the resentation o reality and the exlanation o the illusions that deceive us ro recognizing it. Norative olitical hiloso hy so conceived should have aong its ost central tasks not just a deense o the olitical ideal that is so oen asked by olitical illusion, but also an exlanation o olitical illusion itsel. Norative olitical hilosohy that ails to lace olitical illusion at the sae level o iortance as olitical ideal aces the legitiate objection that the ractice o norative olitical hilosohy is itsel art o the achinery that roduces illusion: in this case, the illusion that there is no illusion.

In The Republic, Plato is clear that one task o hilosohy is to cast o illusions. His worry with the deocratic city is that ost o its ebers are not hilosohers and suer under the illusions that hilosohers have diselled. The liberty granted by deocracy gives tyrants the ower o illusion over the asses. Deocracy suers ro "an excess o liberty" that "sees destined to end u in slavery" (564a).

Plato and Joseh Goebbels, the Reich inister o ro aganda, were both eneies o deocracy, Plato because he thought it was too likely to be exloited by eole such as Goebbels. There are dierences between Plato's concetion o deocracy and the Weiar Reublic, the deocracy whose reedos gave rise to National Socialis. It is nevertheless lausible to take Plato as drawing our attention to the oint Joseh Goebbels is aking in the eigrah to this book. The liberties allowed by deocracy too easily allow deagogues to seize ower and thereby end deocracy. The risk is too great that soeone who is in act a "towering desot" (566d) will reresent hisel as a rotector o the eole and seize ower. This is the classic stateent o the roble roaganda oses or deocracy. Deocracy is a syste o sel- rule that is su osed to axiize liberty. Freedo o seech, esecially ublic olitical seech, cannot be restricted in a deocracy. But the unrestricted use o roaganda is a serious threat to deocracy.

In book 1 o The Social Contract, Jean- Jacques Rousseau oses a question: "Is a ethod o associating discoverable, which will deend and rotect, with all the collective ight, the erson and roerty o each associate, and in virtue o which each associate, though he becoes a eber o the grou, nevertheless obeys hisel, and reains as ree as be ore?" Rousseau's solution is the social contract. There is a tacit agreeent between ebers o a civil society to lace the selves under the sae laws, which "lace the sae burden on everybody." What results is a "collective oral body," which Rousseau calls a Sovereign Power. The sovereign is a state that "consists exclusively o the individuals who are its ebers" and hence "has no interest that goes against theirs, and cannot ossibly have such an interest."9

Rousseau calls the reedo huans have in the state o nature natural liberty. Natural liberty, or Rousseau, is a erson's "unliited right to anything that [she] is teted by and can get." The problem Rousseau seeks to answer is how, in civil society, humans can remain free, despite losing their natural liberty. Rousseau's solution is to motivate a distinct kind of liberty. Natural liberty, for Rousseau, is more often than not mere "motivation by sheer apatite." Genuine liberty is, for Rousseau, different than this; it is what he calls *civil liberty*. By being a citizen in a sovereign power, one gains civil liberty. It is "obedience to self-imposed law," of the sort formed by deliberation in citizens' assemblies, which is genuine freedom. True freedom, for Rousseau, is accepting the decisions that are arrived at by a majority under conditions of full civil liberty, that is, political equality.

The purpose of the assemblies is for each citizen to vote about possible policies for the Sovereign Power. The citizens have a "rudimentary right to vote on every act of the sovereign. This nothing can take away from them, any more than their right to express opinions, offer proposals, disagree, and discuss—the latter being a right that the government is always at great pains to reserve for its members." About the method of deliberation, Rousseau writes, "Men who are simple and upright are hard to deceive."12 But Rousseau is clear about the risks to a Sovereign Power: "If ... debates drag themselves out, if counsels are divided, if voices are raised, this heralds the ascendancy of private interests and the decline of the state."13 A mark of the declining state is when "the people, swayed by intimidation or flattery, now acts by acclamation rather than by casting votes; it has ceased to deliberate, and either worships or damns."14

For the people to be the rulers, there must be genuine individual deliberation. Having one's vote be the result of forcible coercion is not deliberation. But when Rousseau speaks of being "swayed by intimidation or flattery," he does not mean compelling others via forcible coercion. He means something that results in the appearance of an autonomous vote but is not. He uses the word "deception" to describe what is occurring in such cases, cases that are instances of "acclamation" rather than "casting votes." Deagogic roaganda, in the or o "intiidation or attery," leads to acclaation rather than "casting votes." This is a classic stateent o the roble roaganda oses or deocracy.15

The hilosoher Stehen Darwall singles out a notion o resect he calls recognition respect. He distinguishes between two kinds o recognition resect. The rst is the kind o resect one has between equals, with those one regards as having the right "to clai and deand" equal treatent. This is resecting the dignity o soeone as a ellow huan being, a erson whose ersective ust be given equal weight.16 The second kind o recognition resect is that derived ro "recognizing or honoring" soeone as having "soe secic social role, status, or lace that, in rincile, not everyone can have." Darwall calls this honor respect. 17 Honor resect is the aroriate governing rincile o a onarchy, as it is the aroriate set o attitudes to underlie obedience to authority. Resect o the rst kind, treating "eole equally without regard to their status or social lace," underlies the ossibility o genuine deocratic deliberation.18 This is what I have called political equality. Rousseau's oint is that when casting votes descends into "acting by acclaation," it transors into onarchy or authoritarian rule. This is Rousseau's way o describing the descent ro deocracy to tyranny described in book 8 o The Republic.

Rousseau's concetion o liberty is "obedience to sel iosed law." A coon colaint is that Rousseau does not ake roo or the reservation o individual liberty, and hence he has been criticized as deending a syste that allows or desotis. As the French Enlightenent olitical hilosoher Benjain Constant wrote, what Rousseau oits is that "[t]he citizens ossess individual rights indeendently o all social and olitical authority, and any authority which violates these rights becoes illegitiate. The rights o the citizens are individual reedo, religious reedo, reedo o oinion, which includes the reedo to exress onesel oenly, the enjoyent o roerty, a guarantee against all arbitrary ower."19

The general concetion o liberty eerging ro Rousseau is one that ay allow or laws against roaganda. Aer all, it does not obviously lace unrestricted ree seech at its center. I we relace Rousseau's concetion o liberty with prevention from arbitrary domination, we obtain a dierent basis or ex loring the restriction o the robleatic use o roaganda.20 The way to argue or restrictions on seech with a so- called negative concetion o liberty is to show that certain kinds o seech are silencing, as the hilosohers Jennier Hornsby and Rae Langton have argued. The essence o olitical roaganda on y aroach is that it is a kind o seech that undaentally involves olitical, econoic, aesthetic, or rational ideals, obilized or a olitical urose. Supporting Propaganda: A contribution to ublic discourse that is resented as an ebodient o certain ideals, yet is o a kind that tends to increase the realization o those very ideals by either eotional or other nonrational eans.

Undermining Propaganda: A contribution to ublic discourse that is resented as an ebodient o certain ideals, yet is o a kind that tends to erode those very ideals.24

Underining roaganda involves a kind o contradiction between ideal and goal. It's an arguent that aeals to an ideal to draw suort, in the service o a goal that tends to erode the realization o that ideal.

I will restrict attention or the oent to roaganda that suorts or underines a political ideal (where this is broadly construed). Suorting roaganda can be used in the service o worthy goals, neutral goals, or unworthy goals. The goal o suorting roaganda is to increase the realization o a olitical ideal. It does so not by directly roviding a reason that increases the robability o the truth or virtue o the olitical ideal. It does so indirectly by seeking to overload various aective caacities, such as nostalgia, sentient, or ear. These eotional eects can lead to the discovery o reasons, reasons that in turn will suort the olitical ideal in a characteristically rational way. But suorting roaganda does not suort the goal that hels to realize the ideal via belies that aeal solely to what Ianuel Kant calls the rational will: the aculty rational beings have o acting in accordance with rational law.25 Suorting roaganda is intended to close o ossibilities and ove eotion behind a goal that urthers an exlicitly rovided ideal.

The denition I have given o underining roaganda is very secic. It requires the call to action to be one that runs counter to the very olitical ideal it is exlicitly reresented as ebodying. Daniel Putna (in ersonal corresondence) suggested to e a ore exansive characterization o roaganda, according to which one olitical ideal is being deloyed to wear down another olitical ideal held within the sae olitical syste. Consider, or exale, the olitical ideal o liberty. This is a olitical ideal o the United States. Another olitical ideal o the United States is opportunity. This is also a olitical ideal o the United States. One ight characterize as an instance o roaganda an aeal to liberty in the service o wearing down the olitical ideal o oortunity (say, an aeal to cut taxes because o econoic liberties, the consequence o which is to reduce oortunity or ioverished citizens). This would not be an instance o suorting roaganda according to the characterization I have given. Here is a ore inclusive characterization o underining roaganda according to which it does count as underining roaganda:

Undermining Propaganda2 : A contribution to ublic discourse that is resented as an ebodient o certain olitical ideals, but is o a kind that tends to erode a olitical ideal that belongs in the sae aily.

My worry with this ore inclusive characterization is that it threatens to overgeneralize. Soeties olitical ideals si ly do conict; in act, any hold that there is such a con ict between liberty and equality. My concern with Putna's roosed denition is that it will entail that what are, in act, exales o genuine conict between olitical ideals are isclassied as underining roaganda.

The characterizations o roaganda I have given are characterizations o what it is or contributions, aradigatically utterances or iages, to be roaganda. But we also seak o newsaers and schools as being vehicles of propaganda. It is teting to dene a vehicle o roaganda as ollows:

Vehicle for the Production of Propaganda: A site or echanis or the roduction o roaganda.

A edia source or a school can be a vehicle o roaganda, even though it never roduces actual instances o roaganda. It can, or exale, be a vehicle o roaganda in virtue o withholding crucial inoration that, by its nature, it is su osed to rovide. A news station that reresents itsel as roviding all the relevant news or olitical decision aking is, intuitively, a vehicle o roaganda i it regularly withholds highly relevant news or olitical decision aking. A school is a vehicle or roaganda i it reresents itsel as roviding all the relevant inoration or being an inored citizen, yet regularly withholds inoration or being an inored citizen. These are the kinds o vehicles o roaganda we should exect to nd in liberal deocracies. Characterizing vehicles o roaganda in this sense is thereore o crucial iortance to the ais o this book.

Here is the characterization o a sense o a vehicle o roaganda that is erhas ore useul or theorizing about roaganda in liberal deocracy:

Vehicle of Propaganda: An institution that reresents itsel as dened by a certain olitical ideal, yet whose ractice tends to underine the realization o that ideal.

A school in a liberal deocracy is intended to ake its students into inored citizens who have the inoration necessary or inored articiation in olitical deliberation. Su ose the school intentionally leaves out certain inoration, or exale, about the country's systeatic injustice toward certain grous. The students it roduces thereore have inco lete inoration, but believe the inoration to be colete. The school thereore underines the ideal o having ully in ored citizens. A school that roduces artially inored citizens who believe they are ully inored is a vehicle o roaganda, even if it never produces any actual propagandistic claims. Siilarly, i a television news station or newsaer resents itsel as reorting all relevant news or olitical decision aking in a country, yet withholds crucial inoration or decision aking, it too is a vehicle o roaganda, even i it never roduces any actual roagandistic utterances. It is a vehicle o roaganda in virtue o underining the olitical ideal it reresents itsel as ebodying, that o ully inoring its audience.26

I have now characterized two dierent tyes o roaganda, as well as two ways in which institutions can serve as echaniss or the roduction o roaganda. My toic in this book is olitical roaganda, roaganda that exloits olitical ideals, rather than other kinds o ideals. But any ors o advertising are obviously cases o roaganda and count as such according to the characterization I have given. An advertiseent that uses, or exale, the ideal o good health in the service o selling a roduct that underines health is roaganda and counts as such according to y characterization. For exale, an advertiseent that uses ictures o healthy rock clibers to sell an unhealthy beverage or ood ite uses the ideal o good health in the service o a goal that can be easily seen to underine it. So any advertiseents sily are instances o roaganda.

However, another class o advertiseents seeks to associate a goal with an ideal that is sily irrelevant to that ideal. For exale, advertiseents standardly use aesthetic ideals to ro ote a roduct, ossession o which is irrelevant to the urther realization o that aesthetic ideal. An advertiseent that suggests that urchasing a certain kind o car will ake one ore attractive is an exale o this. I we bear in ind that any things we would call "advertiseents" are straightorwardly instances o roaganda, it is still useul to isolate a theoretical category o advertising that catures this class o advertiseents:

Advertising: A contribution to ublic discourse that is resented as an ebodient o certain ideals, but in the service o a goal that is irrelevant to those very ideals.

Coercial advertising is an attet to attach ossession o the roduct advertised to an attractive ideal, when ossessing that roduct is in the noral case irrelevant to achieving that ideal. Eective advertiseent exloits awed ideology that connects, or exale, aterial ossessions to aesthetic worth.

Underining roaganda by its nature underines a olitical ideal. It underines a olitical ideal by using it to co unicate a essage that is inconsistent with it. As I will ex lain below, it is able to achieve the task by exloiting already existing awed ideological belief, and even contributing to the oration o such belie. It is awed ideological belief that masks the contradictions of undermining propaganda.

Flawed ideological belie asks the contradictions o underining roaganda by erecting dicult eisteic obstacles to recognizing tendencies o goals to isalign with certain ideals: or exale, obstructions to understanding liberal deocratic concets and what they entail. In chaters 5 and 6, the chaters on ideology, we will investigate the nature o these eisteic obstacles, and exlain why they are so dicult to surount.

Underining roaganda is thus far more complicated than suorting roaganda. Judging the oral as well as the olitical accetability o an instance o underining roaganda is corresondingly a vexed and colex atter. But there are any other acts at lay in evaluating the accetability o a use o underining roaganda. We can, aer all, evaluate orally and olitically the means by which soeone seeks to achieve a goal. We ust not ignore the ossibility that under ining roaganda is a deocratically unaccetable means, even in cases in which the essage or goal is worthy (or the olitical ideal being underined is unworthy).

Ianuel Kant aously argued that lying was "the greatest violation o an's duty to hisel."27 Kant rejected even lying or a noble urose, categorically rejecting the sread o untruthulness into relationshis between ersons. I have argued that roaganda can be both sincere and true. Nevertheless, insoar as roaganda o either variety is a ethod to byass the rational will o others in the service o soe goal, the Kantian would regard roaganda in either sense as a oral violation. In the Kantian sense, roaganda, regardless o goals, is not orally accetable. The ethical basis o Kantian hilosohy has an undeniable urchase on our intuitive oral judgents, and this is no doubt the source o the sense that roaganda, regardless o its goal, is orally robleatic. For Kant, the rational will is inially one that oerates inde endently o alien causes and is subject to rational rinciles. But roaganda runs counter to rational rinciles. Insoar as a or o roaganda is a kind o aniulation o rational beings toward an end without engaging their rational will, it is a kind o decetion.28

I do not here resuose a Kantian theory o orality. But even rescinding ro a oral evaluation o roaganda as such, one ust worry that ost arguents that eloy underining roaganda are deocratically susect.

Let's now look at soe exales o suorting and under ining roaganda. Exales o suorting roaganda are sile to rovide. One exale o suorting roaganda is the use o a country's ag, or the aeal to a roantic vision o the country's history, to strengthen atriotis. A second exale is delivering a very rightening ublic health warning to raise excessive ears about (or exale) soking, with the goal o increasing ublic health by the use o exaggerated ear. A third exale is aealing to ast wrongs against a grou to strengthen ethnic ride and sel- identication. For exale, Slobodan Milosevic, the orer resident o Serbia, regularly aealed to the deeat o Serbians in the Battle o Kosovo to instill a sense o historical grievance in those o Serbian ethnicity, thereby strengthening their ethnic identication.

I have said that roaganda is invariably deocratically robleatic. As will eerge in the next chater, there is a kind o unrobleatic and indeed necessary or o roaganda. The reason it is necessary is that it is necessary to eloy when there are undeocratic eatures o a state that need to be addressed. So it is not itsel art o deocratic deliberation, roerly conceived. But there are certain exales o suorting roaganda that are erectly accetable in a deocracy. For exale, cigarette acks in any deocratic countries carry stark warnings, such as "cigarettes kill." These are clearly intended to overwhel aective caacities to urther the realization o ideals, in this case, the ideal o health. But ro agandistic warning labels on cigarettes see deocratically accetable, in a way that Milosevic's roaganda is not. What is the dierence?

We can think o a inistry o health as tasked to look out or the hysical health o a deocratic nation when its citizens do not have tie to do the relevant research. In a sense, there ore, we task the inistry o health with giving us warnings that will convey a essage that will have the eect o doing all the work o inoring us about the relevant health issue. In the case o warning labels on cigarette acks, resuably the idea is that we have tacitly granted our erission to the inistry o health to take such stes. I we have not tacitly granted our erission in this anner, then such warning labels are deocratically robleatic.29

Let us now turn to soe basic exales o underining roaganda. Here is the rst. Carl Hart discusses stubling across exlicit racist ideology surrounding Blacks and cocaine, in the early art o the twentieth century. He cites the ollowing assage ro 1914, by a edical doctor in the New York Times, exressing the view that Blacks have an excetional reaction to that drug:

Most o the negroes are oor, illiterate, and shiless . . . Once the negro has ored the habit he is irreclaiable. The only ethod to kee hi away ro the drug is by irisoning hi. And this is erely alliative treatent, or he returns to the drug habit when realized.30

Crack cocaine is a degraded or o the drug avored by wealthy elite in cities. Yet during the "drug war" in the 1980s and 1990s, oliticians successully argued or 100– 1 sentencing disarities between the degraded or that urban Blacks could aord and the urer version avored by wealthy whites. Hart ersuasively argues that it is this awed ideology, so exlicit in the early part of the twentieth century, which operated tacitly during the worst days of the "drug war," when politicians convinced citizens that these sentencing disparities were consistent with law and order. The flawed ideology of Black exceptionalism with regard to reactions to drugs masks the contradiction between the attractive ideal of law and order, or justice, and the otherwise obviously unjust sentencing disparities between the degraded version of the substance used by poor Blacks and the purer version favored by wealthy Whites. The goal of establishing the sentencing disparities is not consistent with law and order, but the ideal used in the service of that goal is law and order.

Here is a second example of undermining propaganda. According to James Hoggan, in his book Climate Cover-Up, the American Petroleum Institute created a team to assemble a "Global Climate Change Communication Action Plan." According to Hoggan, "The document plainly states that its purpose is to convince the public, through the media, that climate science is awash in uncertainty."31 Stephen Milloy was a founding member of that team. Hoggan reports that Milloy now appears on Fox News as a "junk science expert." Millov has "spent his entire career in public relations and lobbying, taking money from companies that include Exxon, Philip Morris, The Edison Electric Institute, the International Food Additives Council, and Monsanto in return for his work declaring environmental concerns to be 'junk science.'"32 Milloy's assertions are presented as embodying the ideals of scientific objectivity. However, anyone not convinced by the ideology of the corporate-funded anti-climate science movement would recognize that they clearly conflict with the ideals of scientific objectivity.33

Here is a third example of undermining propaganda. A poster from the Cultural Revolution in China states: "毛主席的无产阶级革命胜利万岁!" Translated literally, it means "Chairman Mao's proletariat revolution triumphs ten thousand years!" Slightly more loosely, "Let Chairman Mao's roletariat revolution triuh orever!" Here are the literal corresondences between the hrases:

毛主席的 Chairan Mao's 无产阶级 Proletariat 革命 Revolution 胜利 Triuh 万岁 Ten thousand years; etahorically: orever

The hrase that is robleatic is "万岁," or, literally, "ten thousand years." For thousands o years it was used ainly in the sentence "皇帝万岁万岁万万岁!," which citizens would shout toward the eeror. Literally, it says, "Eeror ten thousand years [reetition ignored here]," eaning o course, "Long live the eeror!" The word "万岁" is directly associated with the long rule o an eeror. Here is what is haening. The olitical ideal o a onarchy is obedience to authority. Revolution is being taken as the ebodient o obedience to authority. But it is eective because o the existence o a awed ideology connecting revolution to the will o a state that akes decisions on one's behal. Those who ossess this awed ideology would not see the clear contradiction between revolution and obedience to authority.

Here is a ourth exale. In the US Suree Court decision Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission, written in 2010, the US Suree Court decided that the rights in the US Constitution extended to cororations. The Court resented the decision as i extending ree seech and other rights to cororations was like the Civil Rights Act, naely, an extension o rights to hitherto unrecognized ersons. It was thereore resented as an ebodient o the rinciles o deocracy. Yet the unliited cororate donations that Citizens United gave rise to are theselves an existential threat to deocracy, roising to hand over the echanis o governent to cororations that do the bulk o unding or olitical caaigns.34

Here is a h exale. In the United States, soe Christian televangelists have rooted what has coe to be known as "the Proserity Gosel." According to this doctrine, Jesus shows his avor by disensing wealth. Acceting Jesus is the way to acquire roserity. I one wants roserity, one should accet the Christian aith. Thus, the goal o roserity is advanced as an ebodient o Christian aith. Yet Jesus is as clear as ossible that this could not be correct. In the Gosel o Matthew, Jesus akes clear that it is the oor and ersecuted who are blessed, not the wealthy. Only by acceting a awed ideology linking the aterialist values o caitalis to the doctrines o Christianity could one ail to see the obvious inconsistency between the doctrines esoused by Jesus and the goal o roserity.

Here is a sixth exale. In the National Socialist ress, the Jews were described as a ublic health threat, as in Hitler's clai that "Jews are the Black Death." The clai that "Jews are the Black Death" is clearly a ublic health alert. It akes an assertion that urorts to be true and rovides a genuine reason that reasonable eole ust take into account. Peole in the gri o a awed anti- Seitic ideology delivered the clai sincerely. However, any Gerans at the tie in act were Jewish. The ublic health threat was inconsistent with the health o a grou o citizens o that country. So the content o the clai was clearly inconsistent with the olitical ideals it reresented itsel as ebodying.35

Here is a seventh exale. In 1935, W. E. B. Du Bois ublished the book Black Reconstruction in America: 1860– 1880. In it, Du Bois challenges the then revailing view, exelied by the "Colubia School" o historians, John Burgess and Willia Dunning, that the end o Reconstruction was brought about by incaacity o reed Black citizens to govern the selves. Du Bois's alternative account o the ailure o Reconstruction is that white econoic elites exloited the racis o oor whites to revent oor whites and newly reed Blacks ro joining together in a labor oveent with unied class interests. Though it took several decades, the accuracy o Du Bois's account o why Reconstruction cae to an end has long since been widely acknowledged. At the tie, Du Bois's correct reading was disregarded, in avor o a aniestly racist interretation. In the nal chater o the book, Du Bois argues that Burgess and Dunning's view underines history, by twisting its ideals o truth and narrative accuracy to the service o doinance and ower. The chater is called in "The Proaganda o History," and in it he writes:

I history is going to be scientic, i the record o huan action is going to be set down with that accuracy and aith ulness o detail which will allow its use as a easuring rod and guideost or the uture o nations, there ust be set soe standards o ethics in research and interretation.

I, on the other hand, we are going to use history or our leasure and auseent, or inating our national ego, and giving us a alse but leasurable sense o accolish ent, then we ust give u the idea o history either as a science or as an art using the results o science, and adit rankly that we are using a version o historical act in order to inuence and educate the new generation along the way we wish.36

Du Bois here criticizes Burgess and Dunning, and white history o the Reconstruction ore generally, as roaganda. It is roaganda, because it aeals to the ideals o history in the service o goals, ower, and interest, which underine the ideals o truth and science.

The seven exales o underining roaganda I have given are uniorly negative. It is worthwhile to look at exa les o underining roaganda that targets a robleatic ideal. In his aer "Criteria o Negro Art" ro 1926, Du Bois calls on the Black artist to engage in roaganda to reresent the huanity and value o her eole. Yet he argues that the Black artist cannot sily resent the case or Black huanity directly:

Suose you were to write a story and ut in it the kind o eole you know and like and iagine. You ight get it ublished and you ight not. And the "ight not" is still ar bigger than the "ight." The white ublishers catering to white olk would say, "It is not interesting"— to white olk, naturally not. They want Uncle Tos, Tosies, good "darkies" and clowns.37

Du Bois recognizes that sily directly aealing to whites by showing the the Black ersective will not work. An indirect ethod is required to stir white interest, one that aeals "to white olk," yet will soehow call attention to the Black ersective. Du Bois is calling or a certain kind o underining roaganda.

According to the usicologist Ingrid Monson's analysis o John Coltrane's version o "My Favorite Things," a oular Christas song ro that iconic cineatic celebration o whiteness, The Sound of Music, is a way o taking a white aesthetic ideal and using it to reresent the Black Aerican voice and exerience.38 I so, it is an exale o Du Bois's aeal. Monson writes:

[Coltrane's] transoration o "My Favorite Things," or what Gates would ter signication uon the tune, inverts the iece on nearly every level. It akes the interludes, not the verse, the subject o the erorance; it transors waltz tie into a olyrhythically textured six- eel; and it trans ors a sentiental, otiistic lyric into a vehicle or a ore brooding irovisational exloration. Since the lyrics would have been on the sheet usic the song lugger brought to the quartet, Coltrane would have been well aware o the e hasis on white things in the lyric— girls in white dresses, snowakes on eyelashes, silver white winters, crea- colored onies. In 1960— a year o treendous escalation in the Civil Rights oveent and a tie o growing oliticization o the jazz counity— there was certainly the ossibility that Coltrane looked uon the lyrics with an ironic eye.39

Monson's ersuasive analysis o Coltrane's "My Favorite Things" reresents it as an exelar o Du Bois's call. Coltrane takes the song and gives it a owerul subversive twist, resenting a white aesthetic ideal in a ashion that subverts it to reveal Black exerience and Black identity.40

Aer the killing o Michael Brown in Ferguson, Missouri, in the suer o 2014, the singer Lauryn Hill released a song she had been laying live called "Black Rage." The song is also a version o "My Favorite Things." "My Favorite Things" contains lines like "crea- colored onies and cris ale strudels"; Lauryn Hill's version contains lines like "raings and beatings and suering that worsens." Whether or not Coltrane intended his version o "My Favorite Things" to be roaganda in the sense o Du Bois's call in "Criteria o Negro Art," it is undeniable that Lauryn Hill exlicitly intended it to be so. A song extolling avorite things that are racially and culturally white but assued to be universal is used to exlain the daaging consequences on Blacks o the racial ideology in which that aesthetic ideal is ebedded.

Du Bois does not restrict his call to eloy roaganda to iel whites to recognize the ersectives o Black citizens to the aesthetic real. The rhetorical structure Du Bois describes in "Criteria o Negro Art" has had inuence in diverse areas o intellectual roduction. A good exelar in the eld o hilosohy is Toie Shelby's aer "Justice, Deviance, and the Dark Ghetto," ublished in 2007. The toic o the aer is the oral criticiss o Black inner- city "ghetto" youth, and it exlains what constitutes certain ors o "deviance," naely, "crie, reusing to work in legitiate jobs, and having contet or authority."41 In short, the aer concerns the ideal o obedience to authority and the ursuit o legitiate legal work, taken unrestrictedly. The aer ocuses on the ors o deviance involved in deviating ro these ideals. But in ocusing on those ors o deviance, it ends u deonstrating that the unrestricted or o the ideal ust be rejected.

The aer is resented as an attet to justiy a white ideology that we are in conditions o justice, and so the ideals o obedience to authority and ursuit o legitiate work can be excetionless. However, what the aer in act does is undermine the excetionless ideal, by rejecting the resuosition that we are in a just state; the conditions o the dark ghetto reresent a "ailure o recirocity." So, or exale, Black re usal to take enial jobs is not a or o deviant "laziness," but a rejection o an unjust social order. This is an exale o the structure urged by Du Bois. Shelby's aer targets the excetionless generalization that one should be obedient to authority and ursue legitiate work. It sees at rst to rely on that generalization, and ursue the question o how it is to be alied. Shelby's discussion however eventually reveals that it is alse by rejecting the idealizing resuosition, using the exale o the unjust conditions in "the dark ghetto."

Du Bois's call or roaganda is designed to deal with situations in which a doinant grou is suering under a awed ideology that leads the to ebrace a robleatic ideal, or a robleatic concetion o an ordinary ideal. It is hard to see how direct challenges to the ideals will be eective. Du Bois's roosal is to wra challenges to the ideal in the teting vocabulary o the ideal itsel. It is a novel and owerul rhetorical suggestion.42

There are hard cases that ay not aear to t the odel I have sketched. Liberalis is a view in olitical hilosohy that laces the ideals o autonoy and equality above all others. There are, however, olitical views that are reresented as ebodying liberalis yet interret soe o these ideals in a robleatically narrow way. For exale, what we ight call neoliberalism treats coetitive arkets as the way all goods should be allocated (I ake no clai to accuracy about the descrition o any reexisting view here). One ight naturally think, in the absence o a theoretical arguent, that an arguent or neoliberalis that, or exale, aeals to the ounding athers' concetion o liberalis to justiy the view that arkets are the only legitiate way to divide goods that is consistent with liberty is a deoration or underining o the ideals o liberalis in a way that is characteristic o under ining roaganda. Yet arket exchanges are included in the scoe o liberal autonoy. Markets are a legitiate eans o dividing u soe goods, and dividing soe goods u in this way is allowed by any version o liberalis. So one ight worry that arguents or neoliberalis that are raed as deenses o classical liberalis do not count as roaganda, because arkets are not inconsistent with the doain o liberal reedo.

Let's assue that there is no ersuasive arguent that arket exchange is central to liberalis. I so, then reresenting, or exale, the liberalis o the ounding athers o the United States as the view that every division o goods ust be a arket exchange does count as underining roaganda. It counts as underining roaganda because it is no art o the liberalis o Jaes Madison, or exale, that arkets ossess this universal doain. So on the assution that there is no ersuasive arguent rivileging arket exchange in this way, arguents or neoliberalis that rae it as the exression o the liberalis o (or exale) the ounders o the United States are roagandistic.

There are certain traditional views o liberalis that rivilege arket exchange. But it is nevertheless underining ro aganda to resent even these views as the view that what now asses or arket exchange in the United States is this kind o liberalis, or exale, as the kind o view o liberalis esoused by Ada Sith in The Wealth of Nations. Sith has a very articular concetion o arket exchange, one connected to political equality. 43 But the syste o arket exchange revalent in the United States is one that has systeatically barred certain inorities ro articiation in contracts, or exa le, Blacks ro air ortgages.44 It also tyically involves un air exchanges between rich and oor. I so, it is underining roaganda to reresent the liberalis o Ada Sith as connected to arket exchange as historically racticed in the United States. Market exchange as historically racticed in the United States does not have the connection to olitical equality, air exchange between equals that is at the heart o traditional concetions o liberalis that ehasize arket exchange.

Now that we have drawn the distinction between suorting and underining roaganda, it is sile to characterize the kind o roaganda that is most threatening to liberal de ocracy. The kind o roaganda that is ost threatening to liberal deocracy is a secies o underining roaganda we ay call demagoguery. Deagoguery is roaganda in the service o unworthy olitical ideals. What counts as deagoguery thereore deends on oral and olitical acts. Deagoguery can coe in the or o strengthening unworthy olitical ideals. For exale, Leni Rieenstahl's deiction o Geran athletes in her l Olympia ro 1938 is a glorication o the sueriority o the Teutonic race. But a dierent kind o deagoguery will be o central interest in this book: deagoguery that takes the or o underining roaganda that is resented as ebodying worthy political ideals.

O course, it is a atter o contestation which olitical ideals are worthy. A ascist does not nd the olitical ideals o liberal deocracy worthy. However, our concern in this book is with the roble roaganda raises or liberal deocracy. It is thereore sae to assue or the uroses o this book that the liberal deocratic ideals o liberty, huanity, equality, and objective reason are worthy ideals. In the case o a liberal deocratic state, deagogic seech includes seech that uses liberal deocratic ideals in the service o underining these ideals.

An obvious exale o deagoguery in a liberal deocratic state is occurring at the resent tie, during the writing o this book, in the United States. The Aerican Reublican Party does not draw votes ro Black and Hisanic voters. It has engaged in a ultiyear, concerted, and coletely success ul eort to aeal to the ear o voter iersonation to justiy harsh voter registration laws that eectively disenranchise Black and Hisanic voters. In 2012, a news organization undertook an "exhaustive ublic records search," involving "thousands o requests to elections ocers in all y states, asking or every case o raudulent activity including registration raud, absentee ballot raud, vote buying, alse election counts, caaign raud, casting an ineligible vote, voting twice, voter iersonation raud, and intiidation."45 The study covered a twelve- year eriod between 2000 and 2012 and ound exactly ten cases o voter iersonation out o 146 illion registered voters during that tie. (In that twelve- year eriod, the study uncovered just over two thousand cases o total election raud.) Yet, thirty- seven states as o 2012 had ileented or were considering ileenting tough voter ID laws out o the ear o voter iersonation. Let's take a recent exale o roaganda in a liberal de ocracy that was delivered or a benecial goal.2 The exale coes ro a recent New York Times iece I wrote with y brother, the econoist Marcus Stanley. US scal olicy involves the ways in which the US governent unds its own debt. The exression "the scal cli" was introduced to the broader ublic by Ben Bernanke, the chairan o the Federal Reserve o the United States, in February 2012 to describe the threat to the recovering econoy osed by the conuence o two events. First, Congress was again acing their reeated roise to restore incoe taxes to their levels during the Clinton residency in order to reduce the decit. Secondly, Congress was siultaneously acing large sel- iosed sending cuts (the so- called sequester). Curiously, however, a oll ound that 47 ercent o the ublic thought that it was going over the "cli" that would result in higher decits. Only 14 ercent understood that it would reduce decits. In act, it would have reduced the drastically, eectively eliinating the decit roble.

The oll suggests that the ublic was quite isled. I so, it is not unreasonable to in the blae on the exression "scal cli." Going over clis is clearly a bad thing, ossibly resulting in death. Also, the ajor arties see to agree that decits are a terrible thing. The Deocrats invoke the awulness o decits when discussing additional tax cuts or the wealthy, and the Reublicans invoke decits when conronted with additional entitleent rogras or additional sending on existing ones. A widening government deficit also seems similar to personal borrowing, an excess of which leads to reduced credit scores, calls from bill collectors, and possibly foreclosure. So it was natural to assume that a "fiscal cliff" is simply a metaphorical warning of an especially threatening increase in the deficit.

Let's assume (speculatively but not unreasonably) that Bernanke's introduction of this expression was meant to intentionally steer the debate about this issue. That is, let's assume that Bernanke was well aware that his warning would be misunderstood, and that the misunderstanding would lead to its effectiveness. One reason speaking clearly about deficits is hard is because of the entrenched language that is used to speak about the process by which a government funds its activities, a process that is described as "borrowing." A second reason is that both major parties are invested in using deficit fear strategically.

Describing the process by which the US government funds its activities as involving "borrowing" suggests a false analogy between government borrowing and the borrowing an individual or a family does. The analogy makes some sense for a public entity that does not print its own currency—for example, the state of California or the (Euro-employing) country of Greece. And the analogy also made much more sense during the true gold standard era in this country. For these reasons, and because the government does issue bonds that look like corporate bonds, the vocabulary is entrenched. But a government borrowing in a currency it controls (and can print) has little in common with the borrowing we experience as ordinary citizens. Its benefits—creating jobs and income that prevent a self-perpetuating downward spiral in a slack economy—are not benefits associated with private borrowing. Likewise, its risks—the possibility of inflation, "crowding out" private investment in capital markets, and changing exchange rates—are not factors any individual has experience with through their private borrowing.

Sadly, even many experts do not precisely understand the benefits and risks of government financing through deficits.

The short- ter benets in ters o jobs and incoe are clear (just ask any lobbyist who wants to aintain sending on their riorities or avoid taxation o their clients). But while there are certainly risks to excessive governent debt, controversy continues to rage about when and how these risks occur and what level o debt will create the.
