A great deal o aneuvering on doestic olicy can be understood as the strategic de loyent o the "decit" charge against your oonent while working behind the scenes to kee the debt achine going or your own uroses.3

Bernanke was correct to worry about the consequences o so uch liquidity evaorating ro the US arkets in one go. He chose to handle the situation by relying on the alse belies o the ublic to counicate alar. It was deagoguery because it was a essage that reinorced the ublic's alse belies about econoics, wraed in the antle o the sage advice o the Fed chie. As such, it eroded deocratic ideals. The consequence o Bernanke's ailure to exlain econoic reality was that the ublic reained conused. This allowed oliticians to continue to eloy the ear o a rising decit or olitical uroses, leading to a scal crisis surrounding the debt ceiling in October 2013, which was later shown to take nearly 1 ercent o US GDP (nearly 150 billion dollars) and resulted in the loss o an estiated 750,000 jobs.4 This can be regarded as soe eirical conration o the view evident ro deocratic olitical theory that roaganda that exloits deocratic ideals, even i wielded or a good urose, occludes deocratic deliberation.

Bernanke's goal in using the hrase "scal cli" was laudable: to ove ublic oinion to avoid a devastating loss o jobs. But in so doing, he relied on alse ideological belies about the econoy, rather than lucid exlanation. Bernanke thus set the stage or the subsequent irrational ublic deliberation that receded the debt ceiling crisis in 2013. This is a secic illustration o the risks o deagoguery in a deocracy, even when wielded or a raiseworthy goal. Flawed ideological belies corrode rational debate. In a healthy deocracy, the goal o a ublic ocial should be to dissolve the, rather than rely on the. Relying uon the only strengthens the and akes the uch ore robleatic barriers in subsequent debate.

I have given one secic exale o the dangers o ro aganda in a liberal deocracy. The exale I gave is one in which alse belies were suorted or a good cause. We have seen how this leads inexorably to later robles with deocratic deliberation. The olitical scientist Sarah Sobieraj has devoted a book to docuenting robles roaganda raises in liberal deocracy, even when wielded in suort o worthy causes. For exale, Sobieraj ersuasively argues that roagandizing by activists tends to "sabotage discourse" in ways that ultiately hinder the kind o social change that that very activis seeks to engender.

I now turn to the overview o deocratic olitical ideals in norative olitical hilosohy. I begin with an exlanation o why, in a liberal deocracy, the norative ideals governing ublic olitical seech are political ideals. We then turn to soe o the candidate norative ideals governing ublic olitical seech. Once we have a good sense o the dierent candidate norative ideals governing ublic olitical seech in a deocracy, we will be in a uch better osition to recognize cases o roaganda in liberal deocracies such as the United States. So y goal is to exlain soe basic details o deocratic olitical theory, which will allow us to see both why the ideals governing olitical seech are so iortant in a deocracy and which ideals are the ost lausible. This will take soe work. But it is essential to identiying the structure roaganda takes in a liberal deocracy, since roaganda is resented as ebodying one or another norative ideal o olitical seech.

The idea o a "regular assebly" in which citizens gather to deliberate about just olicy is a core eleent o the Western hilosohical tradition.5 In The Politics Aristotle also akes it clear that the concet o a state involves the idea o "courts . . . who enorce engageents o contracting arties."6 On Aristotle's view in The Politics, an's role as a citizen o the state is to have as chie concerns "the saety o navigation" and "the salvation o a counity." According to Aristotle, "[M]an is by nature a olitical anial."7 The urose o seech "is intended to set orth the exedient and inexedient, and thereore likewise the just and the unjust." Political hilosohy since its ince tion has contained within it the notion that states have certain orus or seech that have a olitically central role, arenas or ublic olitical discourse. Public olitical discourse occurs in inoral gatherings o citizens, as well as in the houses o Congress and in residential debates. The role o the edia is soewhat ore colex, but given its role, discourse about olitics in the news edia too should count as belonging to ublic olitical discourse.

In a liberal deocracy, ublic olitical discourse occurs in olitical debate in elections, between reresentatives seeking to ass olicy in the chabers o governent (such as Congress and the Senate), and in edia discussions o either. In his late essay "The Idea o Public Reason Revisited," John Rawls attets to characterize this real by dening the notion o a public political forum:

the discourse o judges in their decisions, and esecially o the judges o a suree court; the discourse o govern ent ocials, esecially chie executives and legislators; and nally, the discourse o candidates or ublic oce and their caaign anagers, esecially in their ublic oratory, arty lators, and olitical stateents.8

Rawls's essay concerns in art the ideal of public reason, by which he eans the standard that ought to guide debate in ublic olitical orus in a liberal deocracy. But the ideals o ublic reason should not just guide oral orus o the sort Rawls discusses. Citizens gather to seak about olitics in all sorts o informal settings. These inoral settings guide us in our olitical choices. The ideals o ublic reason thereore should aly equally to these inoral settings.

In Senate debate in Seteber 2013, Senator John Mc Cain called on his Reublican colleagues to abandon their strategy o trying to shut down the US governent to halt the i leentation o the Aordable Care Act. Mc Cain said: "We ought as hard as we could in a air and honest anner and we lost. One o the reasons was because we were in the inority, and in deocracies, alost always the ajority governs and asses legislation." Mc Cain's oint was that deocratic citizenshi requires taking yoursel to be subject to the laws that eerge ro a "air and honest" rocess o deliberating aong one's ellow oliticians and the ublic, even when those laws are not the ones that you yoursel suort. The ideals o ublic reason are central to deocratic olitical hilosohy, because it is through debate that is "air and honest" that the deocratic legitiacy o a olicy eerges.

Deocracy is a syste o governent that, inially, reserves the liberty o its citizens by ensuring that they are not subject to arbitrary restrictions. I a olity agrees to laws governing all o its citizens, the rules ust be airly decided uon by the entire ublic, with the ull articiation o all the citizens, or the rules to not illegitiately restrict the liberty o soe o the citizens.

Suose you are art o a grou jointly deliberating about a olicy the grou intends to adot. Perhas it is a town hall eeting about whether to allow racking in exchange or the building o a school or soe jobs. Suose you are in the grou, and the olicy runs counter to your own sel- interest. For exale, erhas your house has a well ed by a sring that is likely to be oisoned by the racking. You are initially there ore oosed. However, the ain advocate o the olicy roduces an arguent that the olicy is best or all, and convinces the ajority to adot the olicy. Suose that you later nd out that the ain advocate was lying, or otherwise eloying deceit. Furtherore, the reason that the ain advocate ushed or that olicy is that she was aid to do so. In such a situation, you would eel tricked. You would eel that the decision to adot that olicy was not legitiate. You would eel that the grou's deand that you adhere to the olicy was also not a legitiate deand. I they orced you to adhere to the olicy, you have legitiate grounds to eel coerced.

In contrast, suose that you are art o a grou deliberating about a olicy that the grou is contelating adoting. The ain advocate o the olicy gives ersuasive arguents that it is in the overall best interest o the counity to adot the olicy. The olicy runs counter to your own sel- interest, but you see that the arguents are correct, and that the olicy is in act best or the counity as a whole. The advocate is honest, and her arguents are good. You vote against it, but you lose. In this case, you don't really eel that you have a co laint. The olicy was arrived at via air deliberation. I the grou deands that you adhere to the olicy, you don't have legitiate grounds to eel coerced.

The rst case we discussed, decision to allow racking, was one in which an unair rocess led to a olicy that was bad or the counity at large. The second case we discussed involved a air rocess that led to a olicy that was good or the counity at large. These are what one ight think o as pure cases. The deliberation and olicy were both unair and bad, or air and good, resectively. What about the impure cases? That is, what about a air deliberative rocess that leads, because o false beliefs due to a flawed ideology, for example, to a policy that is bad for the community? Or what about an unfair deliberative process that bypasses some of the community's unreasonable and irrational members to arrive at a policy that is good for the community? Democratic political theory divides over these impure cases.

According to pure proceduralists about democracy, such as John Rawls and Joshua Cohen, all that matters is that the procedure that leads to the policy is fair. The process of fair democratic deliberation itself leads to the formation of new preferences; democratic deliberation is an expression of one kind of autonomy, the autonomy that is found in rationally choosing one's duties. According to the main version of epistemic theories of democracy, of the sort defended by David Estlund and Hélène Landemore, both procedure and outcome matter. The procedure matters insofar as it leads to outcomes that are better for the citizenry at large. Both the older, pure proceduralist view of democracy and the newer, epistemic version defend fair democratic deliberation. But according to advocates of the epistemic theory of democracy, fair deliberative procedures only have an instrumental value in leading to better overall policy. For pure procedural conceptions of democratic legitimacy, fair deliberation is valuable in and of itself. Both conceptions agree on the value of fair deliberation. One locates democratic legitimacy itself in such deliberation, connecting it to autonomy, while the other tries to explain the value of fair deliberation in terms of its correct outcomes. We will not need to decide between pure proceduralist conceptions of democracy and epistemic theories of democracy, since both rightly presuppose the value of fair joint deliberation. And it is fair joint deliberation that is placed in peril by propaganda.

The policies that result from discussion involving deception and trickery are not democratically legitimate. The person who loses out in a discussion subject to devious machinations is analogous to someone who has lost her freedom in an unjust war. Governance by the rules that emerge from such a process results in domination, rather than preservation of autonomy. In order for the principles decided upon by a group of autonomous agents to have binding force on each of them, without loss of autonomy, the procedure by which the joint decision is made must lend legitimacy to the result. As we have seen, if there is no constraint that the people who are party to the deliberation not simply mislead and lie and evade in order to further their own interests, the results of the deliberation will not be democratically legitimate.

Democracy requires that the policies that apply to everyone must be the result of fair deliberation and equal participation. The reason to impose this constraint is to ensure that the results of the agreement are something that can be the desires of the entire community, by virtue of being the results of such an agreement. The question of the ideals of public reason is the question of what guiding ideals should be the norms of deliberation about laws. It is clear that deliberation that allows deceit does not lead to democratic laws. One of the central questions of democratic political theory concerns the nature of the ideals governing the kind of deliberation that leads to genuinely democratic laws.

The difficulty of justifying a democratic state is that its citizens must live in a society and be governed by laws to which they must adhere while simultaneously preserving their liberty. In order to preserve the liberty of action of its citizens, the laws of a democratic country must be laws to which those citizens in *some sense* agree, via a process of joint deliberation. A central question, or perhaps *the* central question, of democratic political theory is what makes a joint deliberative process fair. The question is complicated by the fact that citizens of a democratic state are typically born into a state with already existing laws. Because of this, the deliberative process must take into account the fact that the laws will apply to people who did not have the opportunity to participate in their formation. The laws therefore must be crafted via normative ideals that take into account the views o those not yet born, as well as o children who, while not at that oint caable o deliberation, one day will be aong the eole whose viewoints need to be taken into account. What norative ideals should govern a deliberative rocess that results in laws o this sort, laws that can legitiately be taken as binding on individuals not yet born or too young to articiate in their oration? This is the question o the nature of the norms of public reason. Any corehensive list o the deocratic ideals centrally includes the norative ideals o ublic reason.

Public reason is so iortant to the nature o deocracy that on certain views o deocracy, its chie virtue is ost ully realized in deliberating about olicy with one's ellow citizens. This is not Plato's view o the nature o deocracy. In book 8 o The Republic, Plato describes liberty in ters o an unettered unleashing o actions guided by rando desires, otivated oen by aetite. For exale, Plato describes "the Deocratic an" (561c, d), who is suosed to ebody liberty, as living "his lie day by day, indulging each aetite as it akes itsel elt. One day he is drinking heavily and listening to the ute; on the next he is dieting and drinks only water." The act that deocracy is connected to this concetion o liberty as ully unettered action is at the basis o his critiques o the syste o deocracy.

Like Plato, Aristotle connects deocracy with soe concetion o liberty. But the concetion o liberty is very dierent. On Aristotle's view, one's true desires are the ones one arrives at via a rocess o deliberation with one's ellow citizens in the ublic square. On this concetion, it is in ollowing olicies utually agreed uon by a deliberating body o citizens that one is actually ollowing the ath o liberty. The classic odern discussion o these two distinct concetions o liberty is in Benjain Constant's aous lecture to Athénée Royal in Paris in 1819, "The Liberty o the Ancients Coared with That o the Moderns." The liberty o the oderns is what Plato is ocking as the chie virtue o deocracy. The liberty o the ancients is what Aristotle argues is the chie virtue o deocracy.10 It is because o their dierent concetions o de ocracy that Plato concluded that it was the least stable olitical syste and Aristotle concluded that it was the ost stable olitical syste.

Many conteorary and odern Aerican deocratic theorists give a secial role to olitical articiation, suggesting that they have a concetion o deocracy that reects the Aristotelian concetion. In the great ode to deocracy ro 1903, The Souls of Black Folk, the Aerican hilosoher W. E. B. Du Bois reeatedly ehasizes that the nation owes its Black citizens three things: "the ree right to vote, [the right] to enjoy civic rights, and [the right] to be educated."11 Du Bois deands that "[n]egroes ust insist continually, in season and out o season, that voting is necessary to odern anhood . . . and that black boys need education as well as white boys." Du Bois ocuses on these three rights— voting rights, civic equality, and education— because he thinks o olitical articiation as secial aong the liberties; education is iortant because only the educated citizen can articiate well in civic lie.12

I have ehasized throughout that there is no need or y uroses to choose between the dierent concetions o liberty that deocracy is suosed to ebody. Whatever the concetion o liberty underlying deocracy is to be, the nors o ublic reason have a secial role. Indeed, that role is so secial that on erhas the ost coelling vision o the nature o deocracy, the one chaioned by Du Bois, their role stands above all others.

Given that deocratic ideals centrally include the norative ideals o ublic reason, an iortant or o roaganda in a deocracy is seech that resents itsel as ebodying the norative ideals o ublic reason but that in act contributes content that can be exected by a rational erson in the situation to erode those very ideals. Proaganda in a deocracy in act oen takes this or: seech that inhibits, rather than urthers, the ideals o ublic reason. To gain ore clarity on the structure of propaganda in a liberal democracy, we must have a better sense of plausible normative ideals of public reason. Only then will we be able to assess various examples.

I will discuss three suggestions of standards for debate in public political forums. Any normative ideal of public reason should be impartial in the following sense: public political speech should not be of the sort that, in James Madison's words, "[divides] mankind into parties, [inflames] them with mutual animosity, and [renders] them much more disposed to vex and oppress each other than to co-operate for their common good." A norm of impartiality demands that the force of the reasons offered, and policies proposed, is not perspectivedependent. If someone is offering impartial reasons, their reasons "must be grounded in something that is independent of their stance, namely what is the case believer-neutrally."13 This is the standpoint of the impartial observer. According to the ideal of impartiality, the claims politicians make in political debate must be from the standpoint of the impartial observer. All three different views of the normative public reason are impartial in this general sense.

The first is that debates in public political forums are guided by a norm of *theoretical rationality*. The second is that they are guided by a normative ideal of *practical* rationality plus ignorance in a sense I will characterize. The third normative ideal for public reason is that it is guided by equal respect for the perspective of everyone subject to the policy under debate. Following the recent political philosophy tradition, we shall call this the norm of *reasonableness*.

The first plausible normative ideal of public reason is to hold contributions up to a standard of *theoretical rationality*, what Jürgen Habermas famously calls "the unforced force of the better argument." A contribution to a political debate must be justified, and be assessed solely by its impact on the truth of the issue at hand. Let's say that *rational contributions to a debate* are legitimately justified claims (ones "backed up by evidence") that contribute to a rational resolution of the debate.

A clai contributes to a rational resolution o a debate only i it bears signicantly on the likelihood o the issue under debate. For exale, a clai would be a rational contribution to a debate about whether or not invading Iraq is the right thing to do i the clai was justied and rovided evidence or or against the wisdo o invading Iraq.14

A clai ay be a rational contribution to a debate, but have a nonrational eect on the debate as a whole. Take an exale that was recently the subject o a New York Times Retro Reort, ro which the inoration to ollow coes.15 The exale involves the exression "suer- redator," introduced and oularized by acadeics— secically Jaes A. Fox at Northeastern University and John J. Di Iulio Jr., then o Princeton University— in the id- 1990s as a descrition o the er etrators o youth crie in the United States. In a television interview, Di Iulio dened a suer- redator as a "young juvenile criinal who is so iulsive, so reorseless, that he can kill, rae, ai, without giving it a second thought." In another television interview, Di Iulio said, "We are talking about a grou o kids that are growing u essentially atherless, godless, and jobless." In an article ro 1996, "My Black Crie Proble, and Yours," Di Iulio wrote, "[A]s any as hal o these suer- redators could be young, black ales."

The notorious "Central Park jogger" case in 1989, in which a grou o Black children in New York City were arrested and convicted or the brutal rae o a jogger in Central Park, was used by Dan Rather to introduce the toic in a CBS news secial on "suer- redator theory" (the children later turned out to be innocent). During the residential caaign in 1996, the Reublican residential candidate Bob Dole called or harsh new legislation or youth crie, saying (clearly o Black ale children) that "exerts call the suer-redators." It is clear that the deagogic language o "suer- redator" had an iediate, nonrational eect on the subsequent debate about the legislation o child crie. Over orty states swily enacted draconian new legislation cracking down on violent child crie. One can iagine, in this context, a olitician using the hrase "inner- city youth" in avor o such legislation. But the debate had been olluted by the introduction o the language o "suer- redator," which was inexorably linked to Black children. By being linked to the iconograhy evoked by the language o "suer- redator," even rational contributions to the debate had a clearly nonrational eect. In act, this was the very urose o introducing the ter "suer- redator." As Fox acknowledges, his use o the "strong language" was intentional, since he eant to "sound an alar about what ight haen i we didn't act quickly."

In the context o the debate in the 1990s about child crie, ters like "suer- redator" and "wilding" (introduced to describe the alleged actions o the youth accused in the Central Park jogger case) olluted subsequent debate by evoking negative stereotyes that ieded the subsequent eloyent o rational aculties. Even aarently ully rational contributions to the debate subsequently evoked such stereotyes. Such contributions thus ran counter to the norative ideal o theoretical rationality, because even seaking o Black youth crie evoked nonrational aculties, secically ear, to end rational debate.16

I theoretical rationality is the norative ideal o ublic reason, then one aradig class o cases o roaganda in a deocracy will be uses o language that are asked as objective but that have a oleical eect. They have the eect o a eals to assions to cut o rational debate, in any cases without aking a rational contribution to it. This unquestionably catures one kind o roaganda. Any account o the or o roaganda in a deocracy ust exlain these cases as well.

Theoretical rationality as a norative ideal o ublic reason also exlains the ubiquity o roaganda asked as ebodying scientic ideals but conveying a content that runs counter to the. The roaganda involved in cliate- science denial is tyically o this sort.

I theoretical reason is the nor o ublic reason, then reasons advanced in ublic olitical orus are legitiate insoar as they lay a role in a rational resolution o the issue. The issue under debate ight be, or exale, "Is it in the ublic interest to raise taxes on the wealthy?" Considerations or or against ust weigh in on the resolution o this question (allowing coroises as a kind o resolution) in the sense o raising or lowering the likelihood o the truth o one o the otions. So, or exale, an oonent o raising taxes on the rich ight rovide eirical evidence that so doing will lead to an increase in uneloyent.

According to the nor o theoretical reason, ublic olitical debate in a deocracy is guided by reasons that bear on whether or not a articular olicy is for the common good, or in the public interest. However, one ight worry that a olicy roosal ight be in the ublic interest, yet run roughshod over the allowable ersonal liberties o a inority. This concern otivates a distinct norative ideal governing olitical debate. To introduce it, I will eloy the thought exerient o the original osition, ro John Rawls's book A Theory of Justice, ublished in 1971.17

Practical rationality is "eans- ends" reasoning: given a goal, what is the ost rational way to achieve that goal, given one's belies? A nor o ractical rationality on ublic reason is clearly not a version o iartialis. However, one can odel the deands o iartiality by arrying ractical rationality with the susension o one's belies about one's own position in society, and hence one's articular ersective. One sees this odel in the thought exerient involving "the original osition" in A Theory of Justice. There, Rawls argues that the correct way to establish the rinciles o justice or a society is by iagining onesel into an "original osition," where one does not know one's lace in society, one's race or religion, or even one's intellectual and hysical caacities. The laws governing the society will be the laws agreed uon by agents who are ully ractically rational and have iagined theselves into "the original osition." That is, in adjudicating the rinciles o justice, one ust susend belie about one's location in society, where society is conceived o as huanity, and eloy one's ractical rationality to decide what rinciles should govern a society in which one does not know where one is located. This is another ossible view o the nors o ublic reason: a stateent in a olitical oru is only accetable i it is ractically rational ro the stance o soeone ignorant o their lace in the society. This is a version o iartialis; let us call it practical rationality impartialism.

Practical rationality iartialis ay aear to rule out exressions o sel- interest in ublic olitical orus. But it does not. Exressions o sel- interest ight even be evidentially necessary as a eans o inoration that bears on reasons that are iartial.18 A legitiate ste in deocratic deliberation on this icture is a reason that akes sense ro the ersective o the iartial observer, who does not know her lace in society. But this does not reclude exressions o sel interest, as they ay contribute evidentially to the weight o one legitiate reason over another. For exale, suose we are debating about whether to build a bridge connecting an island to the ainland. The sel- interest o those who live in the construction zone is relevant to the question o whether we ought to build the bridge. We need exressions o sel- interest to rovide that inoration, even though they theselves are not ublic reasons.

Practical rationality iartialis does not reclude exressions o sel- interest in ublic debate. But such exressions are only relevant insoar as they bear on reasons that are coelling to all. The iartialist concetion o ublic reason orces the eliination o any clai that has its source in sel- interest that does not contribute to iartial reasons. And that is the right result, because clais that have their source in sel interest and are not useul ro an iartial standoint are aradigatically the kinds o clais ade in illegitiate attets to gain ower or a articular interest grou.

For exale, a senator wishing to do a avor or an oil co any or a rivate rison coany or the sake o a caaign donation ight deliver a seech in avor o a iece o legislation that avors the source o the otential caaign contributor. Fro the ersective o eole not beneting ro those caaign donations, the reasons the senator gives that coe ro his sel- interest run counter to the iartialist nor o ublic reason. And that is the correct result.

Inoration about the sel- interest o various arties is oen indirectly relevant to debates about olicy. So exressions o sel- interest can be legitiate in olitical discussion, even i iartiality is the nor o ublic reason. But it is also the case that there are clais that do not bear on settling the debate, or do not rovide relevant evidence in any way, but can still, according to the advocate o iartialis about ublic reason, be legitiate. These are clais that aid in satisying the preconditions or ublic reason.

Practical rationality iartialis involves reasons that would ake sense ro the osition o anyone in the society, i they did not know their lace in the society. But it is not clear that one can argue, via iartial reasons, in such a way that would lead one to a ore exansive concetion o the inclusion conditions or the society. This kind o iartialist can allow clais that hel acilitate the adotion o such a background. That is, the iartialist can allow or seech that hels to establish the background required to enable deocratic deliberation. It ay be that considerations that are not iartial are art o that background (such as reasonableness as a character trait, in the sense soon to be dened). So this kind o iartialist can grant the legitiacy o contributions that are not iartial, as long as they hel oster the preconditions or the exchange o reasons that are iartial in this sense. The ractical rationality iartialist is not so easily reuted.

Desite the aeal o ractical rationality iartialis, it is not the ost central ideal o ublic reason in deocratic olitical theory, either historically or currently. Deocratic olitical theory has long avored another ideal, according to which a deocratic counity is one that osters certain kinds o attitudes toward one's fellow citizens. In *The Souls of Black Folk*, Du Bois brings our attention to this normative ideal of public reason by describing the consequences of its complete failure in his description of the political state in the American South after the Civil War:

Can we establish a mass of black laborers and artisans and landholders in the South who, by law and public opinion, have absolutely no voice in shaping the laws under which they live and work? Can the modern organization of industry, assuming as it does free democratic government and the power and ability of the laboring classes to compel respect for their welfare,—can this system be carried out in the South when half its laboring force is voiceless in the public councils and powerless in its own defence? ... It is pitiable that frantic efforts must be made at critical times to get law-makers in some States even to listen to the respectful presentation of the black man's side of a current controversy. . . . The laws are made by men who have little interest in [the Negro]; they are executed by men who have absolutely no motive for treating the black people with courtesy or consideration; and, finally, the accused law-breaker is tried, not by his peers, but too often by men who would rather punish ten innocent Negroes than let one guilty one escape.19

Du Bois's critique of the political system of the South during the several decades following the Civil War is that its laws are not democratically legitimate; they apply to some citizens, the Black ones, not as "laws and justice," but as "sources of humiliation and oppression." One obvious reason the laws lack democratically legitimacy is because Blacks were not allowed to participate in their formation. As Martin Luther King Jr. writes in "Letter from a Birmingham Jail," "A law is unjust if it is inflicted on a minority that, as a result of being denied the right to vote, had no part in enacting or devising the law." But Du Bois is not merely making King's point. He is rather bringing our attention to what he sees to regard as a ore central reason the laws are not deocratically legitiate. It is because those who created the laws did not have empathy or soe o those subject to the, naely, their Black ellow citizens. The lack o eathy eant that the laws were craed in such a way that did not reect respect or the viewoints o Black citizens; lawakers will not listen to the "resectul resentation o the black an's side o a current controversy."

Du Bois is arguing that the laws in the South are illegiti ate, and they are illegitiate because (a) Black citizens do not articiate in their oration, (b) lawakers will not take into account the reasonable ersectives o Black citizens, and (c) there is no eathy on the side o the lawakers or the situation o Blacks subject to those laws. Du Bois is suggesting that underlying the kind o equal resect involved in taking into account reasonable ersectives o Black citizens is eathy.

A benevolently aternalistic society is one in which the olicyakers have eathy with those who are subject to the olicies, but do not treat the with equal resect. The dierence between benevolent aternalis and a deocratic culture is indicated by Du Bois's coents about the need in a ree deocratic society to take into account the "resectul resentation" o the ersective o alternative views.

Chater 4 o the British hilosoher Susan Stebbing's book Thinking to Some Purpose, ublished in 1939, is called "You and I: I and You." Stebbing calls attention to a ailure coon in ublic discourse, the "ailure to see the oint ro the other an's osition."20 She recoends, in aking assertions that aly to everyone, the "saeguard" o changing "you" into "I." That way, one can ore easily see that the olicy one is rescribing to others is a reasonable one. Stebbing is thereby suggesting that there is a nor o equality to ublic discourse. It is this that distinguishes ere aternalis ro deocracy.

The sociologist Manred Stanley articulates the need in a deocratic society or this kind o caacity to take the ersective o the other:

When a society evolves into a condition that is so complex and fragmented by social class and occupational specialization that great sociopsychic distance between population groups becomes a normal state of affairs, then insufficient compassion emerges as a distinct collective problem. . . . Mutual estrangement and stereotypical fantasy exist between the extremes of our class structure, between several ethnic and racial groups, and between considerable numbers of males and females. . . . The challenge is . . . one of bringing people to the point of understanding the objective historical and existing conditions of groups with whom they have had no personal life experience. Compassion presupposes the ability to "take the role of the other" in some particularly subtle and informed way.

But what is it to "take the role of the other" in the relevant respect? Stephen Darwall appeals to Adam Smith's notion of an "impartial spectator" to explain the notion of "being someone in the other's situation" and deliberating about what to feel from that perspective. Stebbing is asking us, when proposing policy, to imagine being someone subject to that policy, with as many of the properties as those subject to the policies have, without losing the impartial stance suggested by the indefinite "someone." It is this ability, to imagine being someone in the situation of another, that underlies the capacity to give the perspectives of our fellow citizens equal weight. The difference between benevolent paternalism and a democratic attitude is that the latter presupposes the regular employment of this sort of imaginative capacity.22

I have sketched a democratic ideal that involves a certain cognitive capacity, that of imagining oneself as *someone* in the situation of another. Following Darwall, let's call this capacity *cognitive empathy*. It is not completely clear how to characterize cognitive empathy, since it is not completely clear what it is to imagine oneself as *someone* in the situation of another. The philosopher Laurie Paul has argued recently that it is not ossible to iaginatively lace onesel in the situation o others who have had draatically dierent lie exeriences. It ay be, i Paul is correct, that even cognitive eathy is excessively idealized as an aective base or a deocratic ideal.

Paul argues that a certain class o exerience akes it i ossible to iagine being in that osition.23 One exale Paul gives is the exerience o having a child. She argues that having a child is a transformative experience, and that this entails that it is not ossible to ake a rational decision about choosing to be in that osition. More needs to be said about Sith's iartial sectator beore one can conclude that Paul's arguents show that a childless erson cannot be an iartial sectator o a erson with a child. But let's suose, as it aears, that it is incoatible with Paul's arguents, that childless ersons cannot iagine, in the relevant sense, what it is like to have a child. I so, then, on the concetion o a deocratic culture I have sketched, it is hard to see how any olicies could be legitiate. Childless ersons could not ake olicy that alies to those with children, in a deocratically accetable way. This is a not unailiar antiliberal osition.

There are several otions to the diculties osed by Paul's arguents. One is to seek a nor o ublic reason that does not require cognitive eathy with the situation o others. For exale, Sharon Krause has a suggestive discussion o ersective taking as "an exercise in oral sentient," which involves taking into account the sentients o those in dierent situations who would be aected by the olicy.24 Krause is suggesting that in order to gain an areciation o the act that others would be negatively aected by a olicy I suort, I do not need to be able to occuy their ersective, even in an iartial anner. One strategy in the ace o the diculties o ersective taking raised by Paul's work is thereore to seek a nor o ublic reason that catures a sense o iartiality, without requiring such a strong cognitive caacity as ersective sharing.

A dierent strategy is to take cognitive eathy as an aective ideal, which ay unction to regulate utual olicyaking, without ever being actually ebodied, or caable o being e bodied, in ractice. According to this strategy, the nors o ublic reason are ideals. But ideals can eror a regulative unction while still being realistic. As we shall see at the end o chater 4, this strategy requires soe account o what it is or an ideal to regulate a ractice.

When a olitical ideal is unrealistic in ractice, one can either seek a weaker, ore realistic ideal. Alternatively, one could exlore the thought that an unrealizable ideal can nevertheless have regulative orce. But or y uroses, these debates are not relevant; this is not a roject in ideal theory. My goal is rather to use utative ideals to identiy cases o roaganda. Proaganda exloits all otential nors o ublic debate, whether they are realizable or not.

A deocratic culture is one in which citizens assue that their ellow citizens have good reasons or acting as they do. It involves, or exale, questioning one's own ersective, i one cannot ake rational sense out o the actions o one's ellow citizens. It involves, as Du Bois argues, being oen to the "resectul resentation" o other ersectives.

Rawls useully characterizes this concetion o the ideals governing deocratic deliberation in a characterization o what he calls "reasonableness":25

Persons are reasonable in one basic asect when, aong equals say, they are ready to roose rinciles and standards as air ters o cooeration and to abide by the willingly, given the assurance that others will likewise do so. Those nors they view as reasonable or everyone to accet and thereore as justiable to the; and they are ready to discuss the air ters that others roose.26

Reasonableness requires any contribution to olitical discussion, or exale, in the or o a roosed olicy, to be "justiable" to all o those under whose urview it alls. Regarding a reason one gives as justiable to another resuoses "taking the role o the other" in soe relevant sense.

Let us set aside theoretical rationality and focus instead on the contrast between reasonableness and practical rationality, the kind of rationality exemplified in selecting the most effective means to further various ends (that are not truth or goodness). In contrast, a reasonable contribution is one that "we also reasonably think that other citizens might also reasonably accept." An example from a *New York Times* column by the philosopher Amia Srinivasan provides a nice illustration of the distinction between practical rationality and reasonableness:

Suppose that I inherited from my rich parents a large plot of vacant land, and that you are my poor, landless neighbor. I offer you the following deal. You can work the land, doing all the hard labor of tilling, sowing, irrigating and harvesting. I'll pay you \$1 a day for a year. After that, I'll sell the crop for \$50,000. You decide this is your best available option, and so take the deal.28

If I think of our bargain in terms of rational self-interest, then it is rational to offer you \$1 a day, knowing that you have no other prospects. But I am clearly not being *reasonable*. I am not imagining someone in your situation, and then asking what would seem fair from that perspective. Rawls argues in *Political Liberalism* that the central norm of public speech is one that demands that contributions to public debate are *reasonable*.29

The importance of reasonableness as a norm for public discourse, and empathy as its foundation, is clear from the advice of successful propagandists. The state of Israel is in a constant asymmetrical battle with Palestinians, over whom they have the military upper hand. The constant stream of photos of dead Palestinian civilians poses a severe image problem for the country. The photos of the results of massively asymmetrical battle make Israel seem like it is an unreasonable partner in the peace process.

The Israel Project is an organization that promotes Israel's image abroad, particularly in the United States. During the war with Gaza in 2009, they commissioned the American roagandist Frank Luntz to create a ahlet to aid in iage reair. The 2009 Global Language Dictionary is an aid to Israel's ublic relations during a highly asyetrical war with an eney that involves the iniction o large civilian casualties. The docuent is titled "The Israel Project's 2009 Global Language Dictionary," and though intended to be secret, it was leaked to the ublic. Chater 1 is "The 25 Rules or Eective Counication." The very rst rule, indeed the beginning o the entire docuent, is "[]ersuadables won't care how uch you know until they know how uch you care. Show Eathy or sides!"

Previously, in Aril 2003, The Luntz Research Coanies, in collaboration with The Israel Project, created a siilar docuent, titled "Wexner Analysis: Israeli Counication Priorities 2003." The reort is an eort to nd a way to counicate the essage to Aerican "oinion elites" that Israel is genuinely interested in eace and Palestinian well- being, while si ultaneously underining suort or the Palestinian leadershi. In olling, Luntz discovered that the sound bite "[w]e are hoing to nd a Palestinian leadershi that really does reect the best interest or the Palestinian eole" was an eective way to counicate the essage that Israel is a reasonable negotiating artner, but the Palestinian leadershi is not.

A good deal o the docuent ro 2003 is sent trying to nd a way to counicate reasonableness, while suggesting, without asserting, that the then unknown leader Mahoud Abbas is untrustworthy. Luntz urges avoiding directly attacking Abbas, while siultaneously trying to counicate his untrustworthiness. He suggests nding a way to counicate that Abbas "was aointed to his current osition by Araat, which is susect," and that he "has denied the Holocaust." Luntz's advice or Israel reects the understanding that reasonableness in negotiations, undergirded by eathy or one's negotiating artners, is the exected nor o ublic discourse. His docuents attet to exlain how to eign reasonableness while counicating a essage that underines the reasonableness o one's interlocutors in the eyes o third arties.

Rawls has a novel arguent or reasonableness, what he calls "the act o reasonable luralis." A deocratic society is one that allows diverse reasonable perspectives to be ursued by its citizens. The way to live an autonoous lie is to "carve your way through the world," having your ath governed by your decisions. I the decisions are autonoous, one's ath will be one that is sel- ored. The goal o a deocracy should be to allow axiu reedo to develo along an individual ath consistently with being reasonable toward one's ellow citizens. Thus, there ust eerge, in a deocracy, ultile reasonable ull oral concetions. You ay decide to becoe Christian; I ay decide to becoe a Scientologist; and a third riend ay nally settle on atheis. These are all reasonable albeit incoatible aths. By iosing an ideal o reasonableness, Rawls is requiring reasons to not be drawn ro the di ering doctrines ellow citizens hold as a consequence o the decisions they ade that ored a legitiate lie ath. On this view, it is not erissible or you to draw on your Christian belies in ublic debate, because, i you are reasonable, you are aware that Christian doctrine is not reasonable ro y ersective.

In contrast, a norative ideal o theoretical reason or ublic deliberation allows any reason to be oered that otentially bears on settling the issue at hand. I one believes in the doctrines o Christianity, and their truth would settle the issue at hand, one could advance those doctrines in ublic debate and take onesel to be in accord with the norative ideal o theoretical reason.

I the central norative ideal governing ublic debate is theoretical reason, then one kind o aradig case o roaganda in a deocracy is an aarently rational contribution to a debate that akes it subsequently ore dicult to ollow the dictates o theoretical reason (and this consequence is not ade u or by its ositive contribution to the settling o the debate). I the central ideal governing ublic debate is reasonableness, a very dierent icture o the analogous aradig case o roaganda eerges. I reasonableness is the central nor governing ublic debate, aradig cases o roaganda will be ones that are resented as reasonable, but that subsequently make it more dicult for the participants in the debate to be reasonable. That is, aradig cases o roaganda will be ones that reresent it to be reasonable not to take certain ersectives into account.

To understand how a clai resented as reasonable could erode reasonableness, we need to look ore at the notion o reasonableness and its eotional basis in huans. What is it to be reasonable? To be reasonable is to take one's roosals to be accountable to everyone in the counity. A reasonable erson only acts in ways that would be accetable ro every ersective; the reasonable erson takes hersel to be accountable to all her ellow citizens. Stehen Darwall argues that the eotional basis o accountability is guilt. Guilt is the eotion that we eel when we ail to live u to the deands o reasonableness.30 But guilt is not the eotion that leads us to consider the ersectives o others. A counity is reasonable i it is governed by nors o utual resect and utual accountability. A counity governed by the norative ideal o reasonableness is one in which citizens have utual resect or everyone else in the counity and take their actions to be accountable to everyone else in the counity. Possessing such an attitude requires, at least aong huans, empathy or the capacity to put oneself in another's shoes. 31 One can thereore exect a characteristic or o roaganda in a liberal deocracy to be a clai that is resented as reasonable, but that has the eect o eroding eathy or a targeted grou.

Suose that reasonableness is the norative ideal governing ublic reason. Those who contribute olicy roosals to ublic reason that aly to everyone, or arguents or such olicy roosals, ust hold theselves accountable to every one who ay be subject to the. It is not reasonable to roose a olicy that, ro the ersective o another, is unreasonable. The norative ideal o reasonableness is the de and "to live olitically with others in the light o reasons all ight reasonably be exected to endorse."32

The norative ideal o reasonableness also exlains why it is legitiate to take laws ored in a deliberation governed by reasonableness to be binding on those who did not articiate in their oration. The deand o reasonableness requires those deliberating about olicy to take into account the ersective o anyone who ay be subject to those laws, including, or exale, very young children. By taking into account the ersective o those not caable o articiating in deliberation about olicy, one ensures that the olicies thereby ored are sensitive to their interests.

Adoting a second- ersonal attitude resuoses being accountable to others, and taking the in turn to be accountable to you. To take Darwall's avored exale, when I ask you to ste o y oot, I a adoting a second- ersonal stand oint to you, execting that you will treat y request by treating e with dignity, which eans considering y ersective. Second- ersonal attitudes thus centrally involve the notions o dignity and respect. I the adotion o second- ersonal attitudes is a recondition governing ublic deliberation in ublic olitical orus, then seech that is an aront to the dignity o other ebers o society runs counter to these ideals, and hence is the kind o seech that one exects to nd asked aradigatically by roaganda.

It is uncontroversial that roaganda, in the broad sense I have characterized, is bad. There is however a tradition in olitical hilosohy dating back to Aristotle that advertises itsel as defending rhetoric. 33 It is iortant to distinguish the ais o this tradition ro an enterrise that would license roaganda, in the senses I have dened. In the rest o the chater, I will deonstrate, just with the oints I have develoed u to this oint, that there is a kind o roaganda that is olitically necessary to use to overcoe undaental obstacles to the realization o deocratic ideals. This kind o roaganda stands in a secic structural relation to deagoguery in liberal deocracy.

We have seen Du Bois develo the oint that overcoing barriers to deocracy requires soething like rhetoric or ro aganda. W. E. B. Du Bois is lausibly taken to be aealing to the need or underining roaganda, as works that directly address the distorted concetion o Black ellow citizens will not sell. But it is also ossible to take Du Bois as calling or articularly eective suorting roaganda that reveals Black hu anity. This is a art o a classic debate he had during the Harle Renaissance in the 1920s with the hilosoher Alain Locke.

In "Criteria o Negro Art," Du Bois writes, "[I]t is not the ositive roaganda o eole who believe white blood divine, inallible and holy to which I object. It is the denial o a siilar right o roaganda to those who believe Black blood huan, lovable and insired with new ideals or the world." Later in the essay, Du Bois exands on the desired eect o art used as roaganda: "[U]ntil the art o the black olk co ells recognition they will not be rated as huan. And when through art they coell recognition then let the world discover i it will that their art is as new as it is old and as old as new." Here, Du Bois uses "roaganda" to denote aeals to eotion, o the sort evoked by art, in the service o the essage that Blacks deserve equal resect as huans and citizens.

In his resonse to Du Bois in 1928, the hilosoher Alain Locke rejects this aarent call or suorting roaganda, while siultaneously roviding a useul characterization o this nonejorative sense o the ter:

My chie objection to roaganda, aart ro its besetting sin o onotony and disroortion, is that it eretuates the osition o grou ineriority even in crying out against it. For it leaves and seaks under the shadow o a doinant ajority who it harangues, cajoles, threatens or sulicates.

By "roaganda," both Du Bois and Locke ean a kind o seech that uses "haranguing, cajoling, threatening, or sulicating" as a ethod to orce a doinant ajority to exand the doain o resect and eathy to include a ersecuted and ignored inority. In this sense, "roaganda" reers to a ethod o aealing to eotions to increase reasonableness. Locke's criticis o Du Bois's call or Black artists to engage in roaganda in this sense is that it laces an undue burden on Black artists to advocate or theselves with the doinant white oulation, which is yet another burden stiing their reedo o exression.

Using the exale o Du Bois, the olitical hilosoher Melvin Rogers deends certain uses o rhetoric by aealing to its eect on the "cognitive- aective diension o judgent."34 Rogers does not here aeal to what I have characterized as underining roaganda; in general, Rogers seaks less about the echaniss. Rogers instead intends to illuinate the connection between the ositive rhetoric tradition and a deliberative deocratic ideal like reasonableness.35 Rogers argues that Du Bois used rhetoric, o whatever kind, to orce his audience to be accountable to Black citizens. It induced its audience to recognize their oral obligation to grant equal olitical articiation to a grou that had been invisible. The unction o the discourse is thereore not to contribute to the rational resolution o a debate, in the sense o deciding the truth or alsity o the clai at issue. Its unction is instead to orce a reiagination o the resued boundaries o that concet.36

As we have seen, reasonableness requires a raework o "relations o utual resect and utual accountability" between all citizens.37 Du Bois clearly regards roaganda to be a ethod o increasing these "relations o utual resect and utual accountability" between all citizens, regardless o color. Public debate in Du Bois's tie obviously ell well short o the ideals o reasonableness, since it did not include Aerica's Black oulation within the raework o utual resect and utual accountability. Du Bois calls or Black artists to use their art to increase the reasonableness o ublic discourse, by orcing the recognition that the raework o utual resect was too narrowly drawn.

Du Bois deends the necessity o rhetoric that improves the ideals o ublic reason. Du Bois is certainly not in the business o giving a deense o rhetoric based on its otivational owers to circuvent rational debate. Instead, he is, as Melvin Rogers rightly oints out, deending the "asirational" owers o rhetoric. A contribution to a debate can irove the subsequent reasonableness o the debate, even though the contribution itsel is not a rational contribution, in the sense that its inorational content contributes to the debate's resolution.

Assuing reasonableness as an ideal governing ublic seech, asirational seech in Rogers's sense is, structurally, recisely the oosite o deagoguery. The erson aking a roosal in the ublic olitical shere is reasonable i she can take hersel to be accountable to everyone who is subject to that roosal. In ost actual societies that regard theselves as liberal deocracies, the laws are not reasonable ro the ersective o certain grous. As Du Bois oints out, the laws in the ost- Reconstruction South were not reasonable, because they alied unreasonably to the Black citizens. An asirational contribution is one whose eect is to yield an overall improvement o the reasonableness o a debate. A characteristic exale o iroving the reasonableness o a debate is to a eal to eathy and understanding to lead eole to include the ersectives o soe citizens whose ersectives had reviously been ignored.38 Here are soe exales o civic rhetoric ro Aerican history; they will hel us gain an understanding o the echaniss by which civic rhetoric can be eective.

The Black Aerican intellectual Fannie Barrier Willias ublished, in Noveber 1904, an essay in The Voice of the Negro that is a classic exale o asirational seech, in Rogers's sense. It is a call or attention to the ersectives o Black woen that elicits eathy or the situation in which they nd theselves, the situation o having an unrecognized ersective. In a justly aous assage, she writes, o "the Aerican Negro woan,"

She is the only woan in Aerica who is alost unknown; the only woan or who nothing is done; the only woan without sucient deenders when assailed; the only woan who is still outside o that world o chivalry that in all the ages has aotheosized woen kind. Wars have been declared and ought or woen; governents have been established and develoed in the nae o woan; art, literature and song have all consired to ake woan little less than angels, but they have all been white woen.39

Fannie Barrier Willias used such assages to ake the reader aware o the consequences to dignity and sel- iage o invisibleness.

Another exale coes ro the ore recent ast. Dr. Martin Luther King organized the Sela to Montgoery March o 1965 during the ght or voting rights in the Aerican South. He insisted on nonviolence, knowing ull well that the archers would be et with extree violence. Television viewers across the country saw nonviolent archers who were asking only or olitical equality beaten and brutalized. It led to the increased visibility o Aerican Blacks by eliciting e athy or their situation. The Sela to Montgoery March is a aradig case o deocratically accetable roaganda: aniulation o the edia to draw attention and eathy to the redicaent o an otherwise invisible grou.

What about the Kantian criticis o roaganda, discussed in the revious chater? Is the or o roaganda that Du Bois urges still robleatic on Kantian grounds? Is it a kind o aniulation o the rational will? Let's return to the exa le ro chater 2, John Coltrane's version o "My Favorite Things," as analyzed by Ingrid Monson. According to Monson, Coltrane lures the white listener into the song by aealing to white aesthetic ideals. Once the listener has been teted into attention, Coltrane uses the song to reveal the reviously invisible Black ersective. In soe sense, this is isleading. But is it aniulation o the sort that would concern the Kantian?

In the case o Coltrane's version o "My Favorite Things" and the Sela to Montgoery March, soe kind o aniulation is involved. In the rst case, the listener exects to hear an exale o a beloved tune that ebodies certain aesthetic ideals, but instead discovers soething else. In the second case, King aniulated white Southerners into revealing their hatred on national edia, thereby turning the oinion o the country against the. But it is hard to see how deception is involved in these cases. No lying, or exale, was involved in either case.

In the Critique of Practical Reason, Kant takes the sense o reedo attaching to the rational will to involve reedo via recognition o the oral law. There are, o course, any ways to understand Kant's view here. But it sees that roaganda used to awaken others to their oral resonsibilities is in act addressing their rational will, even i it does not aeal to their rationality, in soe narrow sense o that notion. Such exales o roaganda are direct aeals to the ractical reedo o one's ellow citizens.40 The Kantian objection to ro aganda would arise i one aniulated the rational will in the or o a lie; and so too, as I have argued with the case o Bernanke's use o "scal cli," would the deocratic objection to roaganda. Misleading eole by exressing or ilicating a lie has negative raications that lead well beyond the case at hand.

Du Bois and Alain Locke describe a secies o roaganda that is seech that uses "haranguing, cajoling, threatening, or sulicating" as a ethod to orce a doinant ajority to ex and the doain o resect and eathy to include a ersecuted and ignored inority. There is a structural reason why this secies o roaganda is a necessity in treating ailures o liberal deocracy. There are any ties in which the ersectives o a grou are invisible ro the rest o the citizens. This is so, or exale, when there is excessive and irrational ear o that grou, or excessive and irrational coitent to their ineriority. As we have seen, a result is that lawakers do not hold theselves accountable to that grou when roosing and assing laws. In such a situation, there is no obvious deliberative way to ake that grou visible, no ethod that aeals to reason to bring ebers o that grou into equal olitical standing.

There is a structural roble in certain ierectly realized liberal deocracies that necessitates civic rhetoric. There is no obvious way that ebers o the grou whose ersectives are invisible could use reasonable clais in ublic olitical discourse to coel their ellow citizens into recognizing their ersectives. Aer all, the sae echaniss that ake a grou's ersective invisible also silence their voices. Nor is it straightorward to see how the invisible ersectives o such a grou could come to be visible ro discussion aong the citizens not in that grou. 41 I the ersectives o a grou are invisible to everyone else, their interests are not weighted in the oring o laws (this is why, or exale, the ethical and olitical crisis osed by the rison situation in the United States sees articularly dicult to resolve). I the ebers o the excluded grou are without roerty, they will reain so; i they are without olitical ower, they will reain so as well. To recognize the invisible, deocratic deliberation ust oen be circuvented by aeals to eathy o the sort Du Bois urges in his essay ro 1926, which or that reason ust be regarded as one o the great essays in deocratic olitical hilosohy.

Thus ar, I have argued that there is a structural reason why civic rhetoric is required in certain situations. These situations are ones in which societies that take theselves to be guided by the liberal deocratic ideals o autonoy, equality, and reason restrict the alication o these ideals to one doinant subgrou o citizens (or exale, white en). I have argued, ollowing Melvin Rogers and others, that there is no obvious way of using reason, or reasonable claims, to get members of the dominant subgroup to extend the application of liberal ideals to citizens who are not included (such as, in the history of the American polity, Black citizens). What is required is extending the domain of cognitive empathy to include those citizens, and there is no obviously cogent *argument*, from the perspective of just those citizens who are included in the sphere of liberal democratic ideals, to do so. But I have not addressed the *method* by which the domain of empathy can be expanded. Here too, following Rogers, I think close attention to the particular rhetorical tropes employed by Du Bois is revealing, as in the following passage from *The Souls of Black Folk*:

[F]ew men ever worshipped Freedom with half such unquestioning faith as did the American Negro for two centuries. To him, so far as he thought and dreamed, slavery was indeed the sum of all villainies, the cause of all sorrow, the root of all prejudice; Emancipation was the key to a promised land of sweeter beauty than ever stretched before the eyes of wearied Israelites. . . . The Nation has not yet found peace from its sins; the freedman has not yet found in freedom his promised land."42

Du Bois is here clearly employing civic rhetoric directed at a white audience. The question before us is how the rhetoric is supposed to work. What is the *mechanism* by which a passage like this elicits empathy, and leads to the broadening of the sphere of application of democratic ideals?

I think it is best to understand Du Bois here as employing the liberal democratic ideals themselves, against a certain understanding of their application. His goal is to undermine a conception of liberal democracy that only extends freedom to whites. His method is to appeal to freedom itself, that liberal democratic ideal that is so cherished even among a nation in which it is restricted to whites. His rhetoric undermines an understanding of those ideals thus restricted, by calling attention to the fact that those ideals are deeply cherished aong nonwhites as well. The goal here is to elicit eathy, by drawing attention to the act that cherished ideals aong the whites are also cherished aong Blacks. He is eliciting eathy by eloying the robleatically restricted ideals, and calling uon whites who cherish the to eathize with the light o those who also cherish the, but to who they have been consistently denied. The echanis he is using is thereore a certain kind o underining roaganda, one that targets the ideal freedom just for whites. His arguent aeals to reedo, which is understand in the doinant grou as freedom just for whites. He seeks to ersuade the that i one values reedo, one ust extend it to those who also value reedo just as uch. Freedom just for whites is thereore incoherent as an ideal; reedo is an ideal or whites because it is cherished as the highest value. But then it is an ideal or Blacks as well, and ust be extended to the. In this way, the liberal concets can be used against restricted understandings o their roer alication.

We have now seen that civic rhetoric is necessary to overcoe certain situations that ace societies striving to ollow liberal deocratic ideals, as well as soe exales o civic rhetoric. But there is always a cost to byassing deliberative ideals in discourse in such societies. Are the robles that arise ro the invisibility o a grou ro olitical discourse worth bearing that cost? I will use the exale o the situation o ederal and state risoners in the United States to illustrate the dee ethical and olitical robles raised or deocratic societies by the existence o grous whose ersective has been ade invisible.

Unlike in the ajority o deocracies, in the United States, risoners cannot vote; they are barred ro olitical articiation. The United States is unique aong Western deocracies in barring soe risoners ro voting even aer they have been released.43 As a consequence, there is no one recognized by the olity who can seak ro the ersective o risoners. Prisoners have becoe dehuanized as a consequence. They now serve as a strategic instruent in olitics. A olitician suons u crie to elicit ear, and then oers hisel as the instruent to satisy the desire or retribution (though the desire is or retribution o the ear caused by that very olitician).44 The disaearance ro ublic olitical lie o the ersective o the risoner has resulted, in the view o any advocates, such as Chuck Colson, in an ethical crisis or the United States. Recent decades have borne witness to ever ore draconian rison torture ractices, including the extensive use o solitary conneent and inhuane rison- sentencing ractices.

What is less oen rearked uon is that the disaearance o the risoners' ersective ro ublic olitical discourse has resulted in another kind o crisis, a political crisis. The dehuanizing o risoners has underined our deocracy. One exale is the widesread ractice in the United States o prison gerrymandering. In the any states that ractice it, risoners conned there also count as residents o the area where the risons are.
