In the state o Pennsylvania alone, there are eight state legislative districts that have too ew nonincarcerated residents to be state legislative districts without counting the nonvoting (and ostly urban) risoners in their risons. Prisons thus give the rural voters in the areas in which risons are located vastly enhanced olitical ower and oney ro the state. This, in turn, gives such voters extra incentive to roote brutal rison- sentencing ractices to kee the risoners incarcerated and bring ore to their districts.

Prison labor also rovides a salient exale o the olitical crisis osed by dehuanizing risoners. The Thirteenth Aendent, which banned orced unaid labor, allows an excetion in the case o rison labor. This has oered a large oortunity or states to relace good, high- aying ublic service jobs with oen ree rison labor. The governor o the state o Wisconsin in the United States, Scott Walker, in 2011 ushed through a law eliinating collective bargaining by ublic sector unions in Wisconsin, eectively destroying the unions in a state known or its history o union organizing. The law eliinated the ability o unions to label certain jobs as "union only" jobs. One eect was to allow high- aying union jobs to be re laced by rivately contracted rison labor. This eectively incentivizes the state to seek ore risoners or chea labor.

More generally, the widesread ractice o dehuanizing those caught violating laws leads to a situation in which risoners becoe a awn in the hands o Machiavellian oliticians who use the ear o crie to reresent theselves as the eole's rotector.45 Plato traces the weak oint o deocracy to the eole's "roensity to elevate and gloriy one an as the eole's rotector and chaion" (565c). "[T]he tyrant's oint o entry into the society" is his sel- reresentation as "the eole's rotector" (565d). To win elections, oliticians in the United States sel- reresent as being "the eole's rotector," by irrationally creating ear around crie and oering the selves as the ones who will deliver retribution. This erodes genuine deocratic deliberation and acilitates the actions o deagogues. The disaearance o the risoners' ersective ro ublic debate in the United States is thus both a oral crisis and a olitical crisis.

Deocratic countries do not have an ocial inistry o roaganda. Nondeocratic countries, such as Cuba, do. This distinction needs to be exlained. We have seen that civic rhetoric is a legitiate, and even necessary, kind o roaganda in countries guided by incoletely realized deocratic ideals. But this does not ean that a deocratic country could have a inistry o civic rhetoric. The central urose o Du Bois's and Locke's notion o roaganda is to ake those contributing to ublic reason accountable to the ersectives o those o an oressed grou. In eect, it is to ake an allegedly deocratic state into a genuinely deocratic state: to realize deocracy. To have an ocial inistry o roaganda is to adit that the state is not deocratic. It is to adit that there is still work to be done to incororate soe o those subject to the laws into the state as citizens. A state that considers itsel already deocratic could never have a inistry as an ocial state entity that delivers even roaganda o the sort called or by Du Bois. To do so would be to adit that the state has allen systeatically short o the iniu requireents or a deocracy (which requires, inially, awareness by the state that it has allen short). The other unction o inistries o roaganda is to byass deocratic deliberation to elicit su ort or a olicy. Even i this is needed or a state eergency, or or urthering worthwhile secondary olitical goals, it is by its nature, as we have seen, nondeocratic. It is or these reasons that no deocratic state can have a inistry o roaganda.

We can now also recisely identiy the structural relation between roaganda that is in soe sense useul to deocracy and deagoguery. The account o roaganda I rovide exlains recisely why soe kinds o roaganda are erissible, and erhas even necessary, in societies that are guided by liberal deocratic ideals, while others are not. The notion o roaganda at issue in the Du Bois– Locke debate, which increases reasonableness, is legitiate, even i it does not contribute to resolving the debate. Seech that aears reasonable but serves the goal o decreasing reasonableness by reresenting a grou in the society as not worthy o eathy is always deagogic. It is deagoguery. Thus, roaganda o the sort that reairs wounds to deocracy and roaganda that causes such wounds are systeatically related.

Deagoguery in a deocracy takes the or o a contribution that resents itsel as exeliying the nors o ublic reason but akes a contribution a rational erson would recognize to be inconsistent with these nors. I have now given two exales o ideals o ublic reason. The rst ideal o ublic reason centrally involves an ideal o theoretical rationality. On this concetion, deagoguery is discourse that aears to ake a rational contribution to the debate at hand, but instead serves to cut o rational debate by enlisting the orces o assion to ake an iartial reasoned stance iossible. This ts Victor Kleerer's descrition o the eects o using the word "heroic" around those raised under the education syste and oral values o the Third Reich. The second ideal o ublic reason centrally involves an ideal o reasonableness. The caacity to be reasonable requires, as we have seen, a disosition to take the ersective o others in the counity in roosing reasons, to be eathetic to the, and to resect their dignity. A contribution to ublic reason is reasonable only i it takes into account the reasonable ersectives o all those citizens subject to the olicy under debate. On this concetion, de agoguery is discourse that aears to take every ersective into account but has the goal o rendering soe reasonable ersectives invisible.

A salient eature o any aradig cases o roaganda is that it is seech that owes its ecacy in ending rational debate not to its settling o the question, but rather to its erosion o second- ersonal ideals like reasonableness. In any aradig cases o roaganda, its olitical eectiveness is initially thought o as exlained by its eect in eroding the ideals o rationality, say, by cutting o debate. For exale, in the rst instance, linking Sadda Hussein to international terroris aer the tragedy o 9/11 raised ears that cut o rational debate. But it ust be aditted that it is a ossible exlanation o why it was so simple to raise such ears in the absence o coelling evidence that Iraq was a threat to US security: these ears were ebedded within a larger icture that excluded Arab Muslis ro a raework o utual resect. Whether or not this is what was haening in the debate about invading Iraq in 2003, it is undeniable that aeals to assions and ear are oen ore eective when wheeled against an eney one considers to be orally reugnant, to lack the nors o huanity. In such cases, the eectiveness o discourse or halting ideals o theoretical rationality ust be exlained in ters o its eectiveness or ideals o reasonableness, or ideals suciently like reasonableness. The reason we cut off rational debate is usually because of a loss of empathy. It may be, for example, that the real explanation of the effectiveness of fear for the decision to invade Iraq was the lack of the average American's ability to imagine himself as a member of a population being invaded and heavily aerially bombed by a vastly more powerful military force, and that in turn was the consequence of stereotypes about Arab Muslims that robbed us of the capacity for empathy toward them.46

The mechanisms by which one erodes the normative ideals of public reason are often indirect. Consider the right to free speech. The right to free speech is justified by the fact that it is required for the demands of public reason. As John Stuart Mill famously argued, we cannot expect rational deliberation (including about policy) to end in knowledge unless we allow free speech. The case for government openness is also based on the role public reason plays in democratic legitimacy. It is after all not plausible to arrive via deliberation at the best decision even about which representatives to elect without knowing what the government has been up to and what those representatives have done about it. Given the role of the ideals of public reason in conferring democratic legitimacy on state policy, in a democracy, someone who ultimately seeks to bypass democratically legitimate processes to establish a policy will do so by eroding the ideals of public reason. As we have just seen, government transparency is a requirement of public debate in a democracy. Eliminating government transparency is a way to erode the ideals of public reason, by eroding the possibility of fully informed debate about policy.

If the guiding ideal of public reason in a democratic society is reasonableness, then it follows that a paradigm way propaganda in a democratic society manifests is by representing the perspectives of some of our fellow citizens as unworthy of consideration (and, in the international sphere, representing the perspectives of our enemies as such). But it must be acknowledged that much propaganda does not seem to be of this form.

For exale, in the election in 2012, in South Carolina, Mitt Roney accused President Barack Obaa o wanting to li the work requireents o welare. This was widely acknowledged to be roaganda, or exale, it was clearly decetive, since it was known to be alse. But it aears to be econoic rather than an attet to exclude the ersectives o soe o our ellow citizens.

To understand why clais about welare rogras are in act undaentally aeals to exclude the ersectives o soe o our ellow citizens, it is worthwhile to bear in ind the chie Reublican strategist Lee Atwater's aous coents, in a 1981 interview:

You start out in 1954 by saying, "[N- word], [N- word], [N- word]." By 1968 you can't say "[N- word]"— that hurts you, backres. So you say stu like, uh, orced busing, states' rights, and all that stu, and you're getting so abstract. Now, you're talking about cutting taxes, and all these things you're talking about are totally econoic things and a byroduct o the is, blacks get hurt worse than whites.... "We want to cut this," is uch ore abstract than even the busing thing, uh, and a hell o a lot ore abstract than "[N- word], [N- word]."

Subsequent research by the Princeton olitical science ro essors Martin Gilens and Tali Mendelberg has conred the success o the strategy o linking talk o welare rogras to the idea that Black Aericans are unt to have their ersectives taken into account. Their research shows that exressions like "welare," "the oor," "ood stas," and "hoeless" all introduce the thought that Black Aericans are lazy. In his book ro 1999, Gilens shows that "the belie that blacks are lazy is the strongest redictor o the ercetion that welare reciients are undeserving."47 There is a large aount o additional evidence that "welare" has been connected with a awed ideology o race, in addition to the studies Gilens hisel has carried out. Gilens reorts siilar results ro the "wel are other" exerient ro the National Race and Politics Study o 1991: "[R]esondents are asked their iressions o a welare reciient described as either a Black or white woan in her early thirties, who has a ten- year- old child and has been on welare or the ast year. Resondents are rst asked how likely it is that the woan described will try hard to nd a job and second, how likely it is that she will have ore children in order to have a bigger welare check."48 The largest redictor o oosition to welare rogras was one's bias against Black welare others.49

Lee Atwater's quotation shows that there was a deliberate attet to aroriate the language o welare to convey in a nonobvious way what racial slurs did in 1954. Subsequent research shows that the attets o Atwater and those beore hi have been successul. Suose that the ilicitly recognized norative olitical ideal o ublic reason is reasonableness, and suose that y characterization o roaganda is correct. It ollows that one characteristic or o roaganda in a liberal deocracy takes the or o clais that rely on awed ideology to decrease eathy or a inority grou (o course there are others as well). In the next chater, I will exlain the echanis exloited by the kind o roaganda Atwater discusses, and why it is so eective at eretuating doinant grou ideologies.

In this chater, I have exlained the or o roaganda in a deocracy. To reserve the character o deocratic deliberation, those deliberating in oral and inoral debate over olicy are subject to a nor o reasonableness, which requires the to take the ersectives o others into account. Characteristically, then, negative roaganda, or roaganda, will take the or o a reasonable roosal, a roosal that sees to take everyone's ersective into account (or exale, by calling attention to a ublic threat), in the service o a goal that, rationally seaking, erodes reasonableness. Civic rhetoric is an attet to share the ersective o a grou whose ersective has been ade invisible, thereby reventing deocracy; civic rhetoric is the tool required in the service o reairing the ruture.

# LANGUAGE AS A MECHANISM OF CONTROL

What are the echaniss by which roaganda unctions in a liberal deocracy? Liberal deocratic nors ose obstacles or the deagogue. There has been very little discussion in oral seantics and ragatics on the eects o "code words" on discourse. This is robleatic. Since the cognitivist, truth- conditional raework ebodies an account o what haens when co unication unctions well, it allows us recise gras o what haens when counications ails to unction well. My worry with noncognitivist accounts, or accounts that are unsysteatic at their core, is that, while they are soeties well suited to exlain ailures o counication, they are ill suited to exlain the contrast between well- unctioning counication and oorly unctioning counication.

I a grou is deliberating about a olicy or course o action that will aect everyone in the grou, airness requires regarding everyone's viewoint as worthy o resect. But this is just to say that it is natural to exect reasonableness to be the nor governing any such deliberation, including those that are intended to issue in deocratically legitiate olicies. To say that the rincile ideal o ublic reason is reasonableness is not to deny that there are other ideals o ublic reason. Politicians ust also be, or exale, rationally consistent, objective, and logical.

One oral o the revious chater is that deagoguery in a liberal deocracy takes the or o a contribution to ublic debate that is resented as ebodying reasonableness yet in act contributes a content that clearly erodes reasonableness. A contribution is inconsistent with reasonableness i it underines the caacity or the willingness to roduce or be swayed by reasonable roosals. Reasonableness resuoses, at least in huans, the caacity or eathy or others. I I a right, we should exect aradig cases o roaganda to have as art o their counicative content that a group in society is not worthy of our respect. Deagoguery can take both linguistic and nonlinguistic or. Any characterization o deagoguery, or roaganda ore generally, that is ocused secically on language is clearly too narrow. My characterization o roaganda is accordingly erectly general. But I a unable to give an account o the mechanisms by which this occurs.

There is a science o language and counication in lace that enables us to gain soe recision about the echaniss underlying linguistic roaganda. I exloit that account to exlain how soe linguistic roaganda works. I susect the sae level o detail has not yet been achieved in our understanding o iagistic reresentation. Thereore, I will ocus on the linguistic case. I exect that uture research will be able to hel us address how the erhas ore iortant iagistic case works.

I will use oral seantics and ragatics to describe a secic echanis by which deagoguery in linguistic or lays a role in bringing into the context alse ideological belies that are aarently not art o the discussion. As we shall see, there is a great deal o evidence that there is such a linguistic mechanism. And perhaps there are analogous mechanisms in the case of images; indeed, the inspiration point in my analysis, Rae Langton and Caroline West's theory of pornography from 1999, employs similar formal semantic and pragmatic mechanisms to explain the phenomena of subordination with images. But it is not clear to me that all these *exact* mechanisms can function with images and movies, because it is not clear to me that one can make the distinction between at-issue and not-at-issue content that is at the center of the mechanism I describe. My focus is on explaining one way in which demagoguery exploits already existing nonpolitical mechanisms to be effective. This mechanism is well understood in the case of language, so we can describe it with precision.

A number of philosophers in the feminist tradition, including Catherine Mac Kinnon and Jennifer Hornsby, have argued that the function of certain kinds of speech (in their chosen example, pornography) is to silence a targeted group. The philosopher whose work has most inspired and influenced my own is Rae Langton. Langton argues, following Mac Kinnon and Hornsby, that pornographic material *subordinates* women and *silences* them.¹ In depicting subordination, Langton argues, pornographers subordinate women. Langton argues that the function of certain kinds of racist speech is "to rank blacks as inferior." Langton also argues that pornography silences women, by undermining the felicity conditions of their speech; it represents "no" as yes. My aim in this chapter is to explain some of these effects with the tools of contemporary formal semantics, by applying them to the case of propaganda.

Here is one model of how this could work; as is clear from her response to Judith Butler, it is a model from which Langton distances herself.2 An imperative is a command to act a certain way. The imperative statement "eat your beets!" directed at a three year old is a command to the three year old to do something. Pornographic speech could function as a mechanism of subordination by delivering imperative-like orders of some kind. The thought here is not that imperatives bring about their truth. Coands ust be associated with ractical authority in order to have this unction. But so too, as I will argue, does subordinating seech. The relation between ieratives and subordinating seech will be a thee o this chater, as I will draw on both seantic and ragatic eatures o ieratives in y analysis o subordinating seech. I will try to square this use o the seantics o ieratives with Langton's coelling "verdictive" account o subordinating seech.

Our discussion to this oint suggests that there should be exressions at or use in a debate that unction to exclude the perspective o certain grous in the oulation. Since deagoguery, like underining roaganda generally, is asked as ebodying the ideals with which it ultiately clashes, we should exect these exressions to oerate indirectly. That is, there should be systeatic ways o genuinely or aarently contributing to debate, which siultaneously rae the debate in such a way as to exclude the ersective o a targeted grou. The unction o these exressions is to ask the deagogic nature o the contribution, by creating awed ideological belies to the eect that the ersectives o a designated grou are not worthy o reasonable consideration.

We should exect there to be linguistic eans by use o which one can ake an aarently reasonable clai, while siultaneously, erely by using the relevant vocabulary, wearing down the ideal o reasonableness. Because these linguistic eans should be available or use to ake any oint whatsoever that ay coe u in debate about olicy, we should exect that they unction to exclude whether one takes the arative or the negative osition on the debate. Indeed, i there were no linguistic eans o excluding the ersective o certain grous ro debate, while siultaneously reresenting onesel as contributing to the debate, that would raise the susicion that reasonableness is not in act the ideal o ublic reason.

I reasonableness is the nor o ublic reason, we should exect there to be linguistic echaniss, that is, expressions, with the ollowing three roerties:

- 1. Use o the relevant exression has the eect on the conversation o reresenting a certain grou in the counity as having a ersective not worthy o inclusion, that is, they are not worthy o resect.
- 2. The exression has a content that can serve sily to contribute legitiately to resolving the debate at issue in a reasonable way, which is searate ro its unction as a echanis o exclusion.
- 3. Mere use o the exression is enough to have the eect o eroding reasonableness. So the eect on reasonableness occurs just by virtue o using the exression, in whatever linguistic context.

Here is why y characterization o roaganda entails the existence o exressions with these roerties. The exressions would have to have the rst roerty, because that would be the roerty o eroding reasonableness. The exressions would have to have the second roerty, because they would have to be able to be used in discourse that aears to eet the ideal o ublic reason. The exressions would have to have the third roerty, because they would have to be at or use, whatever one's stance on the issue at hand.

We will need soe concets in our analysis o articular cases o roaganda. The rst set o concets is ro the branches o linguistics ost relevant or our uroses, naely, seantics and ragatics. We will also need the concet o social eaning, such as ro the works o the legal theorist Dan Kahan. These will allow us to sell out how a clai can counicate an ilicit essage that runs counter to the ideals its exlicit content sees to ebody. The concets we will need are soewhat technical. But this should not distract ro the act that the henoena they are used to describe are very ailiar.

The notion o a linguistic context is central in conteorary oral seantics and ragatics. What a sentence o a natural language says deends uon the linguistic context in which it is uttered. In a context in 2014 in which President Barack Obaa utters the sentence, "I a the resident o the United States," what he says is true. In a context in which the tie is 2007, or soeone else is the seaker in 2014, what is said is not true. I will sketch soe required concets ro the theory o oral seantics and ragatics.

One notion we need in odeling linguistic context is due to the hilosoher Robert Stalnaker. It is the notion o the common ground o a conversation: "Particiants in a conversation begin with certain inoration in coon, or resued to be in coon, and it is that body o inoration that the seech acts they eror are designed to inuence. The content o an assertion will be a iece o inoration, and i the assertion is successul, then that inoration will becoe art o the body o inoration that rovides the context or the subsequent discourse."3 The coon ground o a conversation is the "inoration in coon, or resued to be co on," in a discourse.

On Stalnaker's view o content, which derives ro Ludwig Wittgenstein's treatent o content in the Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus, a content is a set of possible situations, or "worlds." A roosition on this view is that set o ossible worlds in which it is true. A coon ground is, then, a set o roositions. On Stalnaker's odel o content, the coon ground can be thought o as the intersection o all o the roositions utually resued to be known by the conversational articiants. This is itsel a set o ossible worlds, the set o ossible worlds in which the conjunction o all o the roositions in the coon ground is true. Given the odel o a roosition as a set o ossible worlds, this eans that the coon ground is the intersection o roositions, and itsel is a roosition.

According to Stalnaker's account o counication, successul counication takes the or o ruling out situations. I ask you where the gas station is; you rely that it is to the right. You exress a roosition, one true in just those ossible worlds in which the gas station is to the right, and alse in the others. When I accet your assertion, the coon ground is udated. In the new coon ground, all the ossible worlds are ones in which the gas station is to the right. This is the coon inoration. This is an elegant icture o successul counication. An assertion is ade; it is a roosal to add a roosition to the coon ground. It is debated and, i acceted, added to the coon ground. This leads us to rule out ossibilities that we had reviously entertained.

In recent years, a basically Stalnakerian icture o counication has been altered to include a ore colex notion o a context. The context is not just the set o roositions that are what is resued by the conversational inquirers. It records ore detailed inoration.

Stalnaker's odel o a coon ground is designed around declarative sentences, and the ractice o asserting the. To assert a roosition is to reresent onesel as knowing it, and to ake a roosal to add that roosition to the coon ground. But there are other seech acts that occur in conversation, such as questions ("Who went to the arty?) and co ands ("Eat your beets!"). To accoodate the contextual eects o these other seech acts, one ust have a ore co lex concetion o a context than just the coon ground. The details o this ore colex concetion o context are ront and center in ore recent work on oral seantics and oral ragatics. In Discourse Reresentation Theory, Irene Hei and Hans Ka ake contexts "structured," by aealing to the notion o a le, which records discourse inoration such as reerents or later ronouns.

The work o the oral seanticist Craige Roberts has been very inuential in recent thinking about context. According to Roberts, a context deterines not only what is and what is not known to the articiants in a discourse, but also a record o the questions that have been asked that direct the course o inquiry.4 So Roberts adds to the coon ground a record o the questions under discussion. 5 Roberts thus argues that contexts contain not just sets o roositions, but other eleents as well. I so, linguistic eaning can change not just belies, but also other sychological states.

I will be alying these resources o oral ragatics to odel the workings o deagogic seech. But I a by no eans the rst to use the in an analysis o robleatic olitical seech. As we shall see in what ollows, the hilosohers Rae Langton and Caroline West use Lewis's oral ragatics to address the har o ornograhy.6 More recently, Ishani Maitra suggests the ossibility that subordinating seech is or involves an act o ranking. Ranking is a seech act that, like Robert Stalnaker's account o assertion, involves adding a content to the shared background o a conversation. She argues that rankings don't erely seek to describe the world, but "constitute nors," and she sees that this ay require a dierent account o their content.7 She does not rovide an account o the contents o rankings in her aer. Nevertheless, Maitra clearly sees here the ossibility o extending the kind o dynaic account o conversation that is ailiar ro the work o Stalnaker and others in oral seantics and ragatics to seech acts other than assertions. It is this basic odel I a lling out and develoing in this chater.

The Dutch seanticist Frank Veltan, in his aer "De aults in Udate Seantics," ublished in 1996, adds to the context a reerence ordering on ossible worlds, eant to reect "deeasible knowledge." The idea is that certain ossible situations are conceived o as ore likely than others, and hence to be eisteically reerred. Veltan's theory is eant to handle generic statements, roughly, generalizations that structure our exectations, aking it easier to aneuver around the world. These are stateents like "birds y" or "dogs have our legs."8 An utterance o "birds y," i acceted, akes it the case that, when considering any given bird in context, the ordering on ossible worlds is one according to which worlds in which that bird ies are closer than worlds in which that bird doesn't y. This reects the bias toward situations in which a given bird that one encounters ies.

Another notion we need, in addition to that o a linguistic context, is the distinction between at- issue content and not- atissue content. Christoher Potts uses the ollowing two exa les to illustrate the distinction between at- issue and not- atissue content.9 The rst involves what he calls a "suleental exression," in this case "who lived in a working- class suburb o Boston," to ake the distinction. The second involves what he calls an "exressive," in this case "dan," to ake the distinction:

- 1. I sent art o every suer until I was ten with y grandother, who lived in a working- class suburb o Boston.
- 2. We bought a new electric clothes dryer, and I thought all there was to it was lugging it in' and connecting the vent hose. Nowhere did it say that the damn thing didn't coe with an electric lug!

As Potts writes, "[T]he suleentary relative who lived in a working- class suburb of Boston lays a secondary role relative to the inoration conveyed by the ain clause. The issue is not where the grandother lived, but rather the act that the seaker suered with her as a child." The at- issue content is what is at issue in the debate. Suleental constructions and exressives are "used to guide the discourse in a articular direction or to hel the hearer to better understand why the atissue content is iortant at that stage."

The at- issue content o an utterance is the inoration asserted by the utterance. When I utter (1), what I assert is that I sent art o every suer until I was ten with y grand other. To assert soething, as the linguist Sarah Murray describes, is to propose to add it to the common ground. To assert soething is to advance it as soething the seaker knows, and to thereby propose that its content be added to the co on ground. Subsequent arguent is debate about whether or not to accet the roosal.

In contrast, the claim about my grandmother, that she lived in a working-class suburb of Boston, is additional material that comments on what is asserted. It is not-at-issue content. The not-at-issue content of an utterance is not advanced as a proposal of a content to be added to the common ground. Not-at-issue content is *directly* added to the common ground. For this reason, not-at-issue content is in general "not negotiable, not directly challengeable, and [is] added [to the common ground] even if the at-issue proposition is rejected." This characterization of not-at-issue content is supported by much linguistic evidence; the evidence mostly involves when it is legitimate to retract a claim. The not-at-issue content is often "semantic, part of the conventional meaning."

Rae Langton and Caroline West argue that not-at-issue content is involved in pornography. Specifically, they argue that pornography has the effect of subordinating women, not by explicitly communicating a subordinating message, but by *presupposing* it. In order to make sense of what is explicitly said and illustrated in pornography, they argue, one must make the relevant sexist and subordinating presuppositions, or not-at-issue contents.

Langton and West were writing before the at-issue/not-at-issue distinction was drawn. Their theoretical model is linguistic presupposition, as described in David Lewis's seminal paper "Scorekeeping in a Language Game." Consider the examples:

- 3. It was John who solved the problem.
- 4. My wife is from Chicago.

Linguists generally hold that an utterance of (3) presupposes the proposition that someone solved the problem, and asserts that John solved the problem. Linguists generally hold that an utterance of (4) presupposes the proposition that the speaker has a wife, and asserts that she is from Chicago. One reason to think that this is the right account is that denying the speaker's claim is naturally understood as denying what is asserted, while agreeing with what is resuosed. So i soeone asserts (3), and I resond with "that's alse," the interretation o y denial is as denying that John solved the roble, not as denying that soeone solved the roble. Siilarly, i soeone asserts (4), and I resond with "that's alse," then y denial is standardly taken to be a denial that the seaker's wie is in Chicago, not that the seaker is arried. Presuosed content is a kind o not- at- issue content (roughly). Asserted content is at- issue content.

The linguist Sarah Murray argues that an assertion o a declarative sentence is a roosal to add the at- issue content to the coon ground.12 In contrast, the not- at- issue content is directly added to the coon ground. Using the exale o Cheyenne, she shows that there are exlicit linguistic arkers o not- at- issue content. In English, they are less obvious, but still resent. For exale, the exression "I hear" in (5) unctions as a "hedge"; it introduces not- at- issue content:

### 5. The resident is about to give a seech, I hear.

"I hear" unctions to coent on the at- issue content that the resident is about to give a seech. In the case o hedges like "I hear," Murray argues that they alter the at- issue content. The at- issue content o (5) is that it is possible that the resident is about to give a seech. The not- at- issue content, that the seaker heard that the resident is about to give a seech, is sily added to the coon ground. Challenges to (5) are challenges to the at- issue content, but not to the not- at- issue content that the seaker heard the at- issue content. This raises the ossibility that one can counicate a noneasily challenged eaning by attaching it to an exression as not- at- issue content.

Here is a nal exale o not- at- issue content, involving eisteic "ust" in English:

## 6. It ust be raining outside.

I soeone utters (6), she counicates that she did not hersel exerience rain, that she inferred it indirectly. Kai von Fintel and Anthony Gillies have convincingly argued that this eature o "ust" is like the evidential arkings in other languages. (Von Fintel and Gillies's view is unsurrising, because eisteic "ust" is o course by denition, like evidentials, eisteic.)13 That the agent did not witness the event o raining is not art o the asserted content o an utterance o (6). For exale, it is not easy to deny this content. It is dicult to resond to (6) by resonding with "that's wrong, you are soaking wet." The counicated content that the agent did not witness the rain herself is soething that would be very odd and rude to challenge. So doing would suggest that the agent is decient in soe way, rather than erely ordinarily isin ored. It is not- at- issue content, rather than at- issue content.

Soe kinds o not- at- issue content are easier to recover than other kinds. The kind associated with eisteic "ust" is "baked" deely into the eaning o the odal auxiliary "ust." Other kinds, such as those ound in exlicit suleental ex ressions, are ore easily targeted and identied. The roerties associated with being not- at- issue coe in degree.

Here is a roerty o resuositions that akes the not suitable or analysis as classic not- at- issue content. A resuosition o a word or a linguistic construction can be "ltered" ro a larger construction containing it. Sentence (1) resu oses that the roble was solved. But (7) does not resu ose that the roble was solved.

7. I the roble was solved, it was John who solved it.

In this case, the resuosition that the roble was solved has been "ltered" by the antecedent o the conditional, the sentence ollowing "i," naely, "the roble was solved." Si ilarly, (8) resuoses that John soked, but (9) does not:

- 8. John stoed soking.
- 9. Bill believes that John stoed soking.

In contrast, not- at- issue content cannot be "ltered."

One kind o linguistic roaganda involves reeated association between words and social eanings. Reeated association is also the echanis by which conventional eaning is ored; it is because eole use "dogs" to reer to dogs, re eatedly, that "dogs" coes to reer to dogs. My clai in this chater is that when roagandists use reeated association between words and iages, they are oring connections that serve as the basis o conventional eaning. Tyically, the conventional eaning is not- at- issue content. As is the case with conventional eaning generally, the links between word and eaning are a atter o degree, vague, and negotiable. The word "Madagascar" originally reerred to art o the ainland o Arica, but, because o changing usage, cae to reer to an island o the coast o Arica. We see the sae ossibilities or change and resisting change with the kinds o reeated associations that roaganda involves.

When the news edia connects iages o urban Blacks reeatedly with entions o the ter "welare," the ter "wel are" coes to have the not- at- issue content that Blacks are lazy. At soe oint, the reeated associations are art o the eaning, the not- at- issue content. The negative social eaning associated with "welare" unctions like the content that the agent has not directly witnessed the event that is associated with "ust," as in (6). This does not ean that soeone hearing the ter "welare" autoatically coes to believe that Blacks are lazy. It does ean that they ay have to shi to dierent vocabulary, or consciously resist the eects o the association, in conversation or otherwise, to deter the roagandistic eect.

Langton and West, in their account o ornograhy, ex lain how resuosition can be used to suggle in content that one would not necessarily accet i it was resented as the content asserted. This is a signicant discovery about how robleatic essages are counicated, either intentionally or not. However, I will relace their aeal to resuosition with the related category o not- at- issue content. The act that not- at- issue content is, in Murray's words, "directly added" to the coon ground is what akes it rie or roagandistic abuse. The act that the not- at- issue content cannot be canceled is what akes it so eective.

Those who have theorized about not- at- issue content tend to reresent not- at- issue content as content o the sae sort as at- issue content, but laying a dierent role. Our discussion o reasonableness suggests that this aroach ay be incorrect. The eect o roaganda in a liberal deocracy is to erode resect or a targeted grou. In huans, resect or a grou or a erson is characteristically based uon eathy or the. One characteristic eect o roaganda in a liberal deocracy will be to erode eathy or the ersectives o a grou in a oulation, while resenting itsel as not so doing. This eans that there will be exressions that have noral contents, which ex ress these contents via a way that erodes eathy or a grou.

How should we think o the echanis by which a contribution is in the service o the erosion o eathy or a grou o eole? In an iortant series o aers, Sarah- Jane Leslie has connected generics to robleatic social stereotying o grous. Leslie establishes that generics are cognitively fundamental generalizations that are acquired very early in lie.14 She argues that generics are one echanis, erhas a key one, by which we coe to or social essentialist views about grous.15 In "The Original Sin o Cognition," she rovides an exlanation o the eisteic robles that accetance o a generic engenders; it leads us to generalize the surrising ro erties o soe ebers o a grou onto the grou as a whole (as in "Muslis are terrorists").16 One does not need to accet all o Leslie's theory to accet the arguent that generics lausibly lay the role o stereotypes, including racial stereotyes, in any theories o stereotye (including Walter Liann's original notion, to be discussed in a subsequent chater). I a going to use Leslie's insights, together with the echaniss discussed by Veltan in his theory o generics, to exlain various eatures o roaganda. I a thereby exloiting Leslie's iortant insight that generics are or can lay the role o stereotypes, and the existence of a semantic account of at least one effect of generics that I find persuasive.

Here is one way in which a contribution can erode empathy for a group. The contribution could communicate a certain claim about that group, such as that Jews are the enemy, that women are submissive, that Blacks are violent, or that immigrants are criminal. A claim could have a perfectly ordinary at-issue content, but erode empathy by having such a proposition as its not-at-issue content. For example, if someone utters in a political speech in the United States, "There are Jews among us," it expresses a perfectly ordinary at-issue content, one that is in fact true. There are Jews in the United States. But it equally clearly conveys the not-at-issue content that Jews are the enemy, by suggesting that Jews are enemy invaders distinct from the "us" of the polity. Let us call this the content model of propaganda. According to the content model, one kind of paradigmatic propaganda in a liberal democracy would have a normal at-issue content that seems reasonable, and would also have a not-at-issue content that is not reasonable.

Here is another way of thinking of the mechanism by which a contribution could lead to an erosion of empathy for a group. The contribution could express a perfectly ordinary at-issue content, but cause a decrease in empathy or respect directly, as part of its not-at-issue function. The idea here is not, as on the content model of propaganda, that there is a not-at-issue content, acceptance of which decreases empathy for a group. It is rather that words have direct not-at-issue emotional *effects*. Let us call this *the expressive model of propaganda*. According to the expressive model, one kind of paradigmatic propaganda in a liberal democracy would have a normal at-issue content that seems reasonable, and would also have a not-at-issue effect that would decrease empathy for a group. Since decreasing empathy for a group runs counter to reasonability, its not-at-issue effects would be unreasonable.

The division in the theory of meaning between expressivist theories and content theories is central in twentieth-century philosophy. For example, theorists of value who hold that there are no ethical facts treat ethical assertion as expressive, rather than contentful. Thus, they free themselves from commitment to a domain of moral facts. The problem facing expressivist theories has always been that they cannot explain the kind of linguistic behavior that shows that our interpretation of the relevant sentences is governed by formal rules. For example, the "Frege-Geach Problem" is that declarative sentences can be embedded in more complex constructions. For example, "If you make a lot of money, then you ought to give some money to solve social injustice" is a perfectly wellformed sentence of English. Yet the mechanisms required to explain the process by which the meaning of one sentence contributes to the meanings of larger sentences containing it, the so-called problem of compositionality, all employ models of meaning that assign contents to words and sentences. Expressivists about certain kinds of language have had a very difficult time describing the mechanisms by which what are by their lights sentences that lack content can nevertheless have a systematic effect on larger linguistic constructions that embed them.

Recent work in formal semantics and the philosophy of language has broken down the decades-long impasse between expressivist and content-based models. Imperatives have something to do with *ordering*; an imperative orders actions into a certain hierarchy. "Eat your beets" places the action of eating the audience's beets ahead of the action of not doing so. (As the linguist David Beaver remarked to me, "It can't be an accident that we call commands 'orders.") The philosopher William Starr, in his paper "A Preference Semantics for Imperatives," produces a formal analysis of the effects of imperatives on the common ground. The details of his analysis are not essential to us. But the basic point of Starr's paper is that it is possible to represent imperatives as having a perfectly formally articulable effect on the context set, without representing that effect as adding a *content*. In short, one can accommodate the contextual eects o an ierative without reresenting the as a roosal to add a roosition to the coon ground.

As in Frank Veltan's analysis o the contextual eects o generic stateents, Starr reresents contexts as containing a re erence ordering on worlds. According to Starr, the eect i eratives have on the coon ground is to iose a preference ordering on ossible situations. An utterance o "eat your beets!" structures the context in a way that ranks ossible worlds in which you eat your beets to be reerable to worlds in which you do not eat your beats. Starr shows that there is no obstacle to a ull articulable oral ileentation o this view.

Veltan and Starr eloy reerence rankings in dierent ways. On Veltan's account, "birds y" has the eect o ranking worlds ore closely, in the sense o more likely, in which a given bird ies than worlds in which it doesn't; worlds in which birds one encounters y are closer than worlds in which they do not. On Starr's account, a coand has the eect o aking worlds reerable in which the coand is obeyed. These are dierent orderings on worlds. Subordinating seech eloys both.

Langton distances hersel ro an ierative account o subordinating seech.17 The reason she does is that there is an iortant distinction between a coand and the eects o subordinating seech. A coand is an order to change the world in a certain way; it is an order to change the world to t it. In contrast, uch subordinating seech ais to t the world; it ais to describe the world as it actually is, rather than change it. "Blacks are lazy" is not a coand to change the world to ake Blacks lazy; it is rather an attet to describe the world. The reerence relation relevant to ieratives (coands) has another direction o t, that o desires. Ieratives tell the hearer to change the world. The reerence relation aealed to in Veltan's analysis is o the orer kind; it ais to t the world by describing it. An utterance o "birds y" has the eect o leading one to think that worlds are ore likely in which a bird one encounters ies. Langton is correct that subordinating seech is not adequately characterized in ters o the reerence orderings eloyed in the seantics o ieratives, because it ais to t the world by describing it. But it is adequately characterized by the reerence ordering in Veltan's analysis o generics. A use o the ter "welare," or exale, leads one to udate one's re erences, by thinking that a Black erson one eets is ore likely to be lazy than not.

It is no doubt too silistic to assue that the only eect o subordinating seech is to change eisteic reerence orderings. We can enrich contexts both with eisteic reerences orderings, which order worlds according to their likelihood, and with desire- like reerence orderings. A word like "inner city" or "suer- redator" can have an eect on both; it can tell you that worlds in which young Black teenagers are violent threats tend to be closer than ones in which they are not, and it can order you not to associate with the.

Ieratives are also ilicated in the way I have suggested in exlaining the orce o certain seech acts, which can be taken as coands to udate one's eisteic reerences. The echanis here is ailiar ro the literature on ideology. Figures in the edia, as well as teachers in schools, exloit their osition as eisteic authorities to issue assertions that are not suosed to be taken as roosals, but as coands. The newscaster telling the audience soething like "austerity is needed to cut down debt" is an order to each audience e ber to add it to her stock o belies. It cannot be a roosal to add it to the coon ground, because the relation between the newscaster and the audience is undaentally asyetric. He is telling e things, not roosing things that he ay hisel give u when I resent hi with a good counterargu ent. Telling soeone soething ro a osition o authority is a command, not an assertion; it is what Pierre Bourdieu and Jean- Claude Passeron call a "gae o ctitious counication."18 The social studies teacher in school is not genuinely roosing her clais or debate.

There are any other resources in oral seantics that can be used to odel the eects o subordinating seech. A great deal o oral seantics and the hilosohy o language has been devoted to understanding anahoric ronouns, like the occurrence o "he" in the discourse "A an walked in. He was wearing a hat." It has becoe tyical to add to the context a ranking o salience on objects in the doain, and to treat certain exressions as aecting that ranking. Siilarly, we can iagine rankings o grous o eole as arts o context. Subordinating exressions would alter these rankings in dierent ways.

These echaniss ro oral seantics can be used to odel in a rigorous way an exressivist account o the unction o words that erode eathy. We can think o the not- at- issue eaning o such words, on an exressivist view, as iosing a reerence ordering on ossible situations in the coon ground. We can think o a derogatory word as iosing a re erence ordering that ranks grous in a hierarchy. There is no doubt a lethora o ways in which this occurs. In Veltan's theory, the reerence order is eisteic. An utterance o "birds y" akes the context such that, or any given bird, ossible worlds in which that bird ies are closer (to be eisteically reerred) than ossible words in which that bird doesn't y. But we can also iagine a reerence ordering that holds between worlds that is not eisteic, but rather has to do with what one desires. Certain derogating seech ight lead one to accet a reerence ordering in which worlds in which one is socializing with ebers o one's own grou are to be re erred, in the sense o ore desirable, than worlds in which one is socializing with ebers o the derogated grou.

It is lausible that a word like "welare" has, in the Aerican olitical context, as its not- at- issue content, a generic content like that Blacks are lazy, as Leslie's view would erhas redict. On Veltan's view, the result o using the ter "wel are" would be to change the reerence ordering over worlds in the linguistic context so that, or any given Aerican citizen o Arican descent, worlds in which that erson is lazy are closer than worlds in which he is not lazy. In this way, uses o the ter "welare" change the context in ways that go beyond sily adding roositions to the coon ground, or ro osing to add the to the coon ground. They change the context in a orally tractable way that reects the exressivist's insights.

In certain characteristic cases in which eisteic authority and ractical authority coe together, assertions have an ierative- like orce. Exales, as I have entioned, are teaching, edia, and the news. In such cases, an authority gure's roosal to add soething to the coon ground brings with it, in soe sense, coand- like eatures, which can be orally odeled. We can ake soe o this ore recise by reecting on Sarah Murray's account o assertion. Murray argues that an assertion is a proposal to add soething to the coon ground. Proosing is soething one does with an equal. When I tell y three- year- old son to add soething to the coon ground, say, that the Earth is the third lanet ro the sun, I a not erely proposing it to hi. I a ordering hi to add the content to his set o belies. When there are asyetrical authority relations, a roosal ay becoe a coand. When I tell y three- year- old son, "beets are good or you," I order hi to add it to his coon ground.

One cannot coand another erson to believe soething, unless one siultaneously resents evidence or the belie that is to be adoted. I cannot successully coand you to believe that you are on Mars right now. However, in cobining eisteic and ractical authority, y assertion can have the eect o a coand to change one's belies. This is what haens when we are in school listening to teachers or watching the news. One can coand soeone to believe soething, by resenting oneself as an eisteic authority, whose exert testiony is sucient to back u one's ractical coand.

As we saw reviously, Sauel Huntington's solution to the roble o "an excess o deocracy" in the United States in the 1960s and 1970s was to recoend "clais o exertise, seniority, exerience, and secial talents" in order to "override the clais o deocracy as a way o constituting authority." We can now see the echaniss at lay behind Huntington's roosal. By reresenting onesel as an "exert" on a toic, one gains the authority to coand soeone to believe soething, resenting one's status as an eisteic authority, an "ex ert," as the basis or testionial justication.

An at descrition o the derogatory eect o derogatory words is that in addition to conveying not- at- issue contents, they alter the contextually salient reerence ordering, erhas via the generic nature o the not- at- issue contents. On this view, the word "kike" just has Jewish ersons as its at- issue content, and it has as its not- at- issue content that Jews are greedy (or exale). This has the eect o altering the reerence relation in the context, so that, or any given Jewish erson, ossible situations in which he is greedy are closer than ones in which he is not. My own view is that derogatory words like "kike" have not- at- issue eanings that are both contents, and iose re erence orderings o this sort and others. My view is thereore neither a ure content odel nor a ure exressivist odel. A word can erode reasonableness in either o these ways.19

Rae Langton aously calls to our attention the undaental asymmetry o subordinating seech. Subordinating seech only works when it is eloyed by one o the doinant grous in society against a negatively rivileged grou. In short, it is at least ria acie lausible that subordinating seech ust be delivered ro a osition o authority. A hoeless, enniless an ro Aalachia cannot give coands to anaging directors on Wall Street. For the sae reason, he cannot engage in seech that has even the ossibility o successully establishing a reerence ordering aong worlds that ranks hi higher than anaging directors on Wall Street. Nor can he ake assertions that can unction as coands; his roosals to add things to the coon ground can never, given his lack o ractical authority, rise to the oint o being tellings. This is the problem of authority, which has received uch discussion in the literature on subordinating seech.20

Let's return to the structure o deliberation, any deliberation, about a olicy that is to govern a grou. As we have seen, reasonableness is a nor governing such deliberation. We should exect to nd dialectical ethods to cut o reasonable debate that naturally eerge in any society involving grou deliberation, in other words, any huan society. The ost obvious candidates to lay this role are slur words, such as the N- word (as used or Blacks), "kike," "Kraut," "Sic," and so on. The word "kike" contributes to the at- issue content, the sae denotation as "Jewish erson." But the eect o its use is to guide the discourse in a articular direction, by eroding e athy or the grou the word denotes. I one is seaking with a Jewish erson, aer "kike" has been entioned, one cannot hel but think that it is ore likely that she is greedy than not.

In an unublished aer written in 1897, "Logic," the Ger an logician Gottlob Frege drew our attention to the relation between the Geran translations o the words "dog" and "cur":

I we coare the sentences "This dog howled the whole night" and "This cur howled the whole night," we nd that the thought is the sae. The rst sentence tells us neither ore nor less than does the second. But whilst the word "dog" is neutral as between having leasant or unleasant associations, the word "cur" certainly has unleasant rather than leasant associations and uts us rather in ind o a dog with a soewhat unket aearance. Even i it is grossly unair to the dog to think o it in this way, we cannot say that this akes the second sentence alse. True, anyone who utters this sentence seaks ejoratively, but this is not art o the thought exressed. What distinguishes the second sentence ro the rst is o the nature o an interjection.

Frege's insight is that the word "cur" eans the sae thing as "dog," but contributes a negative association. Moreover, it contributes this negative association wherever it occurs in the sentence. This is a general roerty o slurs; the negative association reains, no atter where in the sentence it occurs. For exale, negated slur words also erode reasonableness:

- 4. Jason is not a kike.
- 5. Bernhard is not a kraut.

Desite the resence o "not," the eect o the use o "kike" and "kraut" reains. One cannot use (4) and (5) to deny that Jews and Gerans should not be slandered. By using (4) and (5), one is endorsing the eect o these slur words. The slurring eect o slurs ersists, in any linguistic context, even under quotation:

## 6. "Kike" is a slur or Jewish eole.

Since grou deliberation about what to do is a eature o huan society generally, we should exect slurs to occur in every huan society, not just in liberal deocracies.

The standard view o slurs is that they exress contet or the targeted grou. This is no doubt true; describing a Jewish erson as a "kike" conveys contet toward Jews. But it is not clear how contet relates to the raework o second- ersonal relations that underlies the reconditions or joint deliberation on olicies that aly to all. Here is the kind o account o slurs I nd lausible; versions have been roosed indeendently by Lynne Tirrell and Elizabeth Ca. 21 Ca argues that slurs "signal allegiance to a ersective: an integrated, intuitive way o cognizing ebers o the targeting grou."22 Siilarly, Tirrell argues that slurs, the category into which her chosen ocus o deely derogatory ters alls, have the unction o creating an "insider/outsider" distinction: "the ters serve to ark e bers o an out- grou (as out), and in so doing, they also ark the in- grou as unarked by the ter."23

Ca argues that while slurs do involve an attitude o contet, this is not their central unction:

Although it is undeniable, and iortant, that slurs denigrate, I think an associated eeling o contet is less i ortant and exlanatory than is usually assued. Rather, I think the association with contet largely alls out o a ore basic one: that the ersective is distancing in the sense that the seaker signals that he is not "o" or aligned with Gs; and ore secically, that it is derogating in the sense that the seaker signals that Gs are not worthy o resect.

According to Ca, a slur like "kike" has, as its at- issue content, Jewish eole. Its not- at- issue content is the eect she describes, o decreasing reasonableness, by lacing the targeted grou outside the second- ersonal raework o "utual resect" that underlies the ossibility o reasonableness. Di erent slurs are associated with dierent generic contents that structure the reerence relation accordingly. In each case, the reerence ordering will be such as to ake it the case that it is uch ore likely that given ebers o the targeting grou have soe roerty that excludes the ro the doain o resect. Ca describes the not- at- issue content o a slur as "signaling allegiance to a ersective," one that distances itsel ro the targeted grou. As we will see, this kind o identication with a grou is a notion that lies at the root o the notion o a awed ideology.

It is by now clear that the toic o slurs is extreely i ortant in understanding the echanis by which genocide occurs. David Livingstone Sith reorts a Jaanese veteran o the Rae o Nanjing, one o history's ost indescribably brutal assacres, as describing the Chinese as "chancorro," eaning like bugs or anials.24 In her aer "Genocidal Language Gaes," ublished in 2012, the hilosoher Lynne Tirrell describes in detail how "[t]he use o derogatory ters layed a signicant role in laying the social groundwork or the 1994 genocide o the Tutsi in Rwanda." David Livingstone Sith argues ersuasively that genocide is oen receded by dehuanization exressed in linguistic and ictorial or. But the well- established link between dehu anizing roaganda and genocide should ake all o us wary when a grou o our ellow huans is reresented as subhuan anials, insects, or verin. The essage o such reresentation is to legitiate the kind o treatent our society recoends or the relevant kind o anial.

There is, however, a robleatic assution behind the sall hilosohy literature on slurs. It is that slurs are special. The ocus in the literature is on describing their secial ro erties. But exressions with the linguistic roerties iuted to slurs are not secial. As we have seen in revious chaters, the distinctive danger o roaganda in a liberal deocracy is that it goes unnoticed. It is hard to think o a better way to exhibit this distinctive danger than by reection on the act that hilosohy roessors in liberal deocratic societies assue that there is a distinctive and easily identiable class o words the unction o which is to decrease reasonableness, which have this eect wherever they occur in a sentence. As liberal deocracy breaks down, as in the case o odern- day Hungary, exlicit slurs becoe ore accetable.25 In a liberal deocracy, slurs just are not a central roble, which is why o course work on the toic o slurs ourishes in hilosohy deartents in liberal deocracies. The roble is, rather, with words that function in discourse as slurs, but are not exlicitly slurs.

Failure to gras the act that the suosedly distinctive eatures o slurs are in act ubiquitous is not erely an oversight. It underines views o roinent recent theories o the unctioning o slurs. Luvell Anderson and Ernest Leore argue against "content theories" o slurs, o the sort that I avor. I a syathetic to Anderson and Leore's clai that ex licit slurs belong on a list o banned words, in liberal de ocratic cultures. But, as I argue below, not only olitics but also everyday discourse involve aarently innocent words that have the eature o slurs, naely, that whenever the words occur in a sentence, they convey the robleatic content. In this chater, ollowing Sally Haslanger, I will show that even aarently unrobleatic words like "other" also convey harul social eanings whenever they are used. Yet "welare" and "other" have exactly the roerty that slurs have, ossession o which Anderson and Leore aintain is only exlicable on the hyothesis that the words with those roerties are on a list o banned words. Thereore, their analysis ails. The Anderson and Leore analysis is in tension with the existence o roaganda.26

The attet to introduce words that unction as slurs is a regular and systeatic eature o olitical debate. This is the oint o the quotation ro Lee Atwater in 1981 with which we concluded the last chater. The Princeton olitical scientist Tali Mendelberg, in her onuental study ro 2001 o racial aeals in Aerican olitics, The Race Card, gives a detailed exlanation o the echaniss involved in ilicit racial aeals.

The racial redisositions o white Aericans are very well docuented; they aear in act to be a eranent eature o the Aerican syche. But in the 1960s, what Mendelberg calls "a nor o racial equality" eerged in the Aerican body olitic. Mendelberg's exression "nor o equality" is however highly isleading. What is true is that certain kinds o reviously accetable, very explicit ors o racis began to elicit strongly negative reactions ro white Aericans. This is not seech that is by any stretch o the iagination atly described as alling under a "nor o equality." But it is clear, as the Lee Atwater quotation we have seen attested, that certain kinds o reviously accetable racist clais becae unaccetable in the late 1960s.

The new, less racist nors o ublic olitical discourse orced olitical roagandists to seek a way o reaching the racial biases o Aericans without exlicitly and obviously violating the new structure o exlicit nors surrounding race. Lee Atwater was by no eans the rst to ursue the search or ilicit eans o counicating disresect or Blacks. It had been a central counication strategy o the Reublican Party or at least a decade. For exale, President Richard Nixon's chie o sta, H. R. Haldean, wrote in his diary, "President ehasized that you have to ace that the whole [wel are] roble is really the blacks. The key is to devise a syste that recognizes this, while not aearing to. . . . Pointed out that there has never in history been an adequate black nation, and they are the only race o which this is true."29

Aer the Civil Rights Moveent, the vast ajority o Aericans consciously adhered to a nor that ade very ex licit racist exression ierissible. However, Aericans retained the racial biases that are so central to the national identity o the country. These acts led olitical strategists to aeal to words that were not obviously slurs, or even obviously reerences to Black Aericans, but unctioned in exactly the way Ca describes the unction o slurs, by reoving resect ro Black Aericans.

Mendelberg and her Princeton colleague Martin Gilens have both studied the eects o the use o the ter "welare" on olitical oinions. They have discovered that the use o the ter "welare" leads to a riing o white racial bias. In other words, the ere use o "welare," and resuably also "ood stas," as well as soe other exressions reerencing social sending rogras, ries racial bias against Blacks. A conclusion ro this research is that "any allusion to a racially tinged issue like welare ay racialize a caaign, even i it alludes to white reciients."30 Most interestingly or the toic o slurs, Mendelberg, via a coelling exerient with nonstudents in Michigan, shows that the racial- bias eects actually decrease i a candidate's essage is ade exlicitly racial in character.31

Studies that docuent the eects o riing are helul or telling us about various eects. They are less helul about the mechanisms. My goal here is to describe the linguistic echaniss that underlie the sorts o eects discussed by Mendelberg and Gilens. It is in eect to say what it is to rie racial bias with words, once those words have been roagandized, within a raework that allows us to see when olitical debate can be successul.

Slurs or Black Aericans are obviously exlicitly racial. In the resence o Mendelberg's so- called nor o equality, slurs are uch less eective than nonslurs in having the kind o e ect that hilosohers assue is indicative o slur words. More generally, in a liberal deocracy like the United States, esecially aer the Civil Rights Moveent, ilicit essages are vastly ore eective in achieving the results that hilosohers attribute to slurs. Philosohers working on slurs, articularly Ca, have arrived at an elegant descrition o how roagandistic language unctions. But to attribute the eect to slurs is to locate the henoenon in the wrong lace. In the resence o a nor o racial equality, the eects on reasonableness are ost draatic in cases in which the grou is only implicitly targeted. In the United States at least, the ocus hilosohers have laced on exlicit slurs is islaced.

In the case o "welare" and "entitleent" and siilar language surrounding social welare rogras, there was a deliberate attet to link the with not- at- issue contents that are racial in character. But any exressions carry with the notat- issue content that is olitical in nature. Many and erhas ost exressions have the roerties that only slurs are suosed to have, not- at- issue content that cannot be denied and is directly added to the coon ground. Most words carry with the, in all o their occurrences, not- atissue eanings that cannot be easily exunged, i at all.

Politics involves a constant search or words that do not a ear to be slurs, but that carry a not- at- issue content that rejudices olitical debate. Consider the recent legal debate about the exressions "illegal iigrant" and "illegal aliens." There is an obvious worry that these exressions carry not- at- issue content that raes debates about iigration in a way that ails to take into account the ersective o the iigrants. In 2006, the National Association o Hisanic Journalists urged the news edia to cease using "dehuanizing ters," such as "illegals" and "illegal aliens."32 In the words o the article, "[T]he association has always denounced the use o the degrading ters 'alien' and 'illegal alien' to describe undocuented iigrants because it casts the as adverse, strange beings, inhuan outsiders who coe to the US with questionable otivations." Desite this and subsequent leas, the suree courts used the exression "illegal iigrant" in a dozen cases. In her very rst decision in 2009, Justice Sonia Sotoayor introduced the exression "undocuented iigrant" into a decision in lace o "illegal iigrant." In a decision by the Caliornia Suree Court led on January 2, 2014, in a long ootnote, the court ollowed suit, aking a note o its use o "undocu ented iigrant," which "avoids the otential robleatic connotations o alternative ters."33 In olitics, as Carl Schitt noted, terinological questions are o the highest iortance.

On the icture I a sketching, certain words are ibued, by a echanis o reeated association, with robleatic i ages or stereotyes. One can use these words to exress ordinary contents, and exlicitly deny colicity with the associated robleatic iage or stereotye. For exale, in a debate during the Reublican riary residential caaign in 2012, Juan Willias asked a candidate, Newt Gingrich:

You recently said, "black Aericans should deand jobs, not ood stas. You also said, "oor kids lack a strong work ethic," and roosed having the work as janitors in their schools. Can't you see that this is viewed, at a iniu, as insulting to all Aericans, but articularly to black Aericans?

Gingrich answered, "No. I don't see that," and received a loud ovation ro the audience. The interest o the exchange is the intensity o the audience's reactions. Clearly, this was the ost eotionally charged oent o the debate. This is recisely because o the racially loaded not- at- issue content o the discourse, exressions like "work ethic" and "ood stas." What is iortant to note is that even the act o raising the ex ressions to salience by Juan Willias conveyed the negative social eanings, insiring characteristically strong eotion in the audience. This is how roaganda works. It is ossible to challenge its eects, but even using the exressions to do so runs the risk o invoking these very eects.35

A urther concet is going to be essential to exlain the echaniss that roaganda exloits. The concet is that o social meaning, as it is ound, or exale, in the works o legal theorists such as Dan Kahan, who advance the exressive theory o law. The institution o arriage is a good exale o soething with a clear social eaning. The social eaning o arriage, as the hilosoher Ralh Wedgwood has argued, involves "sexual intiacy (which in heterosexual coules oen leads to childbirth); it involves the coule's cooeration in dealing with the doestic and econoic necessities o lie (including raising children i they have any); and it is entered into with a utual long- ter coitent to sustaining the relationshi."36 Marriage is an institution that has a owerul social eaning. An exale Kahan uses o social eaning in the law is The Flag Protection Act o 1989.37 The social eaning o the law was to ehasize atriotis. Given the act that hardly anyone ever burns ags in rotest, its only urose in act was to exress this social eaning. Exressive theorists o law bring our attention to the social eaning o laws. Social eaning does not need to take the or o truth- evaluable contents. The social eaning can take the or o a coand, an instruction to reer certain situations above other situations. We have already exlained how to odel orally social eanings that are nontruth conditional in this way.

Institutions and laws have social eaning. Words too have social eanings. As Michael Walzer writes, "[T]he words prostitution and bribery, like simony, describe the sale and urchase o goods that, given certain understandings o their eaning, ought never be sold or urchased."38 Proaganda characteristically involves attaching robleatic social eanings to seeingly innocuous words that are used to describe olicy, in eect aking the word "welare" like the word "rostitution." The social eanings o these words are not- at- issue content. In short, even evaluating the roosal eans that one ust accet the social eaning. It is odd to challenge the social eaning; the social eaning associated with a word is acceted even i the clai ade with the associated word is rejected.

Recall Victor Kleerer's descrition o the associations with the word "herois" during National Socialis at the beginning o this book. The edia associated these words with secic iages: the racecar driver, the tank coander, the Stor Trooer. The iages the edia associated with these words becae art o the social eaning o the ter "herois" or those raised under National Socialis. As with Frege's descrition o the iages associated with the ter "cur," it was iossible or those raised under National Socialis not to have the word evoke those iages. The Reublican "Southern Strategy" was to associate the language surrounding social wel are rogras with iages reinorcing the stereotye o urban Blacks as lazy.

Linda Taylor was a Black woan in Chicago in the 1970s who aears to have been a serious criinal.39 She was arrested on charges o welare raud, o raudulently ling wel are clais under our searate aliases, and charged with stealing \$8000 ro the governent. In reorting on the case, the Chicago Tribune described her as a "welare queen" who rode a Cadillac to ick u her raudulent welare checks. This was a crie or which Taylor was sentenced to rison. Welare raud was, however, the least o her cries.

When Ronald Reagan ran or the residency in 1976, he a roriated the exression "welare queen" to raise the secter o assive Black raud. In a caaign rally, he said, "In Chicago, they ound a woan who . . . used 80 naes, 30 addresses, 15 telephone numbers to collect food stamps, Social Security, veterans' benefits for four nonexistent deceased veteran husbands, as well as welfare. Her tax-free cash income alone has been running \$150,000 a year." This was nowhere near an accurate description of the case, and welfare fraud was not a central source of Taylor's income (robbery was). But the image of the Black, Cadillac-driving welfare queen turned out to be very powerful, and was the dominating motif surrounding political debate about social welfare programs in the United States for decades to come. As with the case of "heroism" for those raised under National Socialism, it is scarcely possible for Americans raised during this time not to find the image of a Cadillac-driving Black urban woman popping into their head when they hear the word "welfare."

The word "welfare" in the United States of America denotes a range of state and federal programs that provide "cash benefits to the able-bodied, working poor," an example of which would be Temporary Assistance for Needy Families (TANF, formerly AFDC).41 The contribution the word "welfare" makes to the explicit claim made by assertions of sentences containing it is these programs. So a politician using the word "welfare" can appear to be making eminently reasonable claims; after all, a politician is supposed to be talking about government programs. Furthermore, politicians who militate against welfare usually do so with the appearance of primarily caring about the well-being of their Black fellow citizens.

In March 2014, US Representative Paul Ryan released a 204-page report titled "The War on Poverty: 50 Years Later." The report argues that welfare programs have removed incentives from work. Welfare programs have created a "poverty trap." Summarizing its findings on Wednesday, March 12, 2014, Representative Paul Ryan said on Bill Bennett's "Morning in America" show, "We have got this tailspin of culture, in our inner cities in particular, of men not working and just generations of men not even thinking about working or learning the value and the culture of work. There is a real culture problem here that has to be dealt with." US Reresentative Barbara Lee resonded, "My colleague Congressan Ryan's coents about 'inner city' overty are a thinly veiled racial attack and cannot be tolerated." Lee said in an eail to reorters, "Let's be clear, when Mr. Ryan says 'inner city,' when he says 'culture,' these are sily code words or what he really eans: 'black.' "

Ryan's roosal sounds on the ace o it very reasonable. He devoted a great deal o tie to writing a two- hundred- age iece all about the robles o the "inner city." It argues, using the language o econoics, that welare rogras are to blae or these robles, chie aong the lack o a "work ethic." It will hel those in the inner city to be orced to work. It will irove their "work ethic." This sounds like a reasonable roosal, devoted to heling those in the "inner city" irove theselves.

O course, the widesread Aerican view that those in "the inner city," that is, Black Aericans, lack a "work ethic" could not ossibly be due to social welare rogras, which, aer all, originated only in the 1960s. As Tali Mendelberg shows, stereotyes o Black Aericans have reained constant throughout the history o the reublic. The justication or slavery was that Black Aericans have a lack o "work ethic," and as a result need secial incentives to work. Ryan is sily suggesting that the secial incentive be starvation, rather than slavery. It is hardly a roosal he would oer to those not in "the inner city."

In the United States, the language that naes ederal aid rogras has acquired a social eaning that exresses disdain or Black Aerican citizens. It counicates that Blacks are lazy. For exale, in Aalachia, there is serious ultigenerational overty and uneloyent. Yet I susect ew Aericans would describe ioverished white Aalachian residents as "lazy." I so, then to clai that ultigenerational overty aong urban Blacks is a cause o, and is caused by, laziness is to endorse a racial dierence between oor whites and oor Blacks. This racial dierence is the social eaning o the word "welare." In other countries, ederal assistance rogras have acquired siilar social eanings connected to i igrant grous. This is one general echanis by which ro aganda unctions, esecially in situations in which there is a nor against the social eaning being exlicitly exressed.

We are now in a osition to see the illiberal nature o ro aganda, in the way it akes deocratic deliberation iossible. The Reublican Party, via its Southern Strategy, associated the ters or certain social rogras with long- standing Aerican racist stereotyes.
