Raising doubts about such rogras requires using the standard ter inology or the. But the standard terinology aects the discussion by aking salient these long- standing racial stereotyes. It thereore becoes dicult to criticize these rogras without seeing to be a racist. Reublican Party roaganda has ade deocratic deliberation about the erits o these rogras ore colicated. This exlains the uzzling henoenon to which Jonathan Chait brought our attention, discussed in the very rst ew ages o this book.

We can also now understand the quotation ro Victor Kleerer about the eects o the word "herois" on those who grew u under National Socialis. The National Socialists successully linked the ter "herois" to various sy bols o Teutonic hegeony. These sybols, that o the Stor Trooer or the racecar driver, were the social eaning o "herois." The concets o liberalis are universal and neutral; no one grou is singled out. In contrast, National Socialist ideology is rooundly illiberal, as it singles out the Teutonic race and the Jews or secial treatent. Kleerer notes that as soon as "herois" was entioned, the eole in the class lost all ability to gras the concets o liberal deocracy. The reason is that the word "herois" has a social eaning that is ro oundly illiberal. Given the nature o not- at- issue content, that social eaning is iediately acceted by those raised under National Socialis once the word "herois" is entioned. Acceting the social eaning o "herois" leaves one in a seech context with a coon ground (in the sense exlained) that is incoatible with the resuositions o liberalis. That exlains Kleerer's coent that "it was iossible to have a roer gras o the true nature o huanitarianis, culture, and deocracy i one endorsed this kind o concetion, or to be ore recise isconcetion, o herois."

Proaganda is o course not just aied at those who share the roagandist's ideology. Proaganda is very oen aied at those who are its targets. We will see, in subsequent chaters, that roaganda is the eans by which the highly rivileged grou in a society controls negatively rivileged grous. I will exlain soe sychological and eisteological echaniss underlying its ecacy. But we now are in a osition to see the linguistic echaniss o ecacy. The notion o not- at- issue content is one way negatively rivileged grous coe to accet the doinant ideology. The doinant grou tries to lace ebers o the subordinated grou in a osition so that erely engaging in debate requires acceting certain clais about their own ineriority. Mebers o subordinate grous ay not believe the not- at- issue content, but to counicate with the chosen words they ust act like they believe it.

None o this is to deny that the use o these ters ay be challenged or rearoriated. For exale, in the United States, the ter "Obaacare" was initially introduced as a eans o reerring negatively to the Aordable Care Act. But then it was rearoriated as a nonnegative way o reerring to the act. However, such challenges require sucient control o edia and other instruents o ower that are oen outside the control o ebers o the subordinated grou. Successul challenge and rearoriation very oen can take lace only in the context o soething aroxiating equal social ooting.

The linguistic echaniss at work exlain why in conversations between ebers o the doinant grou and e bers o the subordinate grou, the ebers o the subordinate grou eel ressure to accet the negative stereotyes o their own grou. For exale, when a white US citizen is seaking to a Black US citizen about the "robles in the inner city," there will be ressure, just to ove the conversation orward, or the Black citizen to say that she recognizes that any eo le in the inner city are in act lazy and violent. So there will be ressure, just or conversation ease, to accet the stereotye o one's grou, and o course then to ersonally distance onesel ro that stereotye. Subordinate grou ebers ay be led to accet, however rovisionally, the negative stereotye o their grou, simply to enter smoothly into any conversation about their group with members of the dominating group. This is a consequence that ollows straightorwardly ro the linguistic echanis involved.

Doinant grou roaganda will tyically roagate negative stereotyes o subordinate grous, via exloitation o notat- issue content. It will reresent ebers o that grou as not worthy o recirocity. So roaganda will lead to diinished sel- resect on the art o subordinate grous. I sel- resect is, as John Rawls has argued, "erhas" the ost iortant riary good, then roaganda will lead to diinished sel resect. So roaganda leads to inequalities in erhas the ost iortant o all riary goods. Any olitical hilosoher concerned with inequalities in the social basis o sel resect ust thereore worry about roaganda.42

The social eaning o "welare" in the United States is soething like "Blacks are lazy." The view that Blacks are lazy is a awed ideological belie, in a sense to be exlained in subsequent chaters. The word "welare" has becoe roagandized, because it has been attached to this social eaning. But the eloyent o ideological social eanings as not- at- issue contents is not the only way in which roaganda that erodes reasonableness works.

Which clais one akes with the sentences one utters vary, deending uon the context in which they are ade. I one erson says, "I a angry," another erson is not contradicting her by saying, "I a not angry." They are aking clais about dierent eole. This kind o context- deendence is due to the English rst- erson ronoun "I," which can contribute di erently to the clais ade by sentences in which it occurs, de ending uon the context. The sae is true o the rst- erson lural ronoun "we." Soeone's use o "we" can reer to the eole in this roo, or it can reer to the inhabitants o Berlin, or the inhabitants o Euroe. One can exloit the contextdeendency o natural language to erode reasonableness.

One kind o context- deendence in natural language is related to the quantier words, such as "every" and "soe." A sentence like "every student seaks Mandarin" akes dierent clais, deending uon the doain or the quantied ex ression "every student." I the doain is the students in the roo, it akes one clai. I the doain is the students in the school, it akes another. Doain restriction o quantiers can be used as a echanis to erode reasonableness. We are all ailiar with strategic uses o the doain o quantication. One lace they eerge is in the use o the rst erson lural ronoun, "we" or "us." Chater 13 o Victor Kleerer's The Language of the Third Reich is called "Naes." Proaganda that erodes reasonableness is not just used to derogate grous. It is oen, in liberal deocracies, used to raise doubts about individuals. Miranda Fricker argues that when a social eaning to the eect that the target is "less than ully huan" is counicated, its urose is oen to iugn the credibility o the target.43 Fricker divides eisteic trustworthiness into two coonents: competence and sincerity. 44 Political roaganda is generally in the service o challenging one o these two coonents o the credibility o its target. Aong the docuents released ro the archives o Edward Snowden are reorts o soething called The Joint Threat Research Intelligence Grou (JTRIG), a secret UK governent oeration under the ausices o GCHQ, the British signals intelligence agency. The stated goal o JTRIG was to use social networking and blogging or "roaganda," "decetion," "ass essaging," "ushing stories," "alias develoent," and "sychology."45 It is a rier in the roduction o roaganda. Soe o it is devoted to the roduction o otivated reasoning via, or exale, identity rotective cognition, by rein orcing ebershi in a nonthreatening or ro- governent grou ("aring one's ebershi in an iortant reerence grou," as in Dan Kahan's work). For exale, one o the reorts is called "The Art o Decetion: Training or Online Covert Oerations"46 and has instructions on bringing grous together (via "shared ideology") and tearing the aart (sowing "ideological dierence"). But other arts o it are devoted to "discrediting targets." There is one slide on discrediting individuals, and another on discrediting cororations. The way to discredit individuals is to ake the see ersonally reugnant, to destroy their ersonal reutations (or exale, "write a blog urorting to be one o their victis"). Fricker's oint that the social eaning "less than ully" is regularly used to iugn the credibility o a target receives a good deal o corroboration ro the act that the anuals o roaganda that are in use in soe roinent Western deocracies aeal to that very ethod o underining credibility.

Seech that counicates the social eaning "less than ully" is generally illiberal, because it reresents an individual or grou as unreasonable, that is, as having clais that are not worthy o our attention. When in a liberal deocracy such seech also exloits the nor o reasonableness, it tyically aniests as a characteristic or o deagogic roaganda.

Focusing on deagogic uses o reasonableness, we have looked at a diversity o ethods that language uses to oent roaganda. Our discussion raises the question o whether it is lausible to ban roaganda, while retaining the reedo o seech, as one ight ban slurs. It is too large o a toic to take on here. But it is worth briey reecting uon the diculty o the task.

Social eaning is ubiquitous. Elizabeth Anderson and Richard Pildes rovide a nuber o good exales o how judges and lawyers eloy social eaning to rejudice debate.47 Their descrition reveals how social eaning is used to rejudice debate in a characteristically roagandistic way. It also reveals the ervasiveness o the use o social eaning in deliberation. In describing the debate about ederalis, they write, "In Printz, Justice Scalia characterizes Congress as having 'dragooned' state ocials and as having reduced the states to '[]uets o a ventriloquist Congress,' which hardly sees consistent with the '[]reseveration o the States as indeendent olitical entities.' "48 In a debate about whether or not the states were being unairly subordinated, Justice Scalia e loyed exressions with a social eaning that conveys "degradation, subordination, and doination," thereby atteting to bias deliberation that is intended recisely to establish whether or not iroer degradation and subordination are in act occurring.

As the descrition given by Anderson and Pildes shows, it will not be obvious in advance which words have olitical not- at- issue contents that shae debate in robleatic ways, or how they do so. In her APA Presidential Address in 2013, Sally Haslanger ade the oint that while the slur word "slut" has an obvious olitical not- at- issue content, the seeingly innocuous word "other" also has a olitical not- at- issue content, one that involves the resuosition that "one's sex is relevant to one's arental nurturing."49 We can think o the eects o "other" either as adding a certain content to the coon ground or as iosing a reerence relation on ossible worlds that ranks worlds in which mothers have these features as preferable. Haslanger's point is that a great many words have some kind of social meaning, that is, not-at-issue content, even a word as innocuous as "mother." These social meanings, like the social meaning of slurs, cannot be divorced from the use of these expressions either. The words with the most political efficacy are presumably going to be the seemingly innocuous ones, those words that do not appear to be slurs but are associated with a social meaning that is disabling in some way.

What are the prospects of coming up with a method of banning social meanings that operate in illiberal ways? I am skeptical. Think of replacing "my wife" and "my husband" with the expression "my marriage partner." There is so much social meaning conveyed by the former expressions that is lacking in the latter; these social meanings are embedded into a lived illiberal practice. The only way to eliminate the problematic social meanings is to undermine the illiberal practices that slot being a wife and being a husband into such different social roles.

We can think, if we wish, of words as names for properties and things. But if we do so, we must simultaneously remember that words are not *just* names for properties and things. In his essay "General Semantics and Propaganda," published in 1939, S. I. Hayakawa writes:

In fact, there is nothing that can be named, let alone described, without invoking the wraiths of an entire contextual system. What is "money"? What is a "house of correction"? What is a "professor"? What is a "musician"? . . . a "tom-boy"? . . . a "mortgage"? . . . a "cat"?50

The word "professor" truly applies to a range of human beings. This may lead us to think that calling someone "professor" is simply to include her among these human beings. But including her among those human beings *in that way* is also to do a number of other things. It is to make salient in the conversation a range of presuppositions about the institution of the university, presuppositions that may naturally lead to the belief that she teaches students. It is also to convey a certain social meaning: perhaps someone with liberal political views, who is practically incompetent. The fact that words are the product of human culture means that reasoning is nowhere near as straightforward as it appears from logic text-books to be.

The fact that there are multiple effects of an utterance of a sentence on a context makes it possible to say something that is reasonable yet alter the context in such a way that is unreasonable. It therefore explains how to appear reasonable while being unreasonable. But it might be natural to infer a more drastic conclusion from the ubiquity of social meaning and institutional presuppositions associated with words. It is tempting to infer that the complexities in communication show that it is not really possible to approximate *any* ideal of public reason. Perhaps this stronger conclusion is true. But it is at this stage unwarranted; it would just be a guess.

Just as it is natural for some to think that the stronger, pessimistic conclusion that communication according to ideal norms is impossible follows from the diverse ways in which utterances affect beliefs, it may also be natural to think that to avoid the stronger, pessimistic conclusion, one needs to speak in some kind of ideal language, which lacks this feature. But the search to make ordinary communication akin to reasoning with schematic letters in logic is futile. And in any case, an ideal language for communication is not necessary to avoid the pessimistic conclusion. All the different ways in which utterances affect context do not necessarily cancel one another out, as they do with propaganda.

The use of the tools of logic and semantics to understand communication and how it is impeded is often thought to carry with it the presupposition that natural language is like the bare forms of logical languages. It is folklore that David Lewis named one of his pioneering papers in formal semantics "General Semantics" in partial mockery of the program of General Semantics, by Alfred Korzybski, between the two world wars. Korzybski sought to describe how propaganda worked, and to devise methods to avoid it, by reflection on logic and psychology. But as Rae Langton, Mary Kate Mc Gowan, and Ishani Maitra have showed, the formal tools described by Lewis, especially in his paper "Scorekeeping in a Language Game," are in fact immensely helpful in isolating problematic effects of language.

My chapter as well is an exercise in showing how the tools of semantics and pragmatics are helpful in understanding the linguistic mechanisms of subordination. The usefulness of the truth-conditional framework at the core of the tools I have employed is that it gives us an account of how deliberation works, when it is successful and straightforward. Successful deliberation is a matter of proposing a content to be added to the common ground, which is then debated. If the participants in the debate accept the assertion, then they eliminate possible situations in which it is false and continue to the next question at issue. This cognitivist framework allows us a clear sense of one part of the *structure* of deliberation.

Many utterances communicate information that is not directly "up for debate." In the main, these are harmless contents. For example, when I speak, it is not "up for debate" that I am speaking. When I say, "that damn table," it is not "up for debate" that I have a negative attitude toward the table. When I say, "it must be raining," the debate is about whether or not it is raining, not whether I inferred it indirectly. We can challenge the material that is "not up for debate." But so doing typically distracts from the topic being discussed.

Because the truth-conditional framework allows us at least to see how the core structure of unbiased communication works, it might be thought that its employment suggests that we could communicate in a way in which that ideal was realized.51 But the fact that we can usefully describe the way that propaganda uses linguistic complexity with precise or simple tools does not mean that our ordinary discourse consists of words that are precise or simple tools. A straightforward exale is an abiguous word that can be described in two recise, nonabiguous ways. This chater has been an exercise in the use o the tools o oral seantics and ragatics to describe various roagandistic eects. I have tried to show that the eort to systeatize the unsysteatic ways in which language oerates can hel us understand when it is used deviously in counication.

The ultiarious eects o ordinary seech do, however, raise an iortant theoretical issue in olitical theory, about the role o ideal nors o ublic reason. John Rawls has argued that ideal concetions ust be "ractically ossible." Given just the linguistic colexity I have described, is it even ractically ossible to ollow nors such as reasonableness, objectivity, and theoretical rationality? This is a large question with no straightorward answer. The reason is that we ust have a better gras o what is it for a discourse to be guided by a norm.

We can think o reasonableness, or theoretical rationality, as ideal deliberative nors guiding discussion. The question at issue is whether the colexity o actual huan counication akes such deliberative ideals hoeless or useless. What role do nors o ublic reason have when counication is so oen indirect and colex?

The ost salient exales o shared nors guiding co unicative acts are the nors governing seech acts, such as assertion and promising. It is widely agreed that in order or there to be a ractice o assertion or roising in a counity, there ust be a regularity within certain ordinary contexts o seakers taking what Haberas calls an "interersonal binding and bonding relationshi" with their audience.52 Di erent seech acts deterine dierent such relations, which are the nors guiding the relevant seech acts:

The binding and bonding relationshi into which the seaker is willing to enter with the erorance o an illocutionary act signies a guarantee that, in consequence o her utterance, she will ulll certain conditions— or exa le, regard a question as settled when a satisactory answer is given; dro an assertion when it roves to be alse; ollow her own advice when nds hersel in the sae situation as the hearer. . . . Thus, the illocutionary force of an acceptable speech act consists in the fact that it can move a hearer to rely on the speech- act- typical obligations of the speaker. 53

As Haberas here akes clear, the existence o a seech act in a counity deends uon the existence o a regularity in the counity, erhas constrained to a range o regularly encountered and identiable contexts, in which seakers ul ll the obligations o that seech act. Tiothy Williason akes a siilar oint when he notes that the seech act o assertion can only exist i there is "at least general sensitivity" to the violation o its governing nor. I it is rare or eole in a counity to be sanctioned or the act o uttering alse sentences in utterances o declarative sentences, or (erhas equivalently) i it is rare or eole to live u to the coit ent o uttering truths (or known roositions) when using declarative sentences, then we ay conclude that there is no seech act o assertion in that counity.

The colexities o counication we have surveyed do not underine, or exale, standard suggestions or nors or assertion. What is asserted is the at- issue content o an utterance. I have argued that roaganda tyically aects the not- at- issue content o an utterance. It enters into the coon ground by routes other than assertion. In act, this is key to the kinds o deagoguery I have in this chater discussed; the assertion ust exress a reasonable at- issue content in order or the act to be eective qua roaganda; roagandists seek to retain reasonableness (or any other deliberative ideal) at the level o assertion, but violate reasonableness at another level.

There is widesread agreeent that in order or a certain kind o seech act to exist in a counity, there ust be nors in lace in that counity, in the way Haberas describes. This suggests that a arallel story can be told about deocratic ideals, which are, aer all, nors governing ublic counication. On this account, the degree to which a society satises a deocratic ideal o rationality or reasonableness can be easured by the degree to which those who enter ublic olitical discourse coit theselves to ollowing these ideals, and the degree to which those who deviate ro it are sanctioned.

One ight, however, worry, given just the colexity about counication surveyed in this chater and the ervasiveness o roaganda, that no actual state would count as deocratic to any reasonable degree, i nor guidance was like what is at issue in the nors governing seech acts like assertion. Given the colexity we have discussed, erhas no deliberative ideal o ublic reason has ever been strictly adhered to in the assing o any olicy in the United States; certainly or the vast ajority o olicies it has not. As we have seen ro Anderson and Pildes, discussion in the Suree Court regularly involves the counication o unreasonable social eanings. In contrast, i ost utterances o declarative sentences were known to be alse by the seaker and never sanctioned, there would be no seech act o assertion. Is there a less deanding odel o nor governance available or the task?

In his book The Public and Its Problems, ublished in 1927, John Dewey conronts one o the ain robles or deocracy osed in Walter Liann's book The Phantom Public, ublished in 1925. Liann there argues that there is no ublic, or at best there is a phantom one. The acts o the division o labor, o geograhical location, and so on threaten the idea o an intersection o interests in a large, geograhically diverse oulation. Anything that holds 51 ercent o the eole together is not a coon good, a set o iortant and valuable coon interests, but rather an aeal to eotion, a "call to ars." There is no interesting notion like that o a ublic, a deocratic counity, or a deocratic society. Arguing or the coon good is arguing or nothing at all.

The problem Lippmann raises is that if there is no set of interests to be taken as the public's interests, one cannot choose to be bound by the result of a public deliberative procedure aimed at furthering the common good, that is, the good of the public. But something like this is Dewey's deliberative ideal. In the face of arguments Dewey admits are cogent in support of the view that there is no public or public interests, Dewey suggests considering the characteristic elements of democracy to be ideals that ought to guide our behavior if we want our society to become more democratic:

[Democracy] is an ideal in the only intelligible sense of an ideal: namely, the tendency and movement of some thing which exists carried to its final limit, viewed as completed, perfected. Since things do not attain such fulfillment, but are in actuality distracted and interfered with, democracy in this sense is not a fact and never will be. But neither in this sense is there or has there ever been anything which is a community in its full measure, a community unalloyed by alien elements. The idea or ideal of a community presents, however, actual phases of associated life as they are freed from restrictive and disturbing elements, and are contemplated as having attained their limit of development.54

Thus, Dewey suggests that democracy functions as an ideal. Dewey even has a particular suggestion about how these ideals ought to regulate the behavior of an actual society struggling with "the ills of democracy." When confronted with the daily reminders of the nonrealistic features inherent in the ideals of democracy, we should nevertheless adhere to the ideals, which means trusting our fellow deliberators and abiding by the outcome of the deliberative process. If this is what it is to follow a deliberative ideal, it is possible to follow it despite its persistent failure to match reality. This attitude is aptly described as *having faith in the democratic process*. That it is so natural to appeal to such language is evocative of John Dewey's contention "that the cure for the ailments of democracy is more democracy."55

As we have seen, what Dewey means is that in the face of the fact that "democracy [in the ideal sense] is not a fact and never will be," we must nevertheless have faith in democratic ideals in our political deliberations. By this, Dewey meant that the ideals should in some sense guide our actions. But in which sense?

Lara Buchak has usefully provided a characterization of faith, which can help us understand more precisely the notion at issue. Her characterization is meant to be perfectly general, by which I mean that it is intended to apply to all the different relations that count as faith: faith between people, faith that a proposition is true, and so on.

A person has faith that X, expressed by A, if and only if that person performs act A when there is some alternative act B such that he strictly prefers A & X to B & X and he strictly prefers B & X to A & X, and the person prefers A & X to A & X, and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X, and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X and the person prefers A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X to A & X

Let us provisionally say that a process is democratically legitimate if it exemplifies reasonableness or rationality, or comes close enough (this is here irrelevant). To exhibit faith that a process is democratically legitimate, or, in this case, that a process is sufficiently close to the ideal deliberative procedure, is to endorse an action over an alternative action that one would prefer if the process were not democratically legitimate.

The idea that participation in democratic deliberation requires faith that the process was governed by an ideal of public reason is much weaker than the norms governing speech acts. Even if *no* procedures by which policies are passed in fact exemplify, or come close to exemplifying, the norms of public reason, the measure of a democracy can be taken by the proportion of participants in its deliberations who have faith that the procedures exemplify those ideals (and hence act on that supposition).

However, the Deweyian concetion o nor guidance as aith is too robleatic to be adoted. The roble is that faith in democratic ideals leads us to blindness about their violations. To sily assue that olicies based on aeal to bias and secial interest were deocratically legitiate risks overlooking too any concrete instances o injustice. This is si ly too large a risk to take.

One ight also reject the deand or ideals to be ractically ossible in order to be useul. Even ractically iossible scientic ideals are nevertheless useul in science.56 However, this deense o olitical ideals is tendentious. Scientic ideals, as Kwae Anthony Aiah has argued in unublished work, are useul because the details ro which they abstract are uniortant to our overall icture o the hysical world. However, olitical ideals are not at all like this. The details ro which they abstract are concrete instances o social injustice. Scientic ideals abstract ro riction; olitical ideals abstract ro the existence o oressed inorities.57

Still, there are any ossible odels o nor guidance that are le oen. In the ace o the colexities we have discussed, erhas a reasonable way to adhere to ideal deliberative nors, or exale, the nor o objectivity, ay be to adot systematic openness to the possibility that one has been unknowingly swayed by bias. I so, the ark o a deocratic culture is one in which articiants in debates regularly check theselves or bias, and subject their own belies and unthinking use o language to the sae critical scrutiny as they do the belies and utterances o others. The question o the ractical ossibility o deliberative ideals then becoes the question o the ractical ossibility o such olicing. It is not just a atter o attending to our own discourse. Since whether or not discourse is roagandistic deends uon awed ideological belie, the ractical ossibility o deliberative ideals ultiately rests uon our caacity to be sensitive to the eects o awed ideologies on our own belie syste.

In the next two chaters, I turn to the toic o ideology. I will suggest that deocratically robleatic ideology is virtually inevitable in societies with substantial inequality. This suggests that the ractical ossibility o the deocratic ideals we have discussed deends uon whether or not huans are caable o itigating the eects o inequality.

## IDEOLOGY

Underining roaganda is a clai that is resented as e bodying a olitical ideal, but that is in the service o the kind o goal that tends to underine that ideal. What this eans is that the success o underining roaganda deends on two things. First, it deends on eole having belies that are resistant to the available evidence, the evidence that reveals the tension between goal and ideal. Secondly, since underining roaganda conceals a contradiction o sorts, the belies that are resistant to evidence ust theselves be awed in soe way.

In this chater, I will sell out a characteristic way in which belies are resistant to evidence. The hilosohical uzzle o ideological belie has always been to exlain how we could coe to have belies that are resistant to evidence in this way. It is the hilosohical uzzle at the heart o, or exale, David Hue's hilosohy. For instance, the hilosohical uzzle raised in "O Sketicis with Regard to the Senses" is not, as is soeties thought, external world sketicis. Hue does not view external world sketicis as a hilosohical uzzle. The hilosohical uzzle is rather that as soon as we reect uon our belie in external things, we realize that it is inconsistent with the available evidence. But we nevertheless continue to believe that external objects exist. The hilosohical uzzle Hue takes hisel to be addressing is this: why is our belie in external things resistant to rational revision in light o available evidence? The urose o "O Sketicis with Regard to the Senses" is to answer this question. Why we have a class o belies that are eculiarly resistant to evidence is one o hilosohy's traditional questions.

The cases that tend to interest hilosohers are ones in which the source o our inability to rationally revise a belie is soe kind o conusion or error. Thus, the ost uzzling cases o ideological belie are what I will call awed ideological belief. In the case o "O Sketicis with Regard to the Senses," the aws that exlain our ideological belie in external things lie in individual sychology. But in the cases o central interest to us in this book, they lie in society, in the or o social injustice. Hue devotes "O Sketicis with Regard to the Senses" to exlaining the echaniss by which a certain kind o awed individual sychology inevitably gives rise to awed ideological belie. I a engaged in a structurally siilar roject, excet y concern is with awed social structures, rather than awed sychology. The goal o the next two chaters is to exlain the echaniss by which awed social structures give rise to awed ideological belie.

David Hue's interest in "O Sketicis with Regard to the Senses" was to exlain awed ideological belies, the source o which lies in aws in our individual sychology. His exlanation or why we cannot revise what he regarded as the awed ideological belie that there are external things is that we cannot change the aws in our sychology that necessitate ossession o that belie. My ai is dierent. I will be concerned with awed ideological belies whose source is aws in society, that is, social arrangeents. In the cases o interest to us in this book, the reason why a belie is resistant to rational revision is because o structural eatures o the society in which the agent is located.

I will argue that societies with awed social structures tend to give rise to awed ideological belie, in a siilar (yet erhas less inevitable) way to the anner in which Hue takes our awed sychology to lead to what he thinks o as our awed ideological belie in external things. We are caable o setting this belie teorarily aside, according to Hue, when we exlicitly rationally reect uon its justication. But as soon as we return to ordinary lie, we sli back into believing in external things. In a siilar way, when we exlicitly rationally reect uon the awed ideological belies that are caused by living in a society with structural injustice, we oen reject the. But when we return to ordinary lie, we nevertheless sli back into the awed ideological belies.

A goal o this book is to rovide an arguent or equality by showing that one central cause o eective roaganda is inequalities, both aterial and olitical. Inequalities tend to result in awed ideology, which exlains the eectiveness o roaganda. To eliinate the kinds o awed ideologies that are articularly robleatic deocratically, we need to seek a society that ebodies equality at the structural level. My goal in this chater is to elucidate the sense o awed ideology that ediates between the acts o substantive inequality and the eectiveness o antideocratic roaganda.

There is a danger that ust be iediately addressed, which is that y clai will be taken as soewhat trivial. Suose that a awed ideology is one that is undaentally orally or olitically bad, and suose that inequality o any kind, even aterial inequality, is orally or olitically bad. Fro the ersective o the view that justice requires aterial equality, and a oral concetion o awed ideology, it is not surrising that the ideology o a aterially unequal society looks to be robleatically awed. And it is not surrising that a aterially unequal society will give rise to the ideology that a just society can tolerate substantial aterial inequality. Eisteic ractices that are partial, in the sense o biased toward the interests o one arty, lead to characteristic ailures o rationality in one's reasoning about what to do olitically. It is a ailiar oint about liberalis that it requires a division between the ersonal and the olitical. However, liberalis condens the billionaire when she seeks to aect ublic olicy in ways guided by the desire to advance her son's interests over the interests o others.1

I will exlain, in what ollows, how a certain kind o artiality underines the kind o deliberation we exect when eole are thinking about ublic olicy or everyone. One secial class that will interest us involves belies that are connected to one's identity, characteristically by legitimating it. We all have such belies, and not all o the are deocratically robleatic. They becoe deocratically robleatic when they revent us ro erceiving i ortant arts o reality, characteristically social reality. Here is a ossible exale o the kind o robleatic artiality in question; I do not clai it is a descrition o how things are. I considered in the introduction the ossibility that olitical arty aliation is a ethod to deceive citizens to i ort artiality that is a noral and healthy art o certain ractices, such as being a sorts an, into a real in which it is not aroriate, naely, olitical decisions. I so, then olitical arty aliation is illiberal. Belies that are connected to one's identity will be dicult to abandon. So it will be dicult to abandon the belies connected to one's identity as a olitical arty eber. But these will be olitically iortant belies, which will now be uch less resistant to rational revision. As long as a artial ersective is ket within its roer doain— or exale, sorts talk radio— it is not awed. But as soon as it is iorted into discourse and reasoning about the ublic good, it unctions as illiberal.

Running through this book is a detailed arguent or equality. Conditions o inequality tend to give rise to awed ideologies, which ake the kind o deagoguery that ierils deocracy articularly eective. One hal o this arguent is the account o roaganda I rovided in revious chaters. The other hal is the account o awed ideology I rovide here. My accounts o ideology and roaganda are indeendent, but they are utually suorting in the book's arguent or equality.

I began this chater by noting that the exlanation o ideological belie is the central roble in the works o the Scottish Enlightenent hilosoher David Hue. We have already discussed how it eatures in his ost aous contribution to eisteology; it is just as clearly at the heart o Hue's naturalistic account o religious belie. Hue argues that enthusias and suerstition are the result o "the intrusion onto the oration o our belies o hope and fear, resectively."2 Hue's account o suerstitious belie (under which he includes religious belie) is that "eotion leads to excessive credulity in judgent— an unwillingness to aend judgent in the light o reection."3 Suerstitious and enthusiastic belies are ideological, because they arise out o the assions, in articular hoe and ear, rather than reason. This is at the basis o Hue's naturalistic exlanation o religious belie.

It is natural to resent Hue's critique o religious belie as ocused solely on individual sychology as the locus o its origin: he is critiquing religious belie by arguing that it arises ro ear and suerstition, which are natural eatures o hu ans. But social ractice, in the or o custo and habit, clearly lays a role even in the ideologies he discussed. Aer all, it is religious social ractices that aintain religious belie. No doubt Hue recognized that awed ideologies can be maintained by social practices and do not just rest on flawed psychology.4

Our focus is on the puzzles raised by societies that tolerate large inequalities. Many such cases, though perhaps not all, are instances of social injustice. The classic source for the notion of ideology whose source is social injustice is the work of Karl Marx. There are distinct views of ideology in Karl Marx's classic paper "The German Ideology." The one that is most useful for our purposes is embodied in Marx's famous quotation from there: "The ideas of the ruling class are in every epoch the ruling ideas: i.e., the class which is the ruling *material* force of society, is at the same time its ruling intellectual force."5 On this view, the ideological beliefs are the ruling classes' selflegitimation, or self-justification, of the expectations that make up their ideologies. Both Hume and Marx emphasize troubling epistemological features of ideological belief that arises from flawed sources. But Marx focuses on certain ideologies in particular, which are among the ones that I will argue are most democratically worrisome.

Marx's focus in this passage from "The German Ideology" is on the ideology formed by "the ruling material force of society," the legitimation narrative they tell themselves about why they are deserving of their position. Here is the concept of ideology I favor, which is influenced principally by Sally Haslanger, but also by Tommie Shelby.6 The beliefs that are part of an ideology are the record of expectations of various goods built out of regularities of convention. They are the beliefs that unreflectively guide our path through the social world. In this sense, everyone in the world has an ideology, since everyone has a social world. Because our ideologies are guided by a desire to retain a sense of normalcy, especially when normalcy is pleasant, they characteristically lead to beliefs that are connected to one's positive self-image, in the way Marx describes. These problematic beliefs are characteristic examples, maybe the characteristic examples, of flawed ideology that has its source in flawed social structures.

Traditionally, hilosohers have soken o ideological belief, and I will as well. However, ocusing on belie as the nexus o ideology is dangerously isleading. The distinctive eature o ideological belie is that it is very dicult to rationally revise in light o counterevidence. This is the eature o belies that a theory o ideology is devoted to exlaining, a eature that is esecially troubling in the case o awed ideological belie, cases in which a bit o exlicit rational reection ay lead us to reject it in light o the evidence. In his nonolitical writings at least, Hue was concerned to exlain the echaniss by which awed sychology leads to awed ideological belie. But in the cases that interest us, the source o both the ideology and the aws is society. The danger o ocusing on ideological belief as what is to be exlained is that we will be teted into the view that there are two kinds o ental states that are intrinsically dierent qua ental states: the kind that is rationally revisable (noral belies) and the kind that is not (ideological belies). We ay even be led into thinking the latter are a dierent kind o ental state altogether. But there is no reason to think that this is so, in the kinds o cases that concern us.

One ain source o the unrevisability o certain belies is that they are connected to social practices. The belies are ones I need to have in order to reain in those ractices. Following Dan Kahan, I will argue that one central source o ideological belies is our social identities. We value our social identities. Social identities are constituted by the ractices and habits in which we engage; those we engage with are our counity. We ust at least act as i certain roositions are true in order to engage in those ractices. To abandon these belies is to abandon certain ractices and habits that constitute our social identity. To abandon these belies is thereore to abandon one's counity, to leave everyone with who you identiy behind. This is very dicult or an individual erson to envisage; usually they can only eror the exerient o setting belies aside that are so connected to their social identities when they exlicitly are asked to rationally reect uon the. But as soon as they sli back into ordinary lie, they reengage in the ractices that ake the who they are.

What this eans is that revision o awed ideological belie whose source is awed social structure is very hard, erhas alost as hard as revising awed ideological belie the source o which is awed sychology. I this is right, then it's not correct to try to solve the uzzle o why even obviously awed ideological belie is hard to revise by looking just at intrinsic eatures o the belies theselves, or even the overall ental structure o individuals. Many ideological belies "look entally" just like nonideological belies. The reason individuals are loathe to abandon the is that they don't like to leave their riends behind.

Because o this, I a sketical about the search or a sychological strategy individuals can use to "rotect theselves" ro robleatic ideological belie on a case- by- case basis. The distinctive eature o ideological belie oen arises ro being ebedded in a ractice together with eole like you, your riends, and aily. What is needed to eliinate roble atic ideological belie is to change the ractice o a large grou o eole siultaneously over tie, to alter a social identity any eole share. It would be hard to see how this would work by assigning to individuals individual sychological curatives to eloy.

It should now be clear that while I theorize with a category o ideological belie, and deend this choice in what iediately ollows, this does not ean that I think that being ideological is an intrinsic roerty o ental states (though I will argue that there are certain interesting, sel- reinorcing ental echaniss ilicated in the aintenance o such belies). In sketching y account o what akes a belie ideological, I will ehasize that any o the interesting roerties that a theory o ideology ust exlain require looking at ractices. As a result, on y account, it is an accidental and not an intrinsic eature o ideological belies that they are ideological.

The hilosoher Taar Gendler has argued that uch o the hilosohical role layed by ideological belie is better layed by a notion she introduces called "alie." In her work, Gendler introduces novel, everyday, nonolitical exales o the ost striking eature o ideological belie, naely, its uzzling resistance to rational revision. For instance, in "Alie and Belie," Gendler begins by describing a transarent walkway over the Grand Canyon, consisting o any layers o glass that ake it coletely sae, and obviously so. Gendler oints out that our knowledge that the walkway is coletely sae does nothing to dislodge an attitude that sees to control our behavior as the belie that it is dangerous to walk on the walkway would. Taking it as obvious that in such a case we believe that the walkway is sae, and that we would not exlicitly hold contradictory belies, Gendler argues at length that we should recognize a distinct category o ental state that she calls alief. The erson standing on the glass skywalk believes that the walkway is sae (i she did not, she would not risk her lie by voluntarily erching there), but her behavior is also governed by a contrary alie: that she is in ortal eril. No atter how convincingly she rehearses to hersel that she is in a situation o colete saety, her behavior will be teered by her disavowed alie.

The distinctive and controversial roerty o ideological belie is its resistance to rational revision. Sketics about the theory o ideology tend to hold that those otivated to theorize about belie- like ental states that are rationally resistant to revision just have a hard tie acceting that belies they reject have large indeendent lausibility. Such sketicis about ideology aces the threat o incoherence; aer all, the sketicis usually takes the or o the charge that theorists o ideology are incaable o rationally evaluating belies that have large indeendent lausibility. And it is the theory o ideology that studies just this. Though standard versions o ske ticis about the roject o ideology critique are incoherent in just this way, there is no denying that such sketicis is widesread. What Gendler's work shows is that the sketicis is islaced, at the level o uncontroversial exale. There are entirely undane exales o ental states that have the ost controversial eature o ideological belie. In the ace o the ubiquity o such exales, there is no reaining case or sketicis.

Gendler's work is a rearkable contribution to the theory o ideology; it should settle a lengthy hilosohical debate about the resuositions o that theory. It is also a rich source o insight into the structure o ideology, uon which I draw in the next two chaters.7 But I disagree with the otivations or her novel ontology o the ental. In articular, y concern is with Gendler's aeal to "iediate rational revisability" as a criterion or a ental state to be a belie, in her arguent that alie is distinct ro belie. On y concetion, a belie is ideological insoar as it is connected with one's various identities. And connection with, or exale, one's social identity coes in degree. It is ossible but hard to resist the conclusion that whether or not a belie is ideological also deends uon degree. Soe belies are ore ideological than others: the ones that are ore closely connected to one o the agent's identities. That ideology coes in degree is hard to air with a binary oosition between rationally revisable states and nonrationally revisable states.

More iortantly, the concetion o ideological belie I will deend sits uneasily with the thought that the ailure to rationally revise in light o available evidence is a direct easure o whether or not a state is a belie. It is natural to think that soe belies are connected to our identity and others aren't; this or e is the source o the incaacity or rational revision, rather than the ailure to be a belie. Belies that are connected to one's identity, which one shares with others, will be hard to revise one by one, because it is hard sily to abandon one's identity.

In her rejoinder to the kind o osition I deend, Gendler draws attention to its aarent incoatibility with a nor governing belie: "whatever belie is— it is noratively governed by the ollowing constraint: belie ais to 'track truth' in the sense that belie is subject to iediate revision in the ace o changes in our all- things- considered evidence."8 Gendler here argues ro the reise that the norative ideal or belie is the odal notion o tracking the truth to the conclusion that the belie ust have the roerty o being i ediately revised, were the available evidence to suggest it to be alse. I will not question the validity o the inerence. My objection is rather to her assution that aong the norative ideals o belie (erhas derivatively, via knowledge) is the roerty o tracking the truth.

There are any ways to sell out the notion o tracking the truth. One way involves what eisteologists call sensitivity: i one believes that , then were to be alse, one would no longer believe that . It is widely agreed that sensitivity is not a lausible norative ideal or belie; there are too any counterexales. But there is a weaker way to construe truthtracking: i one believes that , then were one to be resented with rationally coelling evidence against , one would revise one's belie. Even this weaker version aces counterexa les. As Tiothy Williason has coellingly argued, there are counterexales to any straightorward counteractual analysis o an eisteic ideal.9 Following Williason, I will argue that there is a ore coelling alternative icture o the norative ideals or belie that does not involve truth- tracking.

Let us rst suose the antecedently lausible view that knowledge is the norative ideal or belie: belie ais at knowledge.10 What are the conditions under which a belie is knowledge? One alternative to sensitivity that has arisen in analytic eisteology is the notion o saety, develoed in dierent ways ost roinently by Ernest Sosa and Tiothy Williason.11 Because I a convinced by Williason that there is a general worry with counteractual analyses, I will adot his exosition o the saety condition on knowledge.

One orulation o Williason's saety condition is "i one knows, then one could not easily have been wrong in a siilar case."12 Here is the kind o exale that otivates it. Suose that I a in a stadiu with exactly 10,387 eole. I can coe to know by looking that there are ore than 1,000 eole. I can coe to know by looking that there are less than 100,000 eo le. But I cannot coe to know by looking that there are exactly 10,387 eole. Even i I guess correctly, I could have easily been wrong in a siilar case (where there were, or exale, 10,388 eole). We do not need to investigate the details o this debate in eisteology to see that the view that knowledge is the ai o belie, together with the saety condition on knowledge, does not entail that iediate rational revisability is a norative ideal o belie. My identity ight be connected to social ractices that involve belies that could not have been easily wrong in siilar cases, even i I ail to revise these belies in suciently dissimilar cases. Knowledge is in the rst instance about being connected to the world in the right way. It is not in the rst instance about eatures o y underlying eisteic character that would be revealed in distant counter actual situations. Iediate rational revisability ay be art o having a good overall eisteic character. But it is not a norative ideal o belie.

The central uzzle o the theory o ideology is this: why is our behavior so oen guided by states that do not see to be sensitive to available evidence? There is uch ore to say on either side o the debate that Gendler's work has initiated on the ontology o ideology. But erhas I have said enough here to indicate why I reain with tradition in theorizing about the uzzle riarily in ters o the category o ideological belief.

Gendler's theoretical ocus is on the ental ontology o the theory o ideology; she argues that in order to exlain ideology's distinctive eatures, we need a new ental category. Other theorists o ideology lace their ontological ocus elsewhere, in social ontology. Theorists such as Althusser, Bourdieu, and Haslanger urge that ideologies include social practices, which should be theorized indeendently o the ental states o agents embedded within them.13 A theory of ideology is there to explain certain puzzling behavior: in the most interesting cases, behavior that seems to run contrary to what is rational, given what the obviously available evidence strongly suggests or even demonstrates. These theorists argue that a theory of ideology that does not pay substantial theoretical attention to social structures ignores central issues of explanatory concern.

A theory of ideology that restricts its explanations to the mental states of members of society does not just risk missing the *source* of the most worrisome kinds of ideology, which arise from unjust social structures, for example, the relations of oppression described by Haslanger.14 It also risks analyzing the problematic feature of ideological beliefs in terms of intrinsic features of mental states. But it is still possible to theorize with a category of ideological belief. It is just necessary to insist that notions like *resistance to rational revision*, as well as the deficiencies that attend such resistance in conditions of social injustice, should not be expected to be explained on the level of intrinsic features of mental states. This is a feature of the account to follow, which is framed in terms of an account of ideological belief.

I am going to argue that there are certain ideological beliefs that are particularly problematic democratically. I am not the first to identify these as the most democratically problematic. In book 5, part 2, of *The Politics*, Aristotle provides "the universal and chief cause" of revolution; in fact, Aristotle provides two chief causes. The first is "the desire of equality, when men think that they are equal to others who have more than themselves." The second is "the desire of inequality and superiority, when conceiving themselves to be superior they think that they have not more but the same or less than their inferiors." Aristotle notes that these are "pretensions which may and may not be just." In the case of *unjust* revolutions, either "[i]nferiors revolt in order that they may be equal," or "equals that they may be superior." Aristotle's two "universal and chief" causes of unjust revolutions are the two examples of democratically robleatic awed ideological belie I will discuss. They are recisely the awed ideological belies that arise in conditions o substantive inequality (as Aristotle recognizes). The argu ent o this book is not new.

One o Aristotle's chie causes o injustice requires believing that huans are undaentally naturally unequal. Su ose this is so, and consider a grou o eole who are not as eritorious or soe reason. As a result, they end u without resources. The social scrits that guide the through lie involve dicult searches or ood and housing. They are unwilling to blae their ailures on theselves, as this would involve iugning their sel- resect. They coe then to blae their redicaent not on theselves, but on an unjust distribution o resources in society. They then develo the awed ideological belie that those who have gained ortunes have done so illicitly.

I there were to be a society in which the wealthy have gained their ortunes legitiately, those who acquire the awed ideological belie that the wealthy have gained their ortunes illicitly will be hard- ressed to abandon it, even in the ace o clear evidence to the contrary. For exale, when resented with evidence that everyone can in act succeed in the society, i they ake the correct choices, they will reject the evidence as unconvincing. Giving it u will orce the to adit to theselves that their choices were oor and their struggles their own ault. They would be lled with jealousy and anger at the ruling classes, jealousy that is dicult to justiy but no less real or that.

Marx's exale o an ideology is o the second variety: the sel- legitiations o those who control the aterial goods o society. This is a awed ideology, in the sense I will elucidate. The exale I will give is one in which the sel- legitiation is clearly unjust. Consider a roserous aily in the Antebellu Aerican South who lives on a lantation that has rovided a high level o incoe or the aily or several generations. The aily owns slaves who aintain and cultivate their lantation, and slaves who work in the house doing doestic chores. The ebers o the aily have grown u with the ex ectation that slaves will cook their eals or the, slaves will clean the house and raise the children, and slaves will work on the lantation at no cost to rovide or their well- being, as well as the well- being o uture generations o that aily.

I will take the ideology o this aily to be the belies they have that guide the through their social lives, as well as the concets they use to structure reality around the. The belies that are art o this ideology are belies like (i) the belie that slaves will cook the dinner, (ii) the belie that slaves will clean the house, and (iii) the belie that slaves will work in the eld and collect the cotton that is sold on the arket or the aily's gain. These are belies that constitute the ideology o this aily. They are, as Haslanger writes, "reresentations o social lie that serve in soe way to undergird social ractices."15 It is because o the exectation o a dinner without labor that they arrive at the table without rst cooking in the kitchen. It is because o the belie that slaves should do the eldwork that they send the day in the house rather than laboring in the hot sun. These belies are the ones that exlain their everyday behavior. This is what I will rovisionally at least take to be their ideology.

Now that we have a sense o the ideology o this articular aily, I will exlain how their ideology can be exected to include, and give rise to, belies that will revent the ro gaining knowledge about their social world.

The aily has, or several generations, relied on the work o slaves to create and aintain their ortune, as well as their daily existence. I the institution o slavery is unjust, then their wealth was not roerly obtained. Furtherore, their ancestors, including their own arents, were the ones who built and aintained that wealth by exloiting the institution o slavery. So i slavery is deely unjust, then their own iediate ancestors were eretrators o great wrongs. I slavery is deely unjust, then the coortable aristocratic anner o living to which they are accustoed, relying as it does on the institution o slavery, is unjustiable.

It is very dicult to view one's own arents as evil. It is also dicult to contelate giving u luxuries that one has sent one's lie enjoying. It is thereore natural to exect the e bers o the lantation aily, by virtue o the ideology they have, to or belies that rotect the against considering the hyothesis that slavery is an unjust institution. One ight ex ect the ideology to lead the ebers o the lantation a ily to believe that Blacks are inherently lazy and require the institution o slavery to instill in the a work ethic that they naturally lack. One ight exect their ideology to lead the to believe that Blacks, by virtue o culture or genetics, are not caable o sel- governance. One ight exect the to believe that Blacks are inherently violent and dangerous and require harsh unishent and control to kee the ro osing a threat to civil society. The ideology o the aily can be considered to be the social ractices they engage in, together with the belies that guide their behavior in these ractices. These include their ordinary daily exectations about their social lie: exectations o a clean house and ood without labor, o ree labor or their nancial gain, and so on. These exectations lead the to adot a justication or their exectations. It is justied to ex ect Black slaves to reare one's dinner, clean the house, raise the children, and labor in the hot sun or ree, because Blacks are lazy, incaable o sel- governent, and a danger to civil society. The institution o slavery is good or society, and good or the Black slaves as well. The hilosoher Christoher J. Lebron calls these exectations legitimizing myths; they have iortance as "the eans by which ascendant grous assign to theselves ositive social value while ortraying others . . . as justiably ossessing lower standing. Without legitiizing yths, hierarchy is erely stratication. With legitiizing yths, hierarchy becoes grounded in sueriority and ineriority and oral distinctions becoe laden with nors."16

The notion o a stereotye, as develoed by Walter Li ann in Public Opinion, is his surrogate or an ideology, in the sense I have described. As he there writes:

[Stereotyes] are an ordered, ore or less consistent icture o the world, to which our habits, our tastes, our caacities, our coorts and our hoes have adjusted theselves. They ay not be a colete icture o the world, but they are a icture o a ossible world to which we are adated. In that world eole and things have their well- known laces, and do certain exected things. We eel at hoe there. We t in. We are ebers. We know the way around. There we nd the char o the ailiar, the noral, the deendable; its grooves and shaes are where we are accustoed to nd the. And though we have abandoned uch that ight have teted us beore we creased ourselves into that ould, once we are rly in, it ts as snugly as an old shoe.

No wonder, then, that any disturbance o the stereotyes sees like an attack uon the oundations o the universe. It is an attack uon the oundations o our universe, and, where big things are at stake, we do not readily adit that there is any distinction between our universe and the universe. A world which turns out to be one in which those we honor are unworthy, and those we desise are noble, is nerve- racking.

Liann here is clear that stereotyes are the social scrits that guide us through the world, ake sense o it, and legiti ate our actions within it.17 He also rovides an exlanation o why stereotyes are resistant to rational revision: it is eotionally unsettling, erhas in a distinctive way, to abandon the. They are connected to our identity. Return to the exa le o the slave- owning lantation aily in the Antebellu

Aerican South. Mebers o the aily have certain belies, which arise ro self- interest. They arise ro the aily's desire to aintain their aristocratic liestyle, without oral condenation o theselves or their ancestors. Insoar as their identity is connected to their liestyle, the belies are also connected to their identity. They suort that identity.

Susan Stebbing useully describes ideological belies as cherished beliefs. 18 A cherished belie, as she writes, is a belie that "we want to retain; it is a leasant belie to hold." She warns that "[w]e have to be on our guard against suosing that a belie that is cherished could not be alse because it would be so dreadul i it were." This echoes Liann's descrition. The disturbance o a stereotye sees like "an attack uon the oundations o the universe." Stebbing's vocabulary, "cherished belie," reects a eature that Liann's "stereotye" lacks. A cherished belie is one that we will be reluctant to give up. Stebbing's vocabulary brings out an iortant eature o ideological belie; it is one that is resistant in a distinctive way to rational revision. Both Liann's and Stebbing's discussions bring out the connection between ideological belie and identity; Stebbing's vocabulary additionally calls attention to the act that belies that are connected to our identity will be eotionally dear to us in ways that belies unconnected to our identity are not. As a consequence, they will not be easy to abandon.

Stebbing's ter "cherished" suggests a kind o individualis about ideological belie: that an individual's emotional attachment to a belie is what akes it dicult to rationally revise. While Stebbing's vocabulary and descrition o the henoenon could islead as to its source, it is nevertheless deely insightul. There are dierent ways in which an ideology can be robleatic. Ideologies can, or exale, be orally or olitically robleatic. However, y ocus is on eisteologically robleatic eatures o certain ideologies.

But desite its ossession o this aarent eisteic deect, we should not think o ideological belie as a ortiori eiste ologically deective. It is teting thereore to think that when one singles out a subclass o ideological belie as awed, the aw ust be oral or olitical. Perhas there are just the eisteic deects associated with all o ideology, and then oral and olitical deects? I so, this would be a roble or the roject o y book. The arguent o y book is that there are certain ideologies that becoe olitically robleatic in a liberal deocracy. These ideologies have negative eisteic eatures that other ideologies lack. What are these negative eisteic eatures?

I have argued that not all ideologies are eisteologically disabling. Flawed ideologies are, however, eisteologically disabling; this is why they are awed. Flawed ideologies revent us ro gaining knowledge about eatures o reality, including social reality. Such ideologies are eisteologically disabling about the doain o deocratic decision aking. Let's consider soe exales o ideologies that are awed, in the sense that they involve alse ideological belies that revent us ro gaining knowledge about otherwise obvious eatures o reality.

This will, or exale, close o French eole ro being sources o inoration, which will urther revent y acquiring evidence that it is alse. Here is another exale o the additional eisteically robleatic eatures o ideologies that are awed, in the sense I a characterizing. It is easy to iagine that having this identity ay block e ro acquiring knowledge and contribute to alse belie. Just as a belie can be ideological in virtue o structural eatures o society that inhibit its revision, so too can an ideology be awed, because o awed structural eatures o society that inhibit the rational revision o reexisting alse belie, to reserve a desirable situation or a rivileged grou. White children in the South were raised with the belie ro birth. Debate about it was revented. I debate with others is revented, it is hard to revise a belie. I a arguing that there is a secic subclass o ideologies that have additional eisteological aws, and so warrant being considered awed. Perhas all ideologies have varying degrees o these additional aws; i so, then all ideologies are at least soewhat awed. An ideology, in the sense I use, is sily a social "scrit" that governs one's exectations, nor ative and ractical. O course, considered sily as nonideological alse belies, the belies in question would also be barriers to the acquisition o knowledge. But the reason awed ideologies are so robleatic is that they are ideologies that include belies that are barriers to the acquisition o iortant knowledge. Ordinary alse belies are revisable in ways that ideological alse belies are not. It is the cobination o rational unrevisability with additional eisteic deects that akes awed ideological belie eisteologically robleatic.

I have shown why ideological belie is hard to rationally revise. An ideological belie is connected to identity and/or sel- interest, and tends to contribute to its own aintenance. I have argued that there are certain cases in which the belies in an ideology are articularly eisteologically robleatic, or exale when they seal us o ro good testiony. But there are other ways or an ideology to be awed; because o the concets it contains (or ails to contain).

In her essay "The Idea o Perection," the hilosoher Iris Murdoch considers the exale o a other- in- law, who she calls M, who disaroves o her son's arriage to D.22 She regards her daughter- in- law D as "lacking in dignity and rene ent," "noisy," and "vulgar." As tie asses, M reconsiders her judgent. M decides to think about whether these judgents issue ro M's being "old ashioned and conventional," "rejudiced and narrow inded," or "snobbish" and "jealous." She decides to rethink her judgent, eventually realizing that D is "not vulgar, but rereshingly sile," "not undignied but sontaneous." What has haened to allow M to ove ro a alse icture o reality, one resistant to evidence, to one that accurately reects the acts?

Murdoch calls our attention to the role layed in this change o view by the "norative- descritive words," such as "vulgar" and "sontaneous."23 Murdoch notes that it is characteristic o norative descritive words to belong to "sets or atterns without an areciation o which they cannot be understood."24 Words like "vulgar" exress concets that belong to a concetual schee— a set or attern o concets— that orders the social world in a certain way. M cae to realize that this concetual schee was "old ashioned and conventional." She also cae to realize that her attachent to this concetual schee was otivated by jealousy (a or o sel- interest).

M's change to a ore accurate way o erceiving the social world is the result o her abandonent o a concetual schee that involves the concets vulgar, common, and undigni ed. These concets belong to a hierarchical and robleatic concetion o the social world. Possession o this concetual schee revented M ro acquiring knowledge about it. In its lace, M adots a concetual schee, a attern o concets, including concets such as spontaneity and joy, which do not resuose robleatic social hierarchies. Adotion o this new concetual schee enables M to see that what she regarded as vulgar is in act a sign o being sontaneous, what she regarded as "noisy" and breaking the olite cal a decent household should have was in act joyousness. M thus cae to see her social world ore accurately.

What Murdoch is arguing is that just as sel- interest can lead us to hold and retain beliefs in the ace o contradictory evidence, sel- interest— in this case in the or o jealousy— can also lead us to retain certain conceptual schemes, ones that involve norative concets that islead us about our social world. M solved her roble, on Murdoch's view, by relacing sel- interest with a desire or justice or love, which leads to the "substitution o one set o norative eithets or another."25 In short, just as social identity and sel- interest can lead us to hold and retain awed belies, sel- interest can lead us to adot a set o concets that revent us ro gaining knowledge about the social world. Murdoch urges us to relace sel- interest with love in our relation to others. Murdoch's discussion clearly brings out that the sources o awed ideological belie, such as sel- interest, are sources o awed concets, as well as awed ideological belies.

It is not just that a set o concets, a concetual schee, can be awed because the concets isreresent social reality, or exale, by iuting to it a ctional hierarchy o worth between eole. As Murdoch's discussion brings out, a attern o concets can be awed because it lacks some concepts entirely. Miranda Fricker has recently rovided a striking exale o the henoenon by which a concetual schee lacks a crucial concet. It is ro a eoir o the woen's liberation oveent. On May 13, 2013, the Chinese governent issued a secret docuent, entitled "A Reort about the Current Situation o Ideology," to university adinistrations. The reort was leaked by Gao Yu and ublished in the Geran agazine Der Spiegel in August 2013. The docuent deands that university roessors rerain ro discussing seven toics. These exales suggest a view according to which negatively rivileged grous are hindered ro acting in their own sel- interest by their ailure to have the right concetual schee, a schee that would isolate and exlain the oressive social contexts in which they nd theselves. Sel- interest or jealousy can otivate one to adot and retain a articular concetual schee. It is erhas even easier to see how concets can becoe ideological by being connected to one's identity. Iris Murdoch's discussion o M's robleatic conce tual schee, containing concets such as vulgar and noisy as "descritive- norative concets" o ersons, shows that concepts can be awed.
