One o the ost aous critiques o a concet is the Black Aerican intellectual Sojourner Truth's discussion o the concet o woman, in her seech "Ain't I a Woan?," delivered to the 1851 Woan's Convention, in Akron, Ohio:

That an over there says that woen need to be heled into carriages, and lied over ditches, and to have the best lace everywhere. Nobody ever hels e into carriages, or over ud- uddles, or gives e any best lace! And ain't I a woan? Look at e! Look at y ar! I have loughed and lanted, and gathered into barns, and no an could head e! And ain't I a woan? I could work as uch and eat as uch as a an— when I could get it— and bear the lash as well! And ain't I a woan? I have borne thirteen children, and seen ost all sold o to slavery, and when I cried out with y other's grie, none but Jesus heard e! And ain't I a woan?

Sojourner Truth here calls attention to the act that the concet o a woan is suosed to be connected to einine hellessness. However, she resents hersel as an exale o a woan who does not eet any o these criteria, desite undoubtedly exeriencing the worst travails woen, and only woen, can exerience, a "other's grie." Sojourner Truth in her aous seech is arguing that the concet o woman is awed. Let's exlore ways in which concets can be awed in this way by reventing the acquisition o knowledge about the social world.

A concet is a way of thinking o a roerty. For exale, the concet exressed by "table" is a way o thinking o the ro erty o being a table; the concet o water is a way o thinking o the roerty o H2 O. Let us say that a concet is ety i there is no roerty it denotes. For silicity, I shall stiulate that a roerty only exists i there are things that did or do all under it. The concet o being a unicorn is ety, since there are no unicorns, nor have there ever been unicorns.28 In contrast, the concet o a table is not ety, nor is the concet o water.

I shall use the term "proposition" for the content of a belief. So, when someone believes that Tyrone is about to become CEO, she stands in the belief relation to a proposition, the proposition that Tyrone is about to become CEO. We will take propositions to be built out of *concepts*, rather than the properties or objects they denote. Someone can believe that unicorns have horns, despite the nonexistence of the property of being a unicorn, because the proposition that unicorns have horns is constituted out of concepts, rather than properties.

To explain one model of flawed concepts along these lines, it is helpful to use one theory of concepts, the *inferential role* theory of concepts. According to the inferential role theory of concepts, one possesses a concept if and only if one is disposed to make certain characteristic inferences. The property denoted by a concept is the property that makes those inferences valid.

Here is an example of an inferential role treatment of the concept of conjunction, that is, the concept expressed by "and." According to the inferential role theorist, the concept expressed by "and" is the concept of a property that licenses inferences from the proposition that P and Q to the proposition that P, the inference from the proposition that P and Q to the proposition that Q, and from the proposition that P and Q to the proposition that P and the proposition that Q. The concept of conjunction denotes a *truth-function*, a function from truth-values to truth-values, characteristically supplied by a truth table. The truth-function denoted by the concept of conjunction is the one that makes these inferences valid. What would an account of flawed concepts look like on the inferential model of concepts?

Following Sojourner Truth's famous analysis, let's suppose hypothetically that the concept expressed by "lady" licenses the following inferences: the inference from "x is a well-dressed white woman" to "x is a lady," and the inference from "x is a lady" to "x is submissive and in need of care." This concept is flawed in two ways. First, it is flawed because it leads to false belies. Well- dressed white woen are not in general subissive and in need o care. Second, it is awed because such concets are ety. The inerences it licenses are not valid. There ore, a ortiori, there is no roerty in the world that akes these inerences valid. So the concet exressed by "lady," as I have dened it, is empty, in the sense that it does not denote a roerty. One odel o an ideologically awed concet is a concet that both is ety and leads to alse belies o a certain sort.

According to the inerential role account o awed concets I have just sketched, concets that are awed are ety o content. For exale, the ter "lady" that was stiulated to license the inerences described does not denote any ro erty. In chater 4, I argued that there are words that exress concets that have content, yet that also in soe sense exress awed concets. This is necessary to treat roaganda, which oen involves the use o ordinary ters that have been associated with awed concets or belies.

We can also seak o terms being awed. The discussion in chater 4 revealed another ossible account o how our ters ay exress awed contents, one that does not involve the having ety contents. Perhas "lady" denotes the roerty o being gendered eale, but is associated with another kind o awed content, one that licenses the inerence that anything that is gendered eale has roerties such as being subissive and in need o care. On this account, "lady" would license the inerence, o a articular woan, that she is subissive and in need o care. But "lady" would still denote a noral content, naely, ersons who are gendered eale.

Here is a third way a concet can be awed. Let us say that a concet's atness is deterined by whether or not its e loyent acilitates or iedes the acquisition o knowledge (even i indirectly, via eotional associations). The ore a concet iedes one's acquisition o knowledge, the less at it is. An exlanation o what it is or a concet to ail to be at would exlain one way in which awed ideologies lead to awed ideological belies. We have seen one odel o ailure o atness, naely, when the concet is ety and leads to ideologically awed belie. But it is ossible or a concet to ail to be at, even i it does denote a roerty.

The nineteenth- and twentieth- century Geran logician Gottlob Frege is best thought o as an archrationalist. His hilosohical goal is to show that analytic judgent alone, denitions and logic, could lead to ruitul extensions o our knowledge. In sketching out his rationalist rogra o showing how denitions could lead to genuine extensions o knowledge, Frege writes o "the really ruitul denitions in atheatics," such as "that o the continuity o a unction":

What we nd in these is not a sile list o characteristics; every eleent in the denition is intiately, I ight alost say organically, connected with the others. A geoetrical illustration will ake the distinction clear to intuition. I we reresent the concets (or their extensions) by gures or areas in a lane, then concet dened by a sile list o characteristics corresonds to the area coon to all the areas reresenting the dening characteristics; it is enclosed in segents o their boundary lines. With a denition like this, thereore, what we do— in ters o our illustration— is to use the lines already given in a new say or the urose o dearcating an area. Nothing essentially new, however, eerges in the rocess. But the ore ruitul tye o de nition is a atter o drawing boundary lines that were not reviously given at all. What we shall be able to iner ro it, cannot be insected in advance; here, we are not sily taking out o the box again what we have just ut in.29

Frege here seaks o concets that aid us in benecial ways, which aid our reasoning by heling us to see atterns that we could not otherwise see. But one can then easily see the ossibility o denitions o concets that ool us into thinking that there are atterns that are not there. To take an exale ro atheatics, the concet x is divisible only by 1 and 17 is not a articularly ruitul concet. I we thought it was athe atically central, we would be conused about the actual atterns o atheatics. The ruitul nuber- theoretical concet is x is divisible only by 1 and x, that is, the concet o a rie nuber.30

Frege's discussion o ruitul concets shows us that concets ay islead us about the structure o reality. We can iagine a denition that is not ruitul in this sense, that occludes, rather than illuinates, the "deendence o roositions" on one another. This is one way in which a concet can be awed. I an ideology contains such concets, it will revent the rational acquisition and revision o belie, in uch the sae way as a atheatical ideology containing the concet x is divisible only by 1 and 17 and not the concet o a rie nuber. Iris Murdoch's discussion o M's concetual schee, involving concets such as "vulgar" and "rened," is intended to rovide a case in which certain concetual schees are obstacles to the rational acquisition and revision o belie about the social world.

Given the concetion o awed ideology I have sketched, and their yriad sources, it is natural to exect that no actual huan ideology is coletely nonawed. As theorists o ideology ro Liann to Gendler ehasize, it is art o natural unctioning to have stereotyes that allow us to act raidly, given liitations o working eory. These stereotyes will hel us, but they will also lead us into cognitive error. Siilarly, it is hard to iagine an actual huan ideology that does not contain soe awed concets. We all have awed ideologies, that is, belies or concets that hinder us ro gaining knowledge in the noral way in soe doain.

We are now in a osition to characterize what Manred Stanley calls the ideology of technicism. 31 This is an ideology that excludes (or exale) narrative clais about ersonal exerience as reasons or action or belie. The ideology o technicis is one that restricts genuine reasons in the ublic shere to those whose contents contain only scientic or quantitative concepts. The ideology of technicism does not contain concepts for personal experience; it therefore consigns them, in Stanley's words, to the status of mere "convenient rhetoric."

Manfred Stanley argues that the ideology of technicism undermines democracy, by undermining the autonomy of those who are unfamiliar with the technicist concepts. The ideology of technicism makes citizens feel unqualified to participate democratically in the formation of the laws that govern their behavior. Patricia Hill Collins and Khalil Muhammad have argued that the ideology of technicism is what underlies discounting personal narratives as explanations of patterns of statistics that paint minority groups in an unflattering light. If these theorists are right, the technicist conceptual scheme is typically adopted as an *ideology*. It is employed by those in power to disenfranchise and subordinate those who are not in power, and hence is connected to a distinct social identity, the identity of ruling elites.

Patricia Hill Collins describes the ideology of technicism as the conceptual scheme of "Eurocentric Knowledge Validation Processes." Collins's choice of vocabulary echoes the Senegalese philosopher Léopold Sédar Senghor, who contrasts the European tradition of reason, with the African tradition, as follows:

However paradoxical it may seem, the vital force of the Negro African, his surrender to the object, is animated by reason. Let us understand each other clearly; it is not the *reasoning-eye* of Europe, it is the *reason of the touch*, better still, the *reasoning-embrace*, the sympathetic reason, more closely related to the Greek logos than to the Latin ratio.... At any rate, Negro-African speech does not mold the object into rigid categories and concepts without touching it; it polishes things and restores their original color, with their texture, sound and perfume; it perforates them with its luminous rays to reach the essential surreality in its innate humidity—it would be more accurate to speak of subreality.

Euroean reasoning is analytical, discursive by utilization; Negro- Arican reasoning is intuitive by articiation.33

As we have seen, theorists such as Stanley, Collins, and Muha ad ake a urther clai. They argue that the eloyent o such a radically ioverished concetual schee as the technicist one is not just an accident o history. The idea that ublic reason should be constrained to technocratic concets is there or a secic urose: to serve the interests o the ruling elite. In the ace o Sauel Huntington's aeal to eloy the language o exertise ore widely as a echanis to deal with the "excesses o deocracy," it is dicult to reject this urther clai. I so, then the technocratic concetual schee is very oen ut to ideological uroses, in the sense o ideology I have laid out in this chater. It is a awed ideology, because, by denying us access to narrative accounts and ersonal testiony, it blocks us ro an understanding o the huan suering that exlains the statistics, even when the statistics are actually correct.

Since Aristotle hilosohers have held that there are soe articularly iortant awed ideologies that threaten deocracy. A ersistent worry about deocracy is that these de ocratically robleatic ideologies see inevitable, and so deocracy will invariably be threatened by their existence. What I have atteted in this book is to develo this ancient worry about deocracy in detail. My account o ersuasion and aniulation, that is, y account o roaganda, is that it deends or its eectiveness uon the existence o awed ideology. Any identity that is connected to the belie that a grou o one's ellow citizens does not have a ersective worth taking into account will ake unreasonable olicy aear reasonable. Other kinds o awed ideologies will ake irrational roosals aear rational. Given that there will be the sorts o awed ideologies Aristotle discusses, in the seeingly inevitable situation o substantive inequality even in deocracies, one can see why this has been regarded as an existential roble or deocracy since the Ancient Greeks.

It ay be easy to see how various eatures o society ay lead soeone to acquire a legitiation yth that revents the ro recognizing what is really haening, or to ail to acquire the right concets to think about reality. But it ight be thought that perceptual belief is sae ro this rocess. Perhas the ercetual concets are sile enough, and given a direct enough relation between exerience and belie, awed ideology cannot aect ercetual belie. I so, by restraining ourselves to a oundation o ercetual belie, we can avoid the kind o cognitive errors that result ro awed ideology.

However, recent work in hilosohy o ind and sychology has called into question the idea that ercetual belie is isolated ro cognitive error due to bias and rejudice. Our ercetual echaniss theselves can islead us by delivering isleading inoration, resulting in systeatically awed ercetual belie. Our ercetual aculties are now, as is widely agreed, aected by background belies. There is a relatively uncontroversial sense in which there is "cognitive enetration" o background belie on ercetion. As a result, ercetion itsel can be a source o awed ideological belie.

There are various versions o this thesis, to be distinguished below. But all versions are troublesoe. We do think o erce tion as roviding us with the "acts," uon which we build our theories. But i our ercetual echaniss are theselves a ected by bias, then we are at risk o aealing to biased echaniss to build our theories o the world, yet naturally assue that these echaniss are not biased. What results ro such a situation are biased belies that are assued to be objective.

The notion that ercetual belie is aected by ideology, and can thereby serve to reinorce that very ideology, even when it is awed, is ound quite exlicitly in Walter Liann's introduction o the notion o stereotyes in Public Opinion. There, he writes, about Aristotle's descrition o slaves:

This is the erect stereotye. Its hallark is that it recedes the use o reason; is a or o ercetion, ioses a certain character on the data o our senses beore the data reach the intelligence. The stereotye is like the lavender window- anes on Beacon Street, like the door- keeer at a costue ball who judges whether the guest has an aro riate asquerade. There is nothing so obdurate to education or to criticis as the stereotye. It stas itsel uon the evidence in the very act o securing the evidence.

Liann asserts here that stereotyes aect ercetual belies by aecting the route ro ercetion to belie. I am only interested in explaining why the flawed ideologies that naturally emerge in societies with substantive unjust inequality are democratically problematic. This chapter has been in the service of explaining what Tamar Gendler calls the "unavoidable cognitive consequences" of being a member of a society that "violates one's normative ideals." She marshals psychological evidence in favor of the claim that "either you will need to deliberately restrict your attention or experience so as not to encode certain sorts of genuine regularities . . . [o]r you will need to engage in . . . rationalization, changing your normative ideals to accord with the relevant sorts of experienced regularity (for example, by coming to endorse the legitimacy of these stereotypical associations)." I have sketched in some detail the processes by which this occurs. My focus in the next chapter is to complete the book's argument for equality. It is worthwhile to be clear about its structure.

We began this book with Martin Delany's argument that failures of equality of attainment lead to failures of equal respect by causing the false belief that those who control less of the resources are inferior (as well as loss of self-respect by the negatively privileged groups). He book's argument against inequality is not that failures of equal respect and the undermining of the self-respect are a moral harm (which they no doubt are). It is rather that the beliefs that enable these moral harms are particularly democratically problematic, because such beliefs make antidemocratic demagoguery effective.

The belies that tend to arise in conditions o stark inequality, such as belie in the ineriority o other grous, or one's own, tend to underine deocratic ideals, such as reasonableness. It is natural to think that the ersectives o inerior beings, or those less worthy o equality, are not reasonable ersectives. But this too is not the book's arguent against inequality, or not exactly. The arguent is rather that such ideological belies occlude the unreasonable nature o certain clais, institutions, and olicies. I there are social injustices, a olicy seeking to address the is reasonable. But it will be treated as unreasonable, in a society coitted to an ideology that there are no social injustices. Clais that contribute to injustice will ail to be recognized as such. In short, deagoguery will be eective.

My goal is to develo the best version o this arguent. I a less concerned with deending a reaction to its conclusion. I have argued that this arguent has a lengthy hilosohical tradition as an objection to deocracy. Given the inevitability o certain kinds o inequality, say, due to the act o disability or dierences in natural caacities, one can understand its lengthy hilosohical history as an antideocratic arguent. But it also can be taken as an arguent that deocracy should seek to iniize even aterial inequalities.

We have looked at various ways in which belies can arise that are resistant in distinctive ways to revision (where a belie's resistance to revision ay be due to structural eatures o a society, rather than an intrinsic eature o that belie). Possession o such belies can be a consequence o the noral unctioning o eisteic echaniss in societies with structural eatures that revent acquiring evidence against the or discounting counterevidence. We have also seen how ordinary echaniss o acquiring knowledge ay, as art o their ordinary unctioning, lead to biased belies that seem to be objectively grounded. I now turn to the echaniss at work in the ideologies discussed by hilosohers ro Aristotle to Marx: why do highly rivileged grous believe the ideology o their

own sueriority, and why do oressed grous accet the ideology o their own ineriority? ## POLITICAL IDEOLOGIES

I have argued in the revious chater that ideologies can be awed in a certain sense. They are awed, in the relevant sense, when they unction as ersistent barriers to the acquisition o knowledge. In this cha ter, I consider soe secic awed ideologies, which, I will argue, using tools ro social sychology, characteristically arise under certain social conditions. The second task o the chater is to exlain why these ideologies are deocratically robleatic. Here, I will use the tools o analytic eisteology.

There are two dierent kinds o awed ideologies that have been at the center o olitical hilosohy since at least the tie o Aristotle. These are the awed ideological belies those with control of resources tend to develo, and the awed ideological belies those without control of resources tend to develo. The subject o y chater is the awed ideological belies that arise in societies with an unjust distribution o resources. I will not ollow Aristotle in discussing the awed ideologies that arise in societies with a just but unequal distribution o resources.

I will be discussing the notion o erit, in a clai, or exale, like "the wealthy in Gerany have gained their ortunes by erit." I ysel a susicious o the notion o ure erit. And even i we suose that there is soe natural kind in the world that we are easuring when we ake judgents o erit, y own view is that there will still be any obvious cases in which inequalities are not justied by such judgents. The act o inherited wealth shows that two citizens can be born, beore either has been eritorious at all, with substantial inequality between the. Even i we take judgents o erit at ace- value, as easuring soething real, every actual huan society has countless exales o vast inequalities due sily to birth osition. But nothing in y central arguent deends uon y view that there is uch actual injustice. The arguent concerns substantial inequalities, no atter what their source.

In other words, in discussing the eects o substantial inequality, it will not atter or y central uroses whether or not the substantial inequalities are justied or unjustied. In other words, it will not atter whether those with control o the resources deserve control o the resources. And it will not atter whether those without control o the resources do not deserve control o the resources. The sociologist Max Weber argues that ebers o rivileged grous, by soething like sociological necessity, develo awed ideological belies:

The ates o huan beings are not equal. Men dier in their states o health and wealth or social status or what not. Sile observation shows that in every such situation he who is ore avored eels the never ceasing need to look uon his osition as in soe way "legitiate," uon his advantage being "deserved," and the other's disadvantage as being brought about by the latter's "ault." That the urely accidental causes o the dierence ay be ever so obvious akes no dierence.1

Weber adds, "[T]he continued exercise o every doination . . . always has the strongest need o sel- justication through a ealing to the rinciles o its legitiation."2 Weber describes this as a "need" that also "akes itsel elt in the relation between ositively and negatively rivileged grous o huan beings."3 I so, it is natural to exect that those who are born into a rivileged grou will have awed ideological belies concerning the rivileges they have. In articular, they will believe they deserve the rivileges they obtain as a result o accidental orces, such as birth osition. These belies will be awed ideological belies.

Weber's hyothesis that those who doinate others will develo what Lebron calls a "legitiizing yth" is an eirical clai about rocesses o belie oration. Is there scientic evidence o this sort o belie oration occurring? Or does the clai that awed belie can arise in this deviant ashion see like an unjustied eirical clai. What is the scientic evidence that the sychology o huans works this way?

The scientic evidence or a transition o the sort required by the account o ideology I have sketched is self- armation theory, a view in social sychology deriving ro a aous aer by the Stanord sychologist Claude Steele ublished in 1988. According to sel- aration theory, individuals are otivated to aintain a sel- concetion as a "good and aro riate erson."4 There is a substantial body o results within this literature suggesting that "sel- threatening eedback can exacerbate outgroup derogation and use of stereotypes." In other words, the large body of results that constitutes the self-affirmation theory literature in social psychology is evidence that the possibility of having to confront one's own (for example) racist behavior leads to false stereotypes that serve as self-justifications of one's attitudes. The self-affirmation theory literature is the empirical body of literature that provides evidence that everyone has a strong need for self-justification. It is not implausible to suppose that self-affirmation, in those who control societies' resources, manifests in a narrative that legitimates oppressive domination of others.

Self-affirmation theory is a mechanism that very plausibly underlies Weber's description of the legitimation myth of the elites. There is also additional empirical data in support of the formation of the specific kind of legitimation myth described by Weber.

There is a broad correlation across countries between wealth and support for the right-wing party. The correlation is small yet steady. But the problem with studying changes in political affiliation that co-occur with changes in self-interest is that very few if any political parties in countries are natural groupings. They tend to form in response to contingent events. The fact that political parties are in the main groupings of very different interest groups makes Weber's claim difficult to test.7 Furthermore, "It is extremely difficult to identify and interview a representative sample of wealthy Americans."

Page et al. set out to investigate the policy preferences of high-wealth individuals; the average annual income of their respondents, who lived in Chicago, exceeded \$1,000,000, and the median wealth of their sample was well over \$7,000,000.9 They discovered that this group of high-wealth individuals is very involved in politics: 68 percent of them had contributed to political campaigns, and 21 percent had bundled contributions for politicians. Ironically, their chie concern was ederal decits, resuably because o the ear o increased taxation on their wealth.

A study in 2014 by two econoists, Nattavudh Powdthavee, o the London School o Econoics, and Andrew J. Oswald, o the University o Warwick, took the or o a longitudinal study o lottery winners, looking at changes in their olitical belies.10 The hyothesis that they set out to test was whether "voting choices are ade out o sel- interest and then coe to be ebroidered in the ind with a or o oral rhetoric." The data set they used was ro the British Household Panel survey, a "reresentative rando sale o households, containing over 25,000 unique adult individuals, conducted between Seteber and Christas o each year ro 1991." Data on olitical reerences has been collected since 1991, and data on lottery winners has been gathered since 1997. They also ound "that an increase in a erson's overall household incoe in year t is associated with a rise in their belie in the justice o the current wealth distribution in society." More evidence is o course required; i British society is in act thoroughly eritocratic, erhas the accuulation o wealth caused by a winning lottery ticket rees the winner ro a awed ideological belie about unjust distribution o wealth. But i British society is not eritocratic, then the results seak in avor o Weber's clai. 11

A signicantly unequal distribution o the goods o society leads to exectations on behal o those who benet that they will receive ore o the goods o society than they in act deserve. Failure to receive the goods one is accustoed to receiving will be a disrution o one's exectations that is rone to be istaken as a violation o justice. However, because large aterial inequalities lead to the oration o awed ideologies that underine deocracy, soe kind o general aterial equality is quite likely a prerequisite or states to be caable o ollowing deocratic ideals.

There are causes o awed ideological belie other than ailures o equality in various senses. Societies in which unior ity o ideology is encouraged will give rise to exectations that one will encounter only others who share that ideology.12 I one is used to discussions and riendshis only with those with the sae ideology, encountering soeone whose ideology diers ay violate one's exectations. A violation o the exectation o catching a cold ro a lane tri is welcoe, rather than disturbing.13 But i one is raised to think o certain exectations as oral nors, violations o the conventions to which one has grown accustoed will again characteristically see like a disrution o noral exectations o the sort that is easily istaken or an encounter with soeone who should be orally condened. That is why those who are brought u in counities with a unior ideology tend to exerience those who ail to share that ideology as orally deviant. Those who are raised in counities with a unior ideology will identiy theselves with that ideology. And there are now decades o evidence in social sychology suorting the view that grou identication generally has a distinctive nonrational eect on belie oration. Albert Hastor and Hadley Cantril discovered that students ro two dierent Ivy League universities who watched a ootball gae between their schools cae to very dierent conclusions about the controversial reereeing calls during the gae.14 Their university aliation redicted the dierent conclusions. So students at the two universities arrived at dierent belies on the basis o the sae evidence, because o diering grou loyalties. This is a henoenon that the sychologist Ziva Kunda calls motivated reasoning. 15

There is now a long history o the sychological study o otivated reasoning. In a recent study, the legal theorist Dan Kahan and his colleagues resented 202 exeriental subjects with a three- and- a- hal- inute l o a rotest outside a building, aer testing the or their cultural and olitical worldviews.16 The subjects were asked to iagine theselves as ebers o a jury, deciding whether the olice had violated the rotestor's constitutional right to ree seech in intervening. Hal the subjects were told that the l was o rotesters outside an abortion clinic rotesting legalized abortion, and the other hal were told that the l was o rotestors outside a ilitary recruitent center objecting to the ilitary's ban on oenly gay and lesbian ebers o the ilitary. Kahan and colleagues discovered that olitical orientation strongly affected subjects' legal judgments. Subjects with diametrically opposed political views reacted in "strong and opposite ways to the experimental manipulation": 70 percent of the political conservatives judged that the police had violated the demonstrators' rights in the abortion clinic case, but only 16 percent of political conservatives who saw the same video presented as a protest against the military judged that the police had violated the demonstrators' rights; strong political progressives had precisely the opposite reaction. Kahan's research shows that the effects of motivated reasoning are not limited to watching sports.

Dan Kahan usefully defines motivated reasoning as "the tendency of people to unconsciously process information . . . to promote goals or interests extrinsic to the decision making task at hand." According to Kahan, a goal motivates cognition when it affects the assessment of evidence, for example, the evidence of one's senses (as in when rooting for a team, one sees a controversial referee call), or scientific evidence (as I think of certain kinds of theists confronted with the evidence for evolution, and they no doubt think of me).19

Kahan identifies one overall source of motivated reasoning in the political domain, what he calls "identity protective cognition." Identity protective cognition is motivated reasoning with the goal of "affirming one's membership in an important reference group." Kahan suggests that all cases of motivated political reasoning can be explained by identity protective cognition (though I do not need to be committed to the bold claim that *all* ideological belief comes from identity protective cognition).

Rawls argues that "a permanent feature of the public culture of democracy" is a "diversity of reasonable comprehensive religious, philosophical, and moral doctrines." In other words, the public culture of democracy requires distinct ideologies (this is what Rawls calls "the fact of reasonable pluralism"). Given identity protective cognition, members of a society with a uniform ideology will adopt flawed ideological oral belies when conronted with novel ideologies. I will argue that the resence o awed ideological belies is what allows or eective deagoguery, o the sort that underines deocratic deliberation. Without the existence o reasonable luralis, there is too uch latitude or eective deagoguery to allow or deocracy.

We have seen now in detail two structural societal sources o awed ideological belie in a society. The rst is substantive ailures o equality o dierent sorts. The second is ideological uniority. In arguing that substantive ailures o equality will tend to lead those who control the resources to develo a characteristic kind o legitiation yth, I have justied Weber's view. One task o this chater has been coleted.

What about the awed ideology o those who do not control society's resources? As we have seen, in a society that is in act eritocratic, the awed ideology will take one or. But consider instead the awed ideology o those who do not control society's resources in societies in which there is an unjust distribution o resources. Since ebers o these grous are in fact oressed, a awed ideology could prevent them from recognizing their own oppression, or, with less coitent, prevent them from acting so as to alleviate their oppression. What reason is there to think that those who suer ro unjust inequalities will tend to develo a awed ideology that revents the ro recognizing the injustice?

In Of the First Principles of Government (1.4.1), David Hue writes:

aears ore surrizing to those, who consider huan aairs with a hilosohical eye, than the easiness with which the any are governed by the ew; and the i licit subission, with which en resign their own senti ents and assions to those o their rulers. When we enquire by what eans this wonder is eected, we shall nd, that, as is always on the side o the governed, the governors have nothing to suort the but oinion. It is therefore, on opinion only that government is founded; and this maxim extends to the most despotic and most military governments, as well as to the most free and most popular. The soldan of EGYPT, or the emperor of ROME, might drive his harmless subjects, like brute beasts, against their sentiments and inclination: But he must, at least, have led his mamalukes, or prætorian bands, like men, by their opinion.

The puzzle Hume here raises is as follows. Why are subjects whose interests are not furthered by the agenda of the elite nevertheless guided by the opinions the elite wish them to have? Max Weber later writes:

Every highly privileged group develops the myth of its natural, especially its blood, superiority. Under conditions of stable distribution of power and, consequently, of an estatist order, that myth is accepted by the negatively privileged group.22

It is natural to think that the elite maintain power by promulgating the flawed ideology that their interests are the interests of the society at large. Weber's point concerns the *mechanism* by which they do so, namely, by promulgating the ideology of elite superiority and the belief that the society is a meritocracy. Many other thinkers have made similar points.23 In trying to make the point plausible, I will delve into some detail about the mechanisms in question. But first, I want to free the project of describing this kind of mechanism of oppression from two commitments it lacks.

First, Weber mentions "blood superiority." Since Weber's time, a powerful societal sanction has arisen against claims of blood superiority. The narrative of highly privileged groups, at least in Western societies, is no longer one of the *genetic* inferiority of the negatively privileged groups. Even the most virulently racist groups have replaced claims of genetic inferiority with claims of *cultural* inferiority. The virulently racist and anti-Semitic Hungarian political party Jobbik is clear that it regards the Roa as criinal because o their culture, not because o their genetics. In the West, thereore, Weber's "yth o natural and blood sueriority" has been everywhere relaced by a yth o cultural sueriority.

Second, one ight isunderstand Weber, as well as what ollows, as an attet at a complete exlanation o the ailures o negatively rivileged grous to act olitically in their own interests. But this is not correct about Weber, and not correct about y roject. Weber has any other criticiss o deocracy. For exale, Weber has concerns about the undeocratic nature o bureaucracies. Weber's clai is a artial, not total, exlanation o the ailures o deocracy.

In any case, the notion o "negative rivilege" has otentially any interretations. Suose that we take "negative rivilege" to be easured in ters o relative ownershi o goods. In this case, any eole ay be negatively rivileged toward those they never encounter, but not negatively rivileged relative to those they encounter. This will hinder olitical action to correct unjust distributions with resect to the orer grou. Moreover, negative rivilege coes in degrees. I so, erhas the ex ected utility o acting alone is not suciently great. There are any other otential exlanations o the ailure o negatively rivileged grous to act other than Weber's clai. It is no art o y roject to attribute to Weber's clai all the ailure o negatively rivileged grous to act. Weber's clai hels us to understand how one kind o roaganda exloits the resence o the elite ideology in the negatively rivileged grous to urther soe goal o the elite grou. For exale, i one can get negatively rivileged grous to accet that the syste is basically air and eritocratic, then one can lead the to oose attets to reor corrution. It just akes sound econoic sense or the ositively rivileged grous to get ebers o negatively rivileged grous to accet that the syste is air and eritocratic. That is the only use to which I ut Weber's clai.

I a not deending the view that Weber's clai is a co lete exlanation o the acts o injustice. Nevertheless, desite y odest clais or it, the view that negatively rivileged grous acquire awed ideologies that revent the ro recognizing their own oression aces understandable sketicis. Here is what I think are the two ost lausible sources o sketicis about the view.

The rst source o sketicis is natural beuddleent at why soeone should willingly adot a belie to the eect that a grou to which she belongs is culturally inerior, in those cases in which that belie runs obviously contrary to sel interest. The second source o sketicis about Weber's view, which is ore dicult to disel, is that negatively rivileged grous have a great deal o evidence o the aws in the ideology o the highly rivileged grou. I don't consciously register that virtually every hoeless erson I see on the streets o Aerica is one color, and it is not y skin color. But these acts are ainully obvious to those on the street, who no doubt nd it deely huiliating to have to beg or ood ro ebers o a rivileged class who, ro their ersective at least, can be reasonably taken to have that rivilege because o accidental actors o birth. Why, given what sees to be an undeniable oint about the exeriences o ebers o negatively rivileged grous, should they nevertheless have the tendency to adot the ideology o the highly rivileged grou? I will resond to these objections to Weber in order.

It is indeed ilausible in the extree that soeone would willingly adot a belie that entails the ineriority o her own grou. However, this kind o sketicis about the view relies on a alse icture o how belies are ored. It relies on the alse reise that, in Bernard Willias's evocative hrase, one can decide to believe. 24 Since it is evident that one would not rationally decide to adot a belie that resents one's own grou in a negative light, soeone gried by this odel o belie oration will be sketical o the clai.

However, we cannot sily decide to believe soething. It is not u to e to decide whether or not I a on Mars now. It is siilarly not u to e to decide about the direct evidence o y senses. But testiony ro authoritative sources is a ri ary source o evidence. This is why the school syste o a state is the aradig exale o an ideological state apparatus, in Althusser's sense.25

Colonialis in Arica brought with it schools, but the schools were generally Christian schools. Yet attending Alliance High School involved being told that the dire circustances in which one was raised were due to one's arents' ailure to abandon their "riitive aith."30 To deny the success o these eorts, to deny the sincerity o Christians in Kenya who denounce their very own ancestors as riitive indels, is sily not lausible. History testies to the diculty o rejecting a broad and consistent taestry o testionial evidence ro aarently authoritative inoration roviders, esecially when it begins at an early age.

Bernard Willias has argued, granting that belie is not under direct voluntary control, that belie is nevertheless under indirect voluntary control.31 I can voluntarily join counities that doubt the sources o evidence I have and, aer long exosure, learn to reject such sources o evidence. Perhas I have been raised in a religious cult. I can choose to go to a university and take courses in other cultures that orce e to conront the contingencies o y ubringing. But spontaneous belie or ation is in general an involuntary rocess. We acquire belies sontaneously ro the testiony o authority gures, ro the lack o reliable sources that contradict the, and so on. Even to begin the rocess o indirect voluntary control over belie is clearly an arduous, oen lie- changing task, one that oen involves searation ro aily and counity.

There is a sile arguent ro the reise that belie is not under our direct voluntary control to the conclusion that "negatively rivileged grous" will acquire the awed ideological belies o the "ositively rivileged grou." The ositively rivileged grou will control the doinant narrative. I the arguent is lausible, it raises even ore serious concerns in a liberal deocratic state. A liberal deocratic state requires rovision o schooling or all, as well as a news edia. Control o what is taught in the ublic schools aounts to control o the basic olitical dialectic. It sees ilausible to deny that ebers o the highly rivileged grou will win such battles o control.

Weber's view is consistent with the ossibility o occasional resistance to the highly rivileged grou's ideology. But this will be ossible only by individuals with great orce o will, by the individual eort involved in exercising the kind o "indirect voluntary control" discussed above. Resistance to the adotion o the ideology o the highly rivileged grou will oen involve what Kristie Dotson has called "third order changes."32 A third- order change "requires erceivers to be aware o a range o diering sets o hereneutical resources in order to be ca able o shiing resources aroriately." This requires "uency in diering hereneutical resources." Yet structural eatures o society oen revent third- order changes, or exale, because o the control o the ublic dialectic by a doinant grou. This is what Dotson calls contributory injustice.

It ay see easy to adot such changes in societies characterized by a cobination o easy access to alternative inoration sources and a suciently diverse local counity that suorts entertaining and debating alternative viewoints. But even when both o these actors are in lace, there aears to be no soening o ideological ositions, and i anything, the reverse is the case. As the sociologist C. Wright Mills noted, "Peole, we know, tend to select those oral edia which conr what they already believe and enjoy. In a arallel way, they tend in the etroolitan segregation to coe into live touch with those whose oinions are siilar to theirs. Others they tend to treat unseriously. . . . They do not, accordingly, ex erience genuine clashes o view- oint, about genuine issues. And when they do, they tend to consider it ere rudeness."33 Dotson is surely correct to say that third- order changes "are not easy," even in societies that aear to be suercially oen.34

Unjust societies oen involve structural conditions that iose contributory injustice. In art 1, "Southern Night," o his book Black Boy, ublished in 1944, the Aerican writer Richard Wright rovides vivid testiony o the role layed by ublic schools in reventing the negatively rivileged grou ro entertaining and accessing alternative narratives o itsel:

I dreaed o going north and writing books, novels. . . . I knew that I lived in a country in which the asirations o black eole were liited, arked- o. Yet I elt that I had to go soewhere and do soething to redee y being alive. My classates elt that I was doing soething that was vaguely wrong, but they did not know how to exress it. As the outside world grew ore eaningul, I becae ore concerned, tense; and y classates and y teachers would say: "Why do you ask so any questions?" Or "Kee quiet." . . . In e was shaing a yearning or a kind o consciousness, a ode o being that the way o lie about e had said could not be, ust not be, and uon which the enalty o death had been laced. Soewhere in the dead o the southern night y lie had switched onto the wrong track and, without y knowing it, the locootive o y heart was rushing down a dangerously stee sloe heading or a collision, heedless o the warning red lights that blinked all about e, the sirens and the bells and the screas that lled the air.35

Wright's descrition o the Aerican South o his youth is one in which the education syste and edia are ereated with the yth o Black ineriority, which is largely acceted by the negatively rivileged oulation o southern Blacks. There are structural barriers to accessing alternative concetualizations. Dotson's notion o contributory injustice is a erect descri tion o the kind o injustice involved when a highly rivileged grou uses the school systes to roulgate the ideology o their own sueriority to ebers o the negatively rivileged grou, and to revent the ossibility o adoting new ones.

There is a substantial body o evidence o a ore systeatic sort or Weber's clai that ebers o negatively rivileged grous tend to adot the awed ideology o the elites. Again, the Stanord sychologist Claude Steele has rovided it, in a set o exerients that has given rise to a assive eirical literature. This is the literature on stereotype threat, which has been shown to be a signicant iedient to achieveent by negatively rivileged grous. The literature on stereotye threat is a distinct literature ro the literature on sel- aration theory. But taken together, they comprise the scientific explanation of the flawed ideologies of highly privileged and negatively privileged groups.

Claude Steele and Joshua Aronson describe the phenomenon of stereotype threat as "the immediate situational threat that derives from the broad dissemination of negative stereotypes about one's group—the threat of possibly being judged and treated stereotypically, or of possibly self-fulfilling such a stereotype."36 Steele hypothesized that when members of groups that are negatively stereotyped as poor at a certain task face frustratingly difficult challenges in that task, and their stereotype is highlighted, they more readily adopt the negative stereotype in that situation. In a paper from 1995, Steele and Aronson give difficult GRE verbal questions to college students, in two separate conditions. In the "diagnostic" condition, students are told that it is a test of their "reading and verbal reasoning abilities." In the "nondiagnostic" condition, students are told that the purpose of the research is just to examine "psychological factors involved in solving verbal problems." In the diagnostic condition, Black students performed significantly worse relative to whites than in the nondiagnostic condition.

Steele and Aronson write that because stereotype "threat persists over time, it may have the further effect of pressuring these students to protectively disidentify with achievement in school and related intellectual domains. That is, it may pressure the person to define or redefine the self-concept such that school achievement is neither a basis of self-evaluation nor a personal identity."37 In short, the undeniably real psychological phenomenon of stereotype threat is the naturalistic basis for the adoption of the flawed ideology of the elite group by the negatively privileged group. Black Americans were stigmatized as less intelligent to justify restricting their life prospects. But the very prejudices that operate to oppress them work, via the mechanism Steele and Aronson describe, to lead them to adopt those prejudices as beliefs, in a form of identity protective cognition.

I have argued or the lausibility o Weber's clai that negatively rivileged grous tend to adot the elite ideology o their own ineriority. But this tells us nothing about the mechanisms by which this occurs. Two exlanations are required. First, we need an exlanation o the echaniss by which highly rivileged grous win control o the edia and ublic education. Carl Schitt argues that the liberal concetion o laws that justiy every oreseeable alication ust always give way to what he calls "the excetion."41 The excetion "can at best be characterized as a case o extree eril, a danger to the existence o the state, or the like. The Milgram experiments, and to a considerably more limited extent previous experiments in social psychology by Milgram's advisor, Solomon Asch, show that the ideological beliefs of authority figures, especially when presented as scientific "experts," are given great weight. But social hierarchies work in many less obvious ways to confer epistemic advantage on privileged groups. Susanna Siegel draws our attention, for example, to forthcoming work showing that members of in-groups follow the gaze of fellow in-group members more than they do the gaze of out-group members.51 As Siegel points out, the unconscious perceptual habits associated with social hierarchy deliver an epistemic advantage to in-group members:

Ingroup participants follow gaze more readily of ingroup members than outgroup members, whereas outgroup participants follow gaze of both ingroup and outgroup members. To the extent that gaze-following indicates confidence that the followed-person's object of attention or experience of it is epistemically valuable, it is reasonable to hypothesize that this result reflects an underlying pattern of social valuation. This kind of selection effect shapes our epistemic situation.

Yet another set of considerations bearing on the epistemic advantages of highly privileged groups comes from recent analytic epistemology.

The problem flawed ideological belief poses for negatively privileged groups is that it prevents them from acting to overcome the injustices they face. Max Weber points out that all highly privileged groups throughout history share one thing in common, the ideology that they have achieved their success through merit. Members of the highly privileged group, through control of the media and the education system, will make it part of the dominant narrative of the society that the goods of society are justly distributed, according to merit rather than accident of birth position (this will occur even in conditions in which there are obvious differences in equality ro birth, due, or exale, to inheritance). The negatively rivileged grous will be revented ro attaining the knowledge needed to act recisely by the awed ideological belies they adot ro the doinant narrative. A riary nexus o deocratic contestation is over the distribution o resources. Mebers o negatively rivileged grous will be hindered in such contestation by accetance o the doinant ideology, that society's resources are airly divided by erit.

We have seen that in conditions o substantive unjust inequality, the belie that resources are airly divided by erit will tend to be adoted as a doinant ideology. Langton draws our attention to the act that epistemic authority and practical authority oen interact.52 To take an exa le ro Joseh Raz, a doctor's ractical authority to grant, or exale, rescritions deends uon her eisteic authority, or exertise. In a book I wrote and a aer I coauthored, a ore recise version o the ollowing clai about knowledge is deended.53 The clai concerns the eisteic nors o action (so one ay have satised the eisteic nors, without satisying other nors on action):

The interest- relativis o knowledge has direct consequences or olitical action. Consider an eloyee o a business who believes that its workers are being exloited. She is wondering about whether or not to act on her belie by organizing a union. Given interest- relativis about knowledge, here is the recise role the ideology that the society is eritocratic lays, in a society with unjust inequalities, in deocratic contestation. Because o adherence to this ideology, ebers o highly rivileged grous will believe that they deserve their osition in the status hierarchy. Possession o the ideology akes the unable to recognize the resence o social injustice (since it is an ideology, that is, resistant to rational revision). They will assume that they are not at risk of doing something that violates their system of values. Their practical authority will give them at the very least a *presumed* epistemic authority. The presumed epistemic authority thus attained can be used to strengthen their practical authority, the very practical authority that gives the higher epistemic standing that comes with having less at stake.

Kristie Dotson defines *epistemic oppression* as "epistemic exclusions afforded positions and communities that produce deficiencies in social knowledge," where an *epistemic exclusion* "is an infringement on the epistemic agency of knowers that reduces her or his ability to participate in a given epistemic community." If knowledge is interest-relative, then those who society regularly places in high-stakes positions when deliberating for its goods are epistemically oppressed, in Dotson's sense. The interest-relativism of knowledge explains why those without resources face epistemic oppression. It is at least one part of an explanation of the self-reinforcing mechanism at work in control of both the mechanisms of retribution and the delivery of information, as they play out in the consolidation of oppressive power and domination.

Is knowledge interest-relative? As it turns out, it is not relevant to these consequences. Every competing explanation also results in a very similar and equally destructive form of epistemic debilitation.58 Controversy remains over (a) its extent and (b) its source.

What is the extent of the dependence of knowledge on interests? Empirical surveys of judgments have divided into two groups. One kind of result, due to Ángel Pinillos, has revealed strong effects of practical stakes on judgments about knowledge. The second kind of result reveals clear statistically significant results of stakes on judgments of knowledge, but only in high-stakes cases, where matters of great significance are at issue.

My aim here is to establish that negatively privileged groups are epistemologically disadvantaged in debates about the justiability o distribution o undaental goods in a society. These are very high- stakes situations or negatively rivileged grous. What is at issue in the olitical case are very high- stakes situations. So we ay set aside debates about the scoe o the henoenon as irrelevant to our uroses. Aer a decade o discussion, alost everyone agrees that the henoenon is real in deciding about what to do in atters o great iort, such as those at issue in olitical disute about the distribution o societal goods.

There are dierent alternative accounts o the henoenon o judgents o knowledge being sensitive to what one ought to act uon. I will ocus on three, and show that all three o the also have the sae consequence, that those who are laced by structural eatures o society in high- stakes situations in coeting or its goods are at an eisteic disadvantage, and hence are eisteically oressed, in Dotson's sense.

Here is one account o the henoenon, due to the hilosoher Brian Weatherson.59 On Weatherson's account, those in high- stakes situations still have the sae level o justied credence as those not in lower- stakes situations who have the sae evidence. However, when a great deal is at stake, we have higher standards or ull belie. But it is ull belie that is required or action, and ull belie that is required or knowledge. Those who are in highstakes situations when vying or the goods o society will have to eet higher standards or ull belie. This is an eisteic har. Since it is reasonable to hold, just as above, that negatively rivileged grous will have ore at stake in disutes over society's goods, they will be eisteically disadvantaged in just the ways described above, with the interest- relativity o knowledge.

Here is a second alternative to exlain the data otivating the interest- relativity o knowledge, without endorsing it. It is an alternative exlanation that I have reviously called con dence shaking. 61 Soeone laced in a high- stakes situation has their condence suciently shaken by stress that it reduces their degree o belie "below the threshold required or knowledge."62 Since, as beore, knowledge requires ull belie, the condence- shaking account also redicts that those who society laces in high- stakes situations in negotiations over its goods will be eisteically disadvantaged. Be that as it ay, it too clearly results in certain kinds o systeatic eisteic oression.

Here is a third alternative to exlain the data otivating the interest- relativity o knowledge, without endorsing it; it is a kind o error theory.63 It is natural to assue that those acing high- stakes decisions are rone to wishul thinking. For exa le, both sides o oosing aries acing o in battle tend to be ibued with a belie that they will gain the victory. It's natural to react to soeone who akes an iortant decision based on a "gut reaction," rather than by gathering ore in oration, that this erson has based the decision on wishul thinking. They should have acquired ore inoration. Perhas what is haening in the cases that otivate the interestrelativity o knowledge is that we alsely think that those acing high- stakes decisions are engaged in wishul thinking, and on this basis we reuse to attribute knowledge to the.

There is evidence; or exale, the olitical theorist Archon Fung and the eisteologist Jennier Nagel have argued that there are good eects o what Fung calls "hot deliberation."64 Fung rightly oints out that "hot deliberation— discussions in which articiants have high stakes and aect the exercise o ublic ower— tends to increase the rationality o rocesses."65 But this does not show that the resource oor, or exale, have a deliberative advantage. First, as Fung recognizes, "inor ation is costly."66 The resource oor will not have the sae access to inoration, or any reasons. It is hard to see that the high- stakes eect on increased rationality will ake u or this. Secondly, the key interest- relative exales o i ortance to deocratic deliberation and civic action will be ones in which the resource- oor grou is in a high- stakes osition and the resource- rich grou is not. It would be natural or the resource- rich grou, as we just discussed, to view the resource- oor grou as engaged in wishul thinking, even i the resource- oor grou was ore rational.67 Third, and ost iortantly, the view that hot deliberation surs ore care ul thinking resuoses that those in high- stakes situations do have a higher eisteic bar to reach. As Jennier Nagel argues, this shows that, given the sae evidence available, those in high- stakes situations will ace barriers to knowledge that those in low- stakes situations do not.68 Asyetries between those in high- stakes situations and those in less-high- stake situations will accentuate the cost o the greater cognitive deand.69

In the revious chater, we discussed one kind o "eisteic injustice" introduced by Fricker, naely, hermeneutical injustice. 70 Hereneutical injustice is when a grou is syste atically denied access to the resources to concetualize their social world correctly. Neither the interest- relativity o knowledge nor any o the alternative exlanations I have discussed straightorwardly involves rejudice. So they are not theselves ors o eisteic injustice. The erceived eisteic challenge acing grous that lack society's resources will revent the ro acting. In schools, they will hear that the society in which they live is a eritocracy. They will not be able to act on the basis o knowledge o injustice, because their negatively rivileged status ay, in the resence o the alternative exlanation rovided by the ideology o elite sueriority, as well as the rosect o retribution by those controlling the risons and the olice, revent the ro acquiring knowledge.

The interest- relativity o knowledge, as well as any o the alternatives that exlain the henoena it addresses, also ex lains an iortant eature o eisteic oression: its relativity. Subordinate grous do not just ace higher standards or knowledge generally. They still know ordinary roositions about their location, their households, and so on. Though the raework I sketch elsewhere is well suited to account or the relative nature o eisteic oression, the kinds o acts about ower, subordination, and interests that have an eect on knowledge are not adequately catured by sile aeal to the "ractical interests" o an agent in a situation, o the sort ound there.76 For exale, a woan who aces the threat o being beaten or not cooking dinner in a certain way aces a high- stakes decision with resect to the roosition that that is a way to cook dinner. Nevertheless, it doesn't see that this is an ideal exale o a case in which subordinate status leads to higher eisteic standards.77 The raework o interest- relativity gives one a schea to see how ower and interests can raise obstacles to knowledge o acts in situations in which they have a certain kind o ractical i ortance. The raework o interest- relativity accoodates the secicity o the loss o knowledge; it deends on ractical eatures o the circustance in which the utative knower is laced and the roosition in question. The eects o interests on knowledge, whether direct or indirect, have urther negative ilications or ebers o oressed grous, ones articularly relevant or deocracy. I soeone akes a clai in the or o an assertion, we exect her to know what she is saying. That is why utterances o socalled Moore's Paradoxical sentences like "Tyrone went to the arty, but I do not know that Tyrone went to the arty" are invariably odd. I one asserts soething, one suggests that one knows what one asserts. This is due to the nature o the seech act o assertion. Thus, any hilosohers accet:

(Knowledge Nor or Assertion) Assert that only i you know that .

The knowledge nor o assertion characterizes the seech act o assertion as guided by knowledge. I one asserts without knowing, one is subject to criticis. That is in the nature o assertion. The knowledge nor does not entail that assertions that are alse, or unjustied, or are delivered insincerely ail to be assertions. One can be criticized or not adhering to the rules o the gae while still laying the gae.

The knowledge nor o assertion, together with the interest- relativity o knowledge, or any of the suggested alternatives, entails that negatively rivileged grous will be severely hindered in deocratic deliberation. The assertions o e bers o negatively rivileged grous will be taken less seriously in the oration o olicy that bears heavily on their interests. This is an instance o a ore general henoenon:

Peole with advantages are loath to believe that they just haen to be eole with advantages. In book 1, chater 7, o The Politics, Aristotle argues that the master and the slave engage in two quite distinct kinds o activities. Aer coleting his dissertation at Teacher's College and serving on the aculty there, Snedden went on to becoe the rst state coissioner or education in Massachusetts in 1909. The reort, titled "The Cardinal Princiles o Secondary Education," took three years to dra by a coittee consisting o soe o the ost roinent gures in education. There is soething decidedly odd about this stretch o "The Cardinal Princiles o Secondary Education." A deocracy is dened as a state in which the education syste leads each erson to choose that vocation to which they are naturally suited. Though there are dierent denitions o "liberty," none involves suberging one's will to the needs o the counity or the sake o eciency.28 No deocratic olitical hilosoher in history has argued that deocracy requires an individual to choose a vocation because it is the one in which that individual will be the most eective for society. The educational curricula o Aerican high schools in the twentieth century was doinated by a atriarchal ideology no dierent than the one churches roulgated in the Middle Ages. Yet it was advanced by acadeics who took theselves to be guided by a new objective science o edagogy, one that advanced the ideal o eciency as a or o social control.

In 1916, The National Education Association's Coittee on Social Studies in Secondary Education issued a reort that was the result o a conict between the teaching o history and the teaching o what was regarded as a ore socially use ul disciline, social studies. The legacy o the reort o 1916 is colex. But understanding its colexity is iortant to seeing the dierent eleents aecting the US ublic syste down to the resent tie.

The National Education Association's Social Studies reort reects warring views on the nature and urose o civics education in the ublic schools. John Dewey argued that a deocratic society is one whose ebers should always be seeking to realize its ideals. A necessary art o this rocess is reection on the ailure o those ideals. Dewey's inuence can be seen, or exale, in the recoended course or the twelh grade, "Probles o Deocracy." The "Probles o Deocracy" course that is outlined in the National Education Association's reort turned out to be quite radical. Harold Rugg's popular textbook for that course, *An Introduction to Problems of American Culture*, contains a unit called "Public Opinion and American Life," which is devoted to explaining how people come to have the ideologies they do.30 It includes an explanation, for example, of why the son of a factory owner would be raised to be against unions. It is considerably more political than standard textbooks of later eras.

Nevertheless, advocates of social control as a means to social efficiency also had a substantial effect on the report. In fact, the report is largely written in the language of social efficiency, and it has the goal of inculcating a single ideology in students, evident in the planning for the community civics course. It emphasizes the importance of "the average citizen in a democracy to think in terms of national interest" as a means of promoting "national efficiency," for which "national solidarity" was vital. It warned about the dangers of "internationalism" and of "humanity as greater than its divisions" and of talk of a "world community." According to Evans, its goals could be summarized as the establishment of social studies as a means to instill "reverence for American and Eurocentric ideals and traditions through history courses, social training for social efficiency and conformity through Community civics, and an attitude favoring progressive social betterment through the fledgling and experimental Problems of Democracy."31

The struggle between a conception of social studies as fostering, on the one hand, reflection on obstacles to societal improvement and, on the other, a uniform ideology that allows for efficient social control of the masses, via nationalist appeals to American exceptionalism, continued throughout the twentieth century and on to the present day. However, the role of educational theorists who masked an explicit aim of social control in the language of scientific efficiency in advocating the latter conception is rarely acknowledged.32

We have now looked at soe eatures o the ideology o elites. By investigating a articular exale, I have aied to ake lausible Weber's clai that in societies with, or exa le, large and unequal distribution o goods, the elite are able to transit their awed ideological belies to the negatively rivileged grous as a echanis o social control.

Jaes reinds us that the citizens o the Athenian deocratic state were well aware o the dangers o granting exertise status.36 It is to avoid these dangers that "[t]he essence o the Greek ethod, here as elsewhere, was the reusal to hand over these things to exerts, but to trust to the intelligence and sense o justice o the oulation at large, which eant o course a ajority o the coon eole."

## CONCLUSION

This book has been in the service o warning o alse hoe o realized ideals. Large inequalities in society tend to lead to eisteic ractices that are obstacles to the realization o liberal deocratic ideals. I have argued that, by the nature o things, we will be revented ro erceiving these obstacles. Those who benet ro such inequalities will tend to believe that the ideals have nevertheless been realized, even in the ace o clear evidence that they have not. They will use their rivileged status to erect vehicles o roaganda devoted to obstructing investigation into the gas between ideal and reality. The resulting school systes and edia outlets will revent even ebers o disossessed grous ro recognizing the existence o such gas. When hilosohy is taught in a way that does not include the intractability o these gas as one o its central robles, it is rightly regarded as contributing to the.

But there is a danger that this book will be taken, incorrectly, to disiss or ignore the iortance o social oveents in articulating and acting against inequalities and injustices o various sorts. This would be a drastic isinterretation o y ais. Indeed, I hoe to have ade clear why those oveents are all the ore to be resected. No one ailiar with Aerican history can ignore how dicult it has been to bend the arc o history toward justice. Given the eirical evidence o the dire status o Aerican citizens o Arican descent, it could be claied that the bend in the arc is alost indiscernible.

Nor can the rocess o trying to achieve justice be viewed as sily the unolding o deocratic concets into law and ractice over tie. Huan agency, careully craed aeals, consciousness- raising o various sorts and at diering levels (individual, grou, society- wide), cultural and artistic innovations and aesthetic challenges, years o huan labor, blood, death, suering, dreas, direct collective action, all and ore were and are essential.

And still we ust account or the aoreentioned evidence o how little has been the rogress o equality or soe grous. Counity organizers, agitators, rebels, activists, revolutionaries throughout the history o the Aerican olity ro Patrick Henry to Martin Luther King have known that challenging the awed ideologies that doinate discourse, legitiate ublic ractice, and shae the nors o civil society ust be conronted sooner rather than later.

Radical social oveents in their tie are always viewed as disturbances o the oral order. It is only retrosectively that social oveents are viewed as seaking truth to ower in ways that ake oral sense. In the United States, or exa le, Reverend Martin Luther King Jr. is universally celebrated, including by citizens who share the ideology o those who desised hi in his lietie. This ay be used as evidence o their success. But given ersisting ailures o equality in the United States, a ore lausible exlanation is that they have been assiilated into a rhetoric that views the olity as ever ore just, the society rogressively ore air and decent. The act that social oveents ake retrosective oral sense does not ean that the ractices that accoany the change in aterially signicant ways.

One ust be constantly vigilant o the tendency or the suosed success o social oveents to be used to ask their failure. Embracing the moral sense of a social movement is an effective method to justify the end of critical scrutiny into the contradictions and tensions between professed political ideals and the actual circumstances of citizens. The success of the Civil Rights Movement seemed to invite a retreat from the type of critical inquiry I am engaged in here. There is a case to be made that the nobility and courage of those who participated in the Civil Rights Movement were used to mask continuing inequality.1 Claims of persisting inequality could be dismissed as attempts to diminish the heroic efforts of those who participated in the Civil Rights Movement, who sought a "colorblind" society. An analysis of propaganda and its relationship to flawed ideology helps us understand this danger. As Vesla Weaver and Michelle Alexander have argued with respect to the American Civil Rights Movement in the 1960s, a social movement based on compelling the polity to recognize the role of race in inequality could be utilized to mask continued racial inequality. The heroic narrative of social movements faces the ever-present risk of being co-opted in the service of the message that the problems they addressed are now solved.

All social and political movements have struggled to create cognitive space for ideological moves, assumptions, and alternative narratives. Perhaps chief among these is the democratic revolution against monarchy. I have tried to explain why even its successes will characteristically be distorted, to explain why even liberal democratic ideals relatively quickly become assimilated to the ideology of the privileged that they were intended to supplant. Alongside this, I have sketched the mechanism by which the subversion of democratic ideals occurs. It is my hope, which by no means rises to the arrogance of expectation, that this book will play some positive role in its prevention.

Perhaps the point of frightening cigarette labels is to cause people to pause to check their reflexive bad habit and give them time to make an autonomous decision. Thus, on this account, harsh cigarette warning labels actually aid the deliberative process by disturbing reflex. Regardless, there will be certain practices of the sort I describe with cigarette warning labels that are democratically acceptable: where we in effect cede our autonomy to someone else to control it, when we deem them to be reliable. These are democratically acceptable.

- 33. Here is another example, along the same lines. Many governments, including Western democracies such as the United States, employ propaganda as political warfare. The false messages they deliver, for example, to undermine the reputation of an enemy leader, are often called "counter-information." But, as Susan Stebbing points out, "[t]he word 'counter-information' does not make sense." Calling statements that are known to be false "counter-information" is to wrap contributions that run counter to the norms of rational discourse in a veil that imitates the norms of rational discourse. Stebbing, *Thinking to Some Purpose*, p. 63.

Mary Kate Mc Gowan, in her paper "Oppressive Speech," published in 2009, argues that there is a category of subordinating speech, which she calls "covert excercitives," which do not require the speaker to have a position of authority. These are speech acts that have the effect of permitting oppressive speech in subsequent contexts, such as sexist speech among men that relaxes the norms of conversation to allow explicit speech. But even covert excercitives in Mc Gowan's sense require "the speaker to have a certain status" (p. 402). In the case just mentioned, the speaker must be at minimum "one of the guys." (See also Mc Gowan's earlier paper "Conversational Exercitives," published in 2004, which introduces the basic idea of a covert speech act that changes subject permissibility facts, but without the attendant defense of the lack of a need for the speaker to occupy a position of authority.)

- 19. Kahan also has a good summary of the dangers of motivated reasoning: "When subject to it, individuals can be unwittingly disabled from making dispassionate, open-minded, and fair judgments. Moreover, although people are poor at detecting motivated reasoning in themselves, they can readily discern its effect in others, in whom it is taken to manifest bias or bad faith. Accordingly, in collective deliberations, motivated cognition can trigger a self-reinforcing atmosphere of distrust and recrimination that prevents culturally diverse participants from converging on outcomes that suit their common ends."
- **20.** "Individuals depend on select groups—from families to university faculties, from religious denominations to political parties—for all manner of material and emotional support. Propositions that impugn the character or competence of such groups, or that contradict the groups' shared commitments, can thus jeopardize their individual members' well-being. Assenting to such a proposition him- or herself can sever an individual's bonds with such a group. The prospect that people outside the group might credit this proposition can also harm an individual by reducing the social standing or the self-esteem that person enjoys by virtue of his or her group's reputation." Kahan, "Neutral Principles, Motivated Cognition, and Some Problems," p. 20.

- **16.** For a lengthy discussion of Snedden and his influence, see Krug, *The Shaping of the American High School*.
- 17. "Rejecting the idea of a uniform curriculum or uniform methods, Snedden proposed a conception of civilization as 'a thing of standardized parts'—preparation for which can be achieved by 'quality production.' To this end, Snedden employed the appealing metaphor of teamwork. Schools, he said, should 'be guided chiefly by the purpose of enabling each person, with his personal equipment and in the light of his probable part in the games of life, to make himself as a contributor to the success of the many teams—from family to nation—in which he must play his part.' The 'great community' as he liked to call it, could be achieved by 'division of function' and 'specialization of service.' The specialized skills that each worker brought to the process of production, the particular contributions that each player made to the success of the team, and the distinctive functions that each individual performed in the interest of a placid social order were all of one piece." Kliebard, *Schooled to Work*, p. 123.

As Diane Ravitch writes, "Progressive educators became accustomed to thinking of the schools in terms of their social function and to asserting that the work of the schools must meet the test of social efficiency. In education, social efficiency meant that every subject, every program, every study must be judged by whether it was socially useful. . . . What point was there teaching history, science, literature, mathematics, and foreign language to children who would never go to college? How was society served by wasting their time in such manifestly 'useless' and impractical studies?" Ravitch, "From History to Social Studies," pp. 125–26.

[but also] has been used to describe several attitudes toward society which have as little in common among themselves as they have with those traditionally regarded as their opposites." Hayek, "Individualism," p. 3.
